Quick reference

0 · How to use this book

Goal: answer any L4/L5 Spring Framework / Spring Boot interview question cold, with version awareness, trade-offs and a failure mode.

1. Readthe concept, the diagram, and the config/code. Notice the since tags: interviewers love asking when and why something changed.
2. Answer aloudeach worksheet question before opening the answer. Tick “got it” only if you were fully right; progress is saved in your browser.
3. Filteruse ★ Must-know only in the top bar for a fast pass (about 40% of questions) and save the rest for a second pass.
4. LevelsL4 = solid senior IC: correct usage, internals basics. L5 = design, trade-offs, failure modes, operations.
Reading the tagsEvery question has tags: difficulty (easy/medium/hard), target level L4/L5, ★ MUST when you are expected to answer cold, and the version in which the feature appeared. Where a 2025-26 feature is uncertain it is marked (verify in release notes).
🧑‍💻 From my experienceTeal boxes tie a topic to real work from the resume: the retail platform (optimistic locking, fork-and-join aggregation, KMS field-level encryption at the gateway, hermetic component tests with Postgres + MockServer + Kafka), HR systems (Oracle+MongoDB to Kafka event-driven, payroll outbound, hourly batch), Attestation Authority (x509/HSM), California Student Aid Commission (Spring Boot + enterprise IAM, heap-dump leak fix, AES-256/RSA field-level encryption).

💡 TipBoot 2.7 and 3.0 shipped in the same month (Nov 2022) because 2.7 was the “bridge”: it added the new auto-configuration imports file and deprecations so that the jump to 3.0 (Java 17 + Jakarta) was smaller. Always say “upgrade to latest 2.7 first, then 3.0”.
🌱 SpringMental model. Spring Framework = container + programming model (DI, AOP, tx, MVC, data access abstractions). Spring Boot = opinionated bootstrapping on top: starters (dependency sets), auto-configuration (conditional beans), externalised config, Actuator, executable jar. Almost every interview question is either “how does the container do X” or “what does Boot do for me when I add Y”.

Spring / Boot: 10 things to remember

  1. Prefer constructor injection; beans are singletons by default, so keep them stateless.
  2. @Transactional, @Async, @Cacheable work through proxies: self-invocation and non-public methods bypass them; put them on services.
  3. @SpringBootApplication = @Configuration + @EnableAutoConfiguration + @ComponentScan; auto-config backs off with @ConditionalOnMissingBean.
  4. Use @ConfigurationProperties (records, validated) for groups of settings; know the property precedence.
  5. Boot 3 = Java 17 + jakarta.* + Hibernate 6; Boot 4 / Framework 7 add API versioning and more (verify in release notes).
  6. REST: right status codes, idempotent writes (Idempotency-Key), ETag/If-Match with @Version, one ProblemDetail error shape.
  7. Always set connect/read timeouts on RestClient; retry only idempotent calls, with backoff and a circuit breaker.
  8. Security is a filter chain; resource servers validate JWTs; CORS before auth; mTLS with SSL bundles (Boot 3.1).
  9. Test with slices (@WebMvcTest, @DataJpaTest) plus a few hermetic Testcontainers/MockServer tests; use springdoc 2.x + a committed OpenAPI spec as the contract.
  10. Observe and operate: Actuator liveness vs readiness, Micrometer traces/trace id in logs, graceful shutdown, virtual threads (Boot 3.2) only after measuring.

1 · IoC, DI and bean definitions

The container builds, wires and manages objects so your code only declares dependencies.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: The container builds, wires and manages objects; you only declare dependencies.
  • Must-know: ★ constructor injection · ★ @Primary/@Qualifier resolution · ★ BeanFactory vs ApplicationContext
  • Top pitfall: ambiguous beans give NoUniqueBeanDefinitionException; field injection hides dependencies.
  • Version markers: Spring 2.5 annotations; 4.3 implicit constructor injection
IoC ★ MUST KNOWInversion of control: the container (not new) creates objects and calls you. BeanFactory is the minimal API; ApplicationContext adds events, i18n, resource loading, AOP integration, eager singleton creation.
DI styles ★ MUST KNOWConstructor (preferred: immutable, testable, required deps), setter (optional deps), field (@Autowired on field: avoid, untestable without reflection).
BeanDefinitionMetadata: class, scope, lazy, init/destroy, dependencies, primary, qualifiers. BeanFactoryPostProcessor edits definitions, BeanPostProcessor edits instances (this is where proxies are born).
Component scanning@Component @Service @Repository @Controller found under the @SpringBootApplication package (@ComponentScan). @Repository adds exception translation.
FeatureWhat it doesSince
XML bean definitionsOriginal configuration styleSpring 1.0
@Autowired, @Component scanning ★ MUST KNOWAnnotation-driven DISpring 2.5
@Configuration + @Bean ★ MUST KNOWJava configSpring 3.0 (@Configuration); JavaConfig merged in 3.0
Implicit constructor injection ★ MUST KNOWSingle constructor needs no @AutowiredSpring 4.3
@Lazy, @Primary, @QualifierDisambiguation and lazinessSpring 2.5 / 3.0
Functional bean registration (BeanRegistrar in 7.0)Programmatic registration, no CGLIBSpring 5.0 (GenericApplicationContext.registerBean); BeanRegistrar 7.0 (verify)
Bean overriding disabled by defaultDuplicate bean name fails at startupBoot 2.1
🔑 Key ideaContainer startup in one line: read definitions → run BeanFactoryPostProcessors → instantiate singletons (resolve constructor args, inject, run BeanPostProcessors, init callbacks) → publish ContextRefreshedEvent. Failures in wiring therefore appear at startup, which is exactly why constructor injection + eager singletons is good.
⚠ PitfallNoUniqueBeanDefinitionException = two candidates and no @Primary/@Qualifier. NoSuchBeanDefinitionException = missing (usually not scanned, or a conditional config did not match). Run with --debug to see the conditions report.

Worksheet

What is Inversion of Control and Dependency Injection? Why is constructor injection preferred over field injection?
Answer

1. Model Answer

IoC: the framework controls object creation and lifecycle; DI is the mechanism: dependencies are supplied from outside.

Constructor injection: dependencies can be final (immutable), the object can never exist half-wired, missing dependencies fail at startup, you can instantiate it in a plain unit test with new OrderService(mockRepo, mockClient), and a constructor with 7 parameters visibly signals a class doing too much. Field injection hides dependencies, needs reflection or the container in tests, and permits nulls. Setter injection is for genuinely optional/re-configurable collaborators.

Since Spring 4.3 a single constructor needs no @Autowired; Lombok @RequiredArgsConstructor is a common shortcut.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Whether the candidate can explain IoC/DI as a design idea and justify constructor injection with concrete testability and correctness arguments, not slogans.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'IoC means Spring creates the objects' and 'constructor injection is best practice' without saying why. Often claims field injection is bad only because 'it is not recommended'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains that DI supplies collaborators from outside, and that constructor injection allows final fields, fails at startup if a dependency is missing, and lets you write new OrderService(mockRepo, mockClient) in a plain unit test without a Spring context.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that field injection hides dependency count (a 9-argument constructor is a visible smell), makes reflection-based tests necessary, and allows half-wired objects. Notes the edge cases: constructor cycles fail fast, optional dependencies use ObjectProvider or @Nullable, and a single constructor needs no @Autowired. Decision rule: constructor injection by default, setter only for truly optional or reconfigurable collaborators.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Enforces it rather than preaching it: ArchUnit or Checkstyle rule banning @Autowired on fields, Lombok @RequiredArgsConstructor as the team convention, and a review checklist item for constructors with too many parameters. Treats a long constructor as a signal to split the class, and documents the convention in the service template so new services start compliant.
What is the difference between BeanFactory and ApplicationContext?
Answer

1. Model Answer

BeanFactory is the root container interface: lazy bean lookup and DI. ApplicationContext extends it with: eager singleton pre-instantiation, ApplicationEventPublisher, MessageSource (i18n), ResourcePatternResolver, Environment/profiles, and automatic detection of BeanPostProcessor/BeanFactoryPostProcessor beans. In practice you always use an ApplicationContext (Boot creates AnnotationConfigServletWebServerApplicationContext or the reactive variant).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands the container hierarchy and what ApplicationContext adds on top of BeanFactory, rather than reciting 'ApplicationContext is advanced BeanFactory'.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'ApplicationContext is the newer, more advanced BeanFactory' and cannot name a single concrete feature beyond that.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists the additions: eager singleton pre-instantiation, events via ApplicationEventPublisher, MessageSource for i18n, resource loading, Environment/profiles, and automatic detection of BeanPostProcessor and BeanFactoryPostProcessor beans.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Points out the practical consequence: with plain BeanFactory, post-processors must be registered by hand, so AOP, @Transactional and @Autowired processing do not happen automatically. Explains eager singleton creation means wiring errors surface at startup instead of on first request, and that you still reach the BeanFactory via getBeanFactory() for lazy or programmatic lookups. Rule: never use a bare BeanFactory in application code.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Frames it as a fail-fast startup policy: eager init is why a bad deploy dies in the readiness window, and why a blanket lazy-initialization=true trades that safety for startup time and needs a deliberate decision. Would keep programmatic getBean calls out of business code through an ArchUnit rule, and use the question to teach juniors what happens during refresh().
Two beans implement the same interface. How does Spring decide which to inject, and what are the options to control it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Resolution order for a by-type injection point with several candidates: (1) @Qualifier match, (2) @Primary, (3) @Priority, (4) fall back to matching the parameter/field name to a bean name, else NoUniqueBeanDefinitionException.

Custom qualifier annotations (meta-annotated with @Qualifier) give type-safe selection, and Map<String,PaymentClient> is a cheap strategy-pattern registry.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knowledge of Spring's exact candidate-resolution order and the judgement to choose the right disambiguation mechanism for the situation.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'use @Qualifier' or 'use @Primary' and does not know the order or that falling back to the parameter name exists. May think Spring just picks one at random.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes the order: @Qualifier, then @Primary, then @Priority, then matching the parameter or field name to a bean name, otherwise NoUniqueBeanDefinitionException. Shows @Primary on the default and @Qualifier("adyen") at the injection point.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes that name-based fallback depends on the -parameters compiler flag and silently breaks on a rename, so explicit @Qualifier is safer. Mentions injecting List<PaymentClient> or Map<String, PaymentClient> for strategy-style dispatch, and that @Primary hides which implementation is active, which can cause surprises in tests. Rule: @Primary for a true default, qualifiers for deliberate choices, collection injection for strategies.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Pushes toward making the choice explicit and testable: a custom qualifier annotation or a strategy registry keyed by an enum, instead of bean-name coupling across modules. Would add a context-load smoke test per profile so ambiguity fails in CI, and agree a convention for shared libraries about who may declare @Primary.
Explain BeanFactoryPostProcessor vs BeanPostProcessor. Give a real example of each, and say why a BeanPostProcessor bean should be declared static in a @Configuration class.
Answer

1. Model Answer

BeanFactoryPostProcessor runs once after definitions are loaded but before any regular bean is instantiated; it can modify definitions. Example: PropertySourcesPlaceholderConfigurer resolving ${...}, ConfigurationClassPostProcessor (which processes @Configuration, @Bean, @ComponentScan, @Import).

BeanPostProcessor runs around initialisation of every bean instance (postProcessBeforeInitialization / postProcessAfterInitialization); it can wrap or replace the instance. Examples: AutowiredAnnotationBeanPostProcessor (does @Autowired), CommonAnnotationBeanPostProcessor (@PostConstruct), and AbstractAutoProxyCreator which returns the AOP proxy for @Transactional, @Async, @Cacheable.

Why static: a @Bean method on a configuration class needs that config instance to exist, which forces early instantiation, before other post-processors are registered; the config bean then is not eligible for processing by all BPPs and Spring logs “Bean ... is not eligible for getting processed by all BeanPostProcessors”. A static @Bean method needs no config instance.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands the container extension points and the ordering problem that arises when a post-processor bean is created too early.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says both 'modify beans' and cannot tell them apart. Has no idea why static matters on a @Bean method.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: BFPP runs once after definitions load and before regular beans are instantiated and edits definitions (PropertySourcesPlaceholderConfigurer, ConfigurationClassPostProcessor). BPP wraps every bean around initialisation (@Autowired, @PostConstruct, AOP proxies).
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the static rule: a non-static @Bean method needs its enclosing @Configuration instance, forcing that class to be instantiated before post-processors are registered, so it misses being processed and Spring logs 'not eligible for getting processed by all BeanPostProcessors'. Consequence is a silently un-proxied or un-injected config bean. Verification: look for that INFO log line at startup. Rule: always static for BFPP and BPP bean methods. Ties it to real failures: a custom BPP that injects services pulls them in early, so they lose @Transactional or @Async proxying with no error. Keeps BPPs dependency-free and uses ObjectProvider or lookup inside callbacks. Would grep logs for the 'not eligible' message as part of startup review.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats custom BPPs as platform code: they live in a vetted starter, have a context-level test asserting the proxy is applied, and teams are steered to simpler tools (@ConfigurationProperties, SmartInitializingSingleton, ApplicationRunner) first. Adds a CI check that fails on the 'not eligible' log message.
The application fails with UnsatisfiedDependencyException / NoSuchBeanDefinitionException for a bean that is clearly on the classpath. List your debugging steps.
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. Read the root cause at the bottom of the chain (“Caused by”), not the first line.
  2. Is the class under the @SpringBootApplication package? If it lives in a sibling package, scanning misses it: use scanBasePackages or @Import.
  3. Is it created by a @Conditional.../auto-config that did not match? Start with --debug (or /actuator/conditions) and look in “Negative matches”.
  4. Is the profile active (@Profile)? Check /actuator/env.
  5. Is it a test? A @WebMvcTest slice does not load @Service beans: use @MockitoBean/@Import.
  6. Generic type mismatch (Repo<User> vs Repo<Order>) or proxy typed by interface when you inject the concrete class (JDK proxy; set proxyTargetClass or inject the interface).
  7. Check /actuator/beans for the bean name/type actually registered.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: A systematic debugging method for wiring failures: reading the exception chain, then using Boot's tooling rather than guessing.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Googles the exception, adds @Component or @Autowired randomly, or restarts the IDE. Reads only the first line of the stack trace.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Reads the root cause under 'Caused by', checks the class is under the @SpringBootApplication package or covered by scanBasePackages/@Import, and runs with --debug to see the conditions report for negative matches.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Works through a checklist: component scan reach, conditional that did not match (/actuator/conditions), profile not active, missing @Bean because the starter or class is not on the runtime classpath (mvn dependency:tree), a @ConditionalOnBean ordering trap, and test slices (@WebMvcTest) that do not load services. Verifies by a minimal reproduction with ApplicationContextRunner. Rule: bottom of the chain first, conditions report second.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Turns repeated incidents into guardrails: a context-startup test per profile in CI, a team runbook for wiring failures, shared-library auto-configs that log clear 'why not created' messages via a FailureAnalyzer. Would also question whether the library should be scanned at all instead of exposed as auto-configuration.
When would you use ObjectProvider, @Lazy injection, or Provider instead of plain injection?
Answer

1. Model Answer

ObjectProvider<T> (Spring 4.3) defers lookup and handles optionality/multiplicity: getIfAvailable(), getIfUnique(), orderedStream(). Use it for (a) optional collaborators that exist only in some profiles, (b) fetching a prototype bean each call from a singleton, (c) breaking an initialisation cycle. @Lazy on an injection point injects a lazy-resolution proxy; useful for expensive clients and cycles but adds a proxy. jakarta.inject.Provider is equivalent but Spring-agnostic.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows the lazy-resolution injection tools and when each is appropriate versus a design smell.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'they are for lazy loading' and uses @Lazy as a quick fix whenever a circular dependency error appears.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains ObjectProvider<T> for optional or multiple beans (getIfAvailable, getIfUnique, orderedStream), fetching a prototype per call from a singleton, and @Lazy injecting a lazy-resolution proxy; Provider is the JSR-330 equivalent.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes @Lazy hides a cycle by deferring resolution, so the failure moves to runtime on first use. The proxy also adds indirection and fails for final classes under CGLIB. ObjectProvider keeps the dependency explicit and testable. Rule: ObjectProvider for optionality or prototypes, @Lazy only as a documented stopgap, and refactor the cycle. Uses @Lazy deliberately for startup cost, such as a heavy client not needed on every path, and measures the gain with /actuator/startup. Otherwise redesigns the cycle by extracting a third component or publishing an event.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets the policy: @Lazy to break a cycle requires a ticket and comment, with allow-circular-references left false platform-wide. Notes the Boot 2.6 default change is the long-term direction, so the migration is to remove cycles, not to keep papering over them.

2 · Scopes, lifecycle and circular dependencies

Know where a bean lives, when its callbacks run and why cycles are now an error by default.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Know a bean's scope, lifecycle callbacks and why cycles now fail.
  • Must-know: ★ singleton vs prototype/request · ★ @PostConstruct/@PreDestroy order · ★ circular dependency handling
  • Top pitfall: prototype injected into a singleton is created only once.
  • Version markers: Boot 2.6 bans cycles by default
ScopeInstancesNotesSince
singleton ★ MUST KNOW1 per containerDefault. Must be thread-safe and stateless. Not “one per JVM”.1.0
prototypenew on every lookupContainer does NOT call destroy callbacks. Injected into a singleton it is created once, use ObjectProvider.1.0
request / session / applicationper HTTP request / session / ServletContextInject via scoped proxy (proxyMode = TARGET_CLASS) into singletons.2.0 (annotations 2.5)
websocketper WebSocket session4.0

⚠ PitfallA singleton that injects a prototype or request-scoped bean gets one instance at wiring time. Fix with ObjectProvider, @Lookup, or @Scope(value="request", proxyMode=ScopedProxyMode.TARGET_CLASS).
🧑‍💻 From my experienceRetail-platform-style services should never hold per-request state in singleton beans: request data lives in method parameters or a request-scoped holder, which is also what keeps the fork-and-join aggregation threads safe (no shared mutable singleton state).

Worksheet

Is a Spring singleton bean thread-safe? What does “singleton” mean in Spring?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Spring singleton = one instance per container (per bean definition), not the GoF per-classloader singleton; two contexts or two definitions of the same class produce two instances. Spring does nothing about thread safety: the same instance serves all request threads. Keep singletons stateless or immutable; if you need state use thread-safe structures (ConcurrentHashMap, AtomicLong), method-local data, or a request/prototype scope. Typical bug: a SimpleDateFormat or a mutable List field in a @Service.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Distinguishes Spring's singleton scope from the GoF pattern and reasons correctly about shared mutable state across request threads.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'singleton means one instance, so it is thread-safe' or the reverse 'Spring makes it thread-safe with synchronization'. Both are wrong.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: A singleton is one instance per container per bean definition, shared by all request threads, and Spring does nothing for thread safety. Keep it stateless or immutable, and use thread-safe structures if state is needed.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Gives failure modes: a SimpleDateFormat or mutable list field in a service causing race conditions, request data stashed in a field leaking between users, and two contexts or two definitions producing two instances. Shows the remedies: local variables, ConcurrentHashMap, Atomic*, request-scoped beans via proxy, or ThreadLocal only with cleanup. Would find such bugs through code review and concurrency tests with many threads. Adds that virtual threads and pinned synchronized blocks change the cost of locking inside singletons, and that caches inside beans need eviction. Prefers pushing state to Redis or the DB and keeping beans stateless so replicas scale.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Writes it down as a rule, backed by a static analysis or review checklist that flags non-final instance fields in @Service/@Component. Includes a short 'shared state' section in the service template and uses this as a recurring incident-review theme.
Describe the bean lifecycle and the order of @PostConstruct, InitializingBean.afterPropertiesSet and @Bean(initMethod).
Answer

1. Model Answer

Instantiate (constructor) → populate dependencies → *Aware callbacks → BeanPostProcessor.postProcessBeforeInitialization (this is where @PostConstruct runs, via CommonAnnotationBeanPostProcessor) → InitializingBean.afterPropertiesSet() → custom initMethod → postProcessAfterInitialization (AOP proxy wrapping) → ready. Destruction mirrors it: @PreDestroy → DisposableBean.destroy() → custom destroyMethod. Order of the three init mechanisms: @PostConstruct, afterPropertiesSet, initMethod.

Gotcha: inside @PostConstruct the bean is not yet proxied, so calling your own @Transactional method there does not start a transaction. Use ApplicationReadyEvent/SmartInitializingSingleton after the context is complete.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Command of the exact lifecycle sequence and the awareness of where proxies and annotation callbacks hook in.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Recites 'constructor, then init, then destroy' and guesses the relative order of the three init mechanisms.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Gives the sequence: constructor, dependency population, *Aware callbacks, BPP before-init (where @PostConstruct runs), afterPropertiesSet, custom initMethod, BPP after-init (AOP proxy wrapping). Destruction mirrors it with @PreDestroy, DisposableBean, destroyMethod.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the consequence: inside @PostConstruct the bean is not yet proxied, so a self-call to a @Transactional or @Async method does not get the advice, and other beans may not be fully initialised. Uses ApplicationReadyEvent or SmartInitializingSingleton for work that needs the full context. Verifies by logging in each callback. Rule: no remote calls or transactional work in @PostConstruct. Connects to production: slow @PostConstruct extends startup and can fail the Kubernetes startup probe, and blocking calls there delay readiness. Moves cache warm-up to a readiness-gated runner with timeouts.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises on one init style (@PostConstruct or initMethod) in the team guidelines and flags heavy init in review. Adds startup-time budgets measured via BufferingApplicationStartup in CI so regressions are visible.
What is a circular dependency, how does Spring resolve it for singletons, and why did Boot 2.6 prohibit it by default?
Answer

1. Model Answer

A→B→A. For setter/field injection of singletons Spring resolves it with three-level singleton caches: it exposes an early reference (an ObjectFactory) of A before A is fully initialised, so B can receive it. Constructor cycles cannot be resolved: BeanCurrentlyInCreationException. Prototype cycles also fail.

Boot 2.6 sets spring.main.allow-circular-references=false because cycles hide design problems (tight coupling, unclear initialisation order, partially built beans seen by others, trouble with AOP early proxies). Fix by design: extract the shared logic into a third bean, publish an application event, invert one direction, or as a last resort @Lazy on one constructor parameter.

Setting the property to true is a migration stop-gap, not a fix.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understanding of the three-level singleton cache mechanics, why constructor cycles cannot work, and the design rationale behind Boot 2.6's change.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Spring handles circular dependencies automatically' and does not know about constructor failure or the Boot 2.6 default.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains early references via an ObjectFactory in the third-level cache, that setter/field cycles of singletons work while constructor and prototype cycles throw BeanCurrentlyInCreationException, and that Boot 2.6 sets spring.main.allow-circular-references=false.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains why it was prohibited: cycles signal poor design, behaviour depends on instantiation order, and early references can expose objects before proxying or initialisation, leading to subtle bugs. Notes the escape hatch property is a migration aid, not a solution. Fixes: extract a third bean, invert via events, or ObjectProvider. Verifies with a context test. Describes the migration: flip the flag off in a branch, list every cycle from failures, fix by extraction, and never re-enable globally. Notes the early-reference path can hand out a raw bean when the final one is proxied, which is a real source of missing-advice bugs.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes 'no cycles' a platform default with an ArchUnit slices().should().beFreeOfCycles() rule at package level too. Runs a migration campaign across services with an owner list, and treats the property being true in any service as a tracked tech-debt item.
A singleton needs a fresh prototype bean on every call. Show three ways.
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. ObjectProvider<Command> and call provider.getObject() each time (preferred).
  2. @Lookup abstract method: Spring generates a CGLIB subclass that overrides it to call getBean.
  3. Scoped proxy: @Scope(value = "prototype", proxyMode = ScopedProxyMode.TARGET_CLASS) so each method call on the injected proxy hits a new instance (surprising semantics, avoid for stateful calls).

Injecting ApplicationContext and calling getBean works but couples you to the container.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Whether the candidate knows the scope-mismatch problem (prototype inside singleton) and picks the least surprising solution.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'just annotate it @Scope("prototype")' not realising the singleton only gets one instance at wiring time.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Names three approaches: ObjectProvider<Command>.getObject() per call, an abstract @Lookup method (CGLIB-overridden to call getBean), and a scoped proxy with proxyMode.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the pitfall of the scoped proxy: every method call on the proxy creates a new instance, so a multi-call sequence uses different objects, which is surprising. @Lookup needs a non-final class and CGLIB. ObjectProvider is explicit and easy to mock, so it is the preferred choice. Notes prototype beans do not get destroy callbacks, which can leak resources. Questions whether a prototype bean is needed at all: a plain factory or Supplier<Command> with constructor-passed state is often simpler and has clearer ownership. Verifies instance distinctness with a test.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets guidance that scoped proxies are for request/session scope only and prototype injection uses ObjectProvider or an explicit factory, documented in the engineering handbook with the leak caveat about destruction.
What happens to a bean’s destroy callbacks on JVM shutdown and on Boot graceful shutdown? How can a @PreDestroy hang a Kubernetes rollout?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Boot registers a JVM shutdown hook (SpringApplication.setRegisterShutdownHook default true) that closes the context: ContextClosedEvent, then SmartLifecycle beans stop in reverse phase order (web server graceful stop is a phase, Kafka listener containers are SmartLifecycle), then singletons are destroyed in reverse dependency order, calling @PreDestroy. With server.shutdown=graceful the web server stops accepting new requests and waits up to spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase (default 30s) for in-flight ones.

A @PreDestroy that blocks (awaiting an executor that never drains, flushing to an unreachable broker) delays the exit past the pod’s terminationGracePeriodSeconds and Kubernetes sends SIGKILL, so in-flight work is lost anyway. Put timeouts on every shutdown wait, size the grace period = preStop sleep + shutdown phase timeout + margin, and never call System.exit from callbacks. Prototype beans are never destroyed by the container; kill -9 runs nothing.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Production shutdown reasoning: ordering of lifecycle phases, in-flight request handling, and how shutdown interacts with Kubernetes termination.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Spring calls @PreDestroy when the app stops' without knowing about phases, graceful shutdown or the grace period.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Boot registers a shutdown hook that closes the context, publishes ContextClosedEvent, stops SmartLifecycle beans in reverse phase order (web server graceful stop, Kafka listeners), then destroys singletons in reverse dependency order calling @PreDestroy.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the hang: a @PreDestroy that waits on an executor, a blocking queue or a remote call never returns, so the pod stays Terminating until terminationGracePeriodSeconds expires and SIGKILL hits, possibly mid-work. Also spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase bounds graceful phases. Mitigations: bounded waits, shutdown() plus awaitTermination(timeout), and a preStop sleep so endpoints deregister before SIGTERM. Verify with a local SIGTERM test and thread dump. Aligns the budget: grace period greater than preStop delay plus shutdown phase timeout plus longest in-flight request, and Kafka consumers commit offsets before close. Uses thread dumps during a stuck rollout to find the blocking @PreDestroy.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Bakes a tested shutdown contract into the platform chart and base config (graceful on, timeouts, preStop), and adds a chaos or rollout test in CI that asserts clean exit under load. Tracks rollout duration and SIGKILL exits as SLO signals.

3 · Java config, @Configuration proxies and profiles

Declare beans in code; understand full vs lite mode and conditional wiring.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Declare beans in Java; understand full vs lite configuration.
  • Must-know: ★ @Configuration CGLIB proxy · ★ @Bean inter-method calls · ★ @Profile/@Conditional
  • Top pitfall: @Configuration(proxyBeanMethods=false) breaks inter-@Bean calls.
  • Version markers: Spring 3.0 / 3.1 profiles / 5.2 proxyBeanMethods
FeatureMeaningSince
@Configuration / @Bean ★ MUST KNOWFactory methods registering beans; method name = bean nameSpring 3.0
proxyBeanMethods ★ MUST KNOWtrue (default): CGLIB subclass so inter-@Bean calls return the singleton. false: “lite”, plain calls, faster startup, native-friendlySpring 5.2 / Boot 2.2
@Profile, spring.profiles.active ★ MUST KNOWConditional beans per environmentSpring 3.1
Profile expressions !prod & cloud, profile groupsComplex activation; spring.profiles.group.prod=db,metricsSpring 5.1 / Boot 2.4
@Import, ImportSelector, @ImportRuntimeHintsCompose configs, back auto-configSpring 3.0 / 6.0 hints
@Conditional, @ConditionalOnPropertyConditional bean registrationSpring 4.0 / Boot 1.0
@Bean with @Scope, @DependsOn, @OrderControl scope, creation order, collection ordercore
🔑 Key ideaFull vs lite. In a @Configuration class with proxyBeanMethods=true, b() calling a() is intercepted and returns the container singleton. In a @Component (lite mode) or proxyBeanMethods=false, a() is a plain Java call and creates a new object. With false, take dependencies as method parameters instead.
💡 TipPrefer @ConditionalOnProperty or @Profile only on infrastructure choices. Profiles that change business logic make tests lie: staging runs a different graph than prod. Keep the prod profile set tiny and put differences in properties.

Worksheet

What does @Configuration(proxyBeanMethods = false) do, and when is it safe?
Answer

1. Model Answer

With the default true, Spring generates a CGLIB subclass of the config class at startup so @Bean methods called from other @Bean methods return the existing singleton (honouring scope). That costs startup time, memory and prevents final config classes. With false there is no subclass (“lite” mode): faster startup, and required for GraalVM-friendly code; but a direct call dataSource() from another @Bean method creates a second instance.

Safe when no @Bean method calls another @Bean method directly; inject collaborators as parameters. All Spring Boot auto-configurations use proxyBeanMethods = false (Boot 2.2+).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands what the CGLIB config proxy buys you and when removing it is safe, linked to startup and native-image concerns.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it makes startup faster' with no sense of what behaviour is lost.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: By default Spring subclasses the config class so inter-@Bean method calls return the existing singleton. With false there is no subclass ('lite' mode), giving faster startup, less memory, and GraalVM friendliness.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the safe condition: no @Bean method calls another @Bean method directly; dependencies come via method parameters. If one does call it, you silently get a new instance each call, breaking singleton semantics and sometimes resource handling. Verify by a test asserting identity or by reviewing for direct calls. Rule: false plus parameter injection for all new config classes. Notes that Boot's own auto-configurations use proxyBeanMethods = false and that the saving scales with many config classes, so measure with startup tracing before claiming gains. Warns that final config classes become possible only in lite mode.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes it the default in the starter archetype and adds an ArchUnit or errorprone-style rule that flags inter-bean method calls in lite configs. Frames it as part of a native-image readiness effort with a migration backlog.
How do profiles work? How do you activate them and what is the precedence between the options?
Answer

1. Model Answer

A bean/config annotated @Profile("x") is registered only when profile x is active; no profile = always. Activate via spring.profiles.active=prod,cloud (property, env var SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE, --spring.profiles.active CLI, SpringApplication.setAdditionalProfiles, @ActiveProfiles in tests). Property-source precedence applies, so CLI args beat env vars beat application.yml. spring.profiles.include adds profiles unconditionally; profile groups (Boot 2.4) let prod imply [db, metrics]. Profile-specific files application-prod.yml override the base file. In multi-document YAML use spring.config.activate.on-profile (Boot 2.4; replaced spring.profiles).

Pitfall: spring.profiles.active cannot be set inside a profile-specific file or an on-profile document (fails at startup).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Whether the candidate understands profiles as bean registration filters and the property precedence, plus their misuse risks.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'profiles are for dev and prod' and cannot list ways to activate them or the precedence.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: @Profile("x") registers a bean only when active; activation via spring.profiles.active, env var, CLI arg, setAdditionalProfiles, @ActiveProfiles in tests; normal property-source precedence applies so CLI beats env beats file.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Mentions spring.profiles.include versus active, profile groups, profile expressions (!prod), and profile-specific config files overriding defaults. Warns against putting business logic branches on profiles and prod secrets in profile files. Verifies with /actuator/env and startup log 'The following profiles are active'. Observes that environment-specific bean swapping creates untested code paths: the prod wiring is never run in CI. Prefers config values and feature toggles over profile-switched beans, and keeps profile names to a small set.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises the allowed profile set and what each may change, enforced by review and a context test running the prod profile with test doubles for externals. Moves per-environment values into the deployment layer, not in jar profile files.
Write a bean that only exists when a property is set, and one that is only created if another bean is missing. Which annotations?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@Bean @ConditionalOnProperty(prefix="feature", name="audit", havingValue="true", matchIfMissing=false)
AuditListener audit() {...}

@Bean @ConditionalOnMissingBean(Clock.class)
Clock clock() { return Clock.systemUTC(); }   // user can override by defining their own Clock

These are Boot conditions; plain Spring has only @Conditional(MyCondition.class) with a Condition.matches. @ConditionalOnMissingBean only behaves predictably in auto-configuration classes (processed after user config); in ordinary config the order of registration matters.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Practical fluency with Boot conditional annotations and the override-friendly back-off pattern.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Writes @Profile for both, or @ConditionalOnBean where @ConditionalOnMissingBean is wanted.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Uses @ConditionalOnProperty(prefix, name, havingValue, matchIfMissing) for a property-gated bean and @ConditionalOnMissingBean(Clock.class) for a default that the user can override. Notes plain Spring only has @Conditional.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains matchIfMissing semantics and that @ConditionalOnMissingBean is only reliable in auto-configuration because ordering is controlled. In user configs it depends on registration order. Verifies via the conditions report and ApplicationContextRunner tests with and without the property or user bean. Adds that property names are relaxed-bound and typos silently disable the bean, so the master switch should be a documented @ConfigurationProperties field with metadata. Chooses the type in @ConditionalOnMissingBean carefully to avoid matching unintended subtypes.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats feature toggles as a managed lifecycle: owner, default, removal date. Provides a shared test harness with ApplicationContextRunner for library authors and a convention that every conditional bean documents its property in the starter README.
You have a @Configuration class that defines a @Bean returning a Kafka producer used by 40 services in a shared library. How do you make it overridable and safe?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Ship it as an auto-configuration (@AutoConfiguration + META-INF/spring/org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.AutoConfiguration.imports) rather than a scanned config; guard each bean with @ConditionalOnMissingBean so consumers can supply their own; gate the whole thing with @ConditionalOnClass(KafkaTemplate.class) and a master switch property; bind settings with @ConfigurationProperties and validation; use proxyBeanMethods=false; add the spring-boot-configuration-processor for IDE metadata; test with ApplicationContextRunner (including “user bean wins” and “property off” cases). Avoid component-scanning library packages from the app: it makes library internals part of the app’s bean graph.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Library design judgement: how to ship shared infrastructure to dozens of services without coupling them or removing their ability to override.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Puts a @Configuration in the shared jar and tells teams to @ComponentScan it or @Import it.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Ships it as an auto-configuration (@AutoConfiguration plus the AutoConfiguration.imports file), guards beans with @ConditionalOnMissingBean, gates with @ConditionalOnClass and a master switch property, and binds settings via @ConfigurationProperties.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds safe defaults (idempotence, acks=all, timeouts), customizer hooks like a ProducerFactoryCustomizer so teams extend without replacing, metrics and health integration, and @AutoConfiguration(after = KafkaAutoConfiguration.class). Tests with ApplicationContextRunner for back-off. Rule: back off by default, customise via callbacks. Considers blast radius: changing a default affects 40 services, so releases are versioned with a changelog, an opt-out flag, and canary adoption. Keeps the library's dependencies optional to avoid classpath conflicts.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Runs it as an internal platform product: semantic versioning, deprecation policy, a compatibility matrix for Boot versions, automated upgrade PRs across repos, and usage telemetry to see who overrides what. Argues for a thin starter plus documented customizer SPI.
How can the same jar run with different DB URLs in dev, QA and prod without rebuilding?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Never bake environment values into the artifact. Externalise: profile-specific files only for non-secret defaults, and inject environment-specific/secret values via env vars (SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL, relaxed binding), Kubernetes ConfigMap/Secret mounted as env or via spring.config.import=optional:configtree:/etc/config/ (config tree, Boot 2.4), or Spring Cloud Config/Vault. One immutable image promoted through environments (Argo CD / Jenkins pipelines) is the point.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Twelve-factor mindset: separating build artifact from environment config and secrets, using Boot's externalisation correctly.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Builds a separate jar per environment or commits application-prod.properties with the real DB URL and password.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: One immutable artifact; externalise through env vars (SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL via relaxed binding), Kubernetes ConfigMap/Secret as env or spring.config.import=optional:configtree:, or a config server. Profile files only for non-secret defaults.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes precedence means env beats in-jar files, that secrets never belong in the image or Git, and that optional: prevents a missing mount from failing local runs but can hide a prod misconfig. Verifies with /actuator/env (sanitised) and by deploying the same digest to QA and prod. Adds that the artifact being identical is what makes QA results meaningful, and that secrets mounted as files avoid appearing in kubectl describe and env dumps. Validates at startup with @Validated properties so a wrong environment fails fast.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines an organisation config contract: naming scheme, which layer owns what, secret rotation via an external vault operator, and drift detection between environments. Audits images for embedded secrets in CI.

4 · @ConfigurationProperties and externalised configuration

Type-safe, validated, relaxed-bound configuration and the precedence order.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Type-safe, validated, relaxed-bound configuration with a defined precedence.
  • Must-know: ★ @ConfigurationProperties vs @Value · ★ property source precedence · ★ constructor binding on records
  • Top pitfall: env var and relaxed names silently override YAML.
  • Version markers: Boot 1.0; 2.2 @ConstructorBinding; 3.0 implicit constructor binding
FeatureDetailSince
@ConfigurationProperties ★ MUST KNOWBind a prefix to a typed object; relaxed binding (my-prop, myProp, MY_PROP all work)Boot 1.0
@ConfigurationPropertiesScanRegister annotated classes without @EnableConfigurationPropertiesBoot 2.2
Constructor binding / records ★ MUST KNOWImmutable properties. Needs @ConstructorBinding in 2.2-2.7; implicit for a single constructor / record in 3.0+Boot 2.2 / 3.0
Validation@Validated + Jakarta Bean Validation on the properties class; fail fast at startupBoot 1.x
spring.config.import ★ MUST KNOWImport extra files, configtree:, optional:, Vault/Config serverBoot 2.4
Config Data (multi-doc, on-profile)New loading mechanism; legacy via spring.config.use-legacy-processing removed in 2.5Boot 2.4
@ValueSingle value, SpEL; no relaxed binding for ${} keys, no validationcore

Precedence (highest wins) ★ MUST KNOW

  1. Test @TestPropertySource, @SpringBootTest(properties=...)
  2. Command-line args (--server.port=9)
  3. SPRING_APPLICATION_JSON
  4. JNDI / Java system properties (-D)
  5. OS environment variables
  6. Profile-specific files outside the jar, then inside the jar (application-{profile}.yml)
  7. Default files outside the jar, then inside (application.yml)
  8. @PropertySource on config classes
  9. Defaults via SpringApplication.setDefaultProperties
🧠 Remember“CLI beats Env beats files; outside beats inside; profile beats default”.
⚠ PitfallEnv var binding for lists/maps: MY_LIST_0=a, MY_LIST_1=b; for map keys with dots or dashes env var names lose information, so prefer a .yml or SPRING_APPLICATION_JSON for complex structures.
💡 TipAdd spring-boot-configuration-processor (annotation processor) to generate META-INF/spring-configuration-metadata.json: IDE autocompletion and deprecation hints for your own properties.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceA CSAC-style field-level encryption feature (AES-256/RSA for SSN/PII) is a good @ConfigurationProperties candidate: key alias, algorithm, key-store location as validated, immutable properties; the key material itself comes from a secret store or KMS and never from a committed yml.

Worksheet

@Value vs @ConfigurationProperties: when to use which?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@Value("${x}"): one-off scalar, supports SpEL, no relaxed binding of the key spelling, scattered strings, not validated as a group. @ConfigurationProperties: grouped, typed (Duration, DataSize, enums, lists, nested objects), relaxed binding, JSR-380 validation with fail-fast startup, IDE metadata, easy to unit test (just construct the record), and can be refreshed/rebound by tooling. Rule: more than two related values, or anything with structure → @ConfigurationProperties.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows when typed, validated configuration binding is the right tool and can articulate what @Value cannot do.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'both read properties; @ConfigurationProperties is just cleaner'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: @Value suits a one-off scalar with SpEL; @ConfigurationProperties gives grouped typed binding (Duration, DataSize, enums, nested), relaxed binding, JSR-380 validation, IDE metadata, and easy unit tests.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains @Value key strings are scattered and not relaxed-bound for the key, with no group validation or metadata, so typos fail late. Shows a record with @Validated failing startup. Rule: @ConfigurationProperties for anything beyond a single value, @Value for SpEL or truly one-off values. Adds the spring-boot-configuration-processor for metadata, and prefix-per-component naming to avoid collisions. Tests binding with ApplicationContextRunner.withPropertyValues.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a team convention (one properties class per component, immutable records, validation required) enforced by an ArchUnit rule against @Value in business classes, plus a template for libraries so IDE autocomplete documents every knob.
Explain constructor binding with records. What changed between Boot 2.x and 3.x?
Answer

1. Model Answer

An immutable properties class has no setters; Boot binds by calling the constructor with converted values (@DefaultValue supplies defaults, nested records bind recursively). In 2.2–2.7 you had to annotate the class (or constructor) with @ConstructorBinding and register via @ConfigurationPropertiesScan/@EnableConfigurationProperties. In 3.0 binding is automatic when the class has a single parameterised constructor (records qualify); annotate the constructor with @ConstructorBinding only when there are several constructors. It does not work on a class that is @Component-scanned (the bean is created by normal DI instead); use scanning/enable annotations.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Awareness of immutable configuration binding and the Boot 2 to 3 migration detail that trips teams up.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'use records for properties' without knowing how binding occurs or about @ConstructorBinding.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Binding calls the constructor with converted values, @DefaultValue provides defaults, nested records bind recursively. In 2.2-2.7 @ConstructorBinding was needed; in 3.0 a single parameterised constructor is used automatically, and you still register via @ConfigurationPropertiesScan or @EnableConfigurationProperties.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Names the pitfalls: with multiple constructors you must mark one with @ConstructorBinding in 3.x, Lombok @Data mixes setters back in, and missing values silently become null unless validated. Needs -parameters (Boot's plugins set it). Verifies with a binding test. On upgrade, search for leftover @ConstructorBinding at class level (the import moves packages) and compile errors there. Notes that records plus @Validated give immutability and fail-fast in one place.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes the upgrade a scripted codemod (OpenRewrite recipe) across repos and adds the pattern to the service template. Treats immutable config as the default so runtime mutation of settings is a deliberate design choice.
What is the property precedence order, and where do Kubernetes ConfigMap/Secret values fit?
Answer

1. Model Answer

See the list above: test props > CLI > SPRING_APPLICATION_JSON > system props > env vars > profile files (outside, then inside jar) > default files > @PropertySource > defaults. Secrets mounted as env vars (secretKeyRef) therefore override anything in the jar; mounted as files they are read through spring.config.import=optional:configtree:/etc/secrets/ where each file name is a key and its content the value. ConfigMaps mounted as application.yml in ./config/ or /config/ next to the jar win over the packaged one.

Trap: an empty-string env var still counts as “set” and overrides a yml value.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows precedence order precisely enough to predict which value wins in a Kubernetes deployment and debug surprises.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Remembers 'application.properties then env vars' and is unsure which wins.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Recites the order: test properties, CLI args, SPRING_APPLICATION_JSON, system props, env vars, profile files (outside then inside the jar), default files, @PropertySource, defaults. Env-mounted Secrets therefore override the jar; file mounts use configtree.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the practical effects: a stray env var can silently override a ConfigMap-mounted file, and spring.config.import order matters. Debugs using /actuator/env, which shows each property's source, and /actuator/configprops. Rule: one authoritative layer per key. Warns about relaxed-binding collisions (MY_APP_URL vs my.app.url) and that Helm values, ConfigMaps and secrets can define the same key in different places. Documents expected sources per setting.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines the platform contract of which layer may set which keys, adds a deploy-time lint that detects duplicate definitions, and exposes effective config (sanitised) in a support dashboard to cut incident triage.
A property change is deployed via ConfigMap but pods do not pick it up. Why, and what are your options?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Boot binds properties at startup; env vars from a ConfigMap are only read at container start, and even mounted files are not re-read (@ConfigurationProperties beans are not rebound without Spring Cloud’s /actuator/refresh and @RefreshScope, or Spring Cloud Kubernetes reload). Options: (1) rolling restart triggered by a config checksum annotation (Helm checksum/config) which is simple and safe; (2) Spring Cloud Config + refresh for non-critical toggles; (3) feature-flag service for runtime toggles. Trade-off: live refresh risks half-applied state (a pool size changed while connections exist); restart gives atomic, auditable change and works with Argo CD’s declarative flow.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Production config-change thinking: understanding that binding is startup-time and choosing a safe reload strategy.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'the pod should pick up the ConfigMap automatically' or just 'restart it' with no understanding of why.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Boot binds at startup; env vars are read at container start, and mounted files are not rebound. Options: rolling restart triggered by a config checksum annotation, Spring Cloud /actuator/refresh with @RefreshScope, or Spring Cloud Kubernetes reload.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Compares them: rolling restart is simple and safe with consistent state across replicas; refresh is faster but only rebinds refresh-scoped beans, not already-constructed pools or clients, and can leave replicas inconsistent. Mounted files update with a delay (kubelet sync). Verify by checking /actuator/env after the change. Rule: restart for anything structural, refresh only for safe, simple values. Raises that a reload that partly applies is worse than a restart: connection pools, scheduled jobs, and caches built at startup. Uses canary and readiness gating to roll config like code.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats config as deployable: stored in Git, rolled out via the same pipeline with checksum-driven restarts, audit trail and rollback. Discourages live refresh platform-wide except for explicitly designed dynamic flags handled by a feature-flag service.
How do you validate configuration and make the app fail fast on a bad value? What if you want a warning only?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Annotate the properties type with @Validated and use constraints (@NotBlank, @Min, @Valid on nested). Binding errors or violations throw BindValidationException at context refresh with a clear “Binding to target ... failed” report, so the pod crash-loops before receiving traffic and the readiness probe never passes: a good failure mode. For soft checks implement InitializingBean/@PostConstruct and log a warning, or use a custom HealthIndicator that reports OUT_OF_SERVICE without blocking startup. You can also implement Validator as a bean named configurationPropertiesValidator for cross-field rules.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Fail-fast configuration discipline and the judgement about when a violation should block startup versus warn.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add @NotNull' and does not know where or when validation runs, or that @Validated is needed.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Annotate the properties type with @Validated plus constraints and @Valid on nested objects. Violations throw BindValidationException at refresh, the pod crash-loops and never becomes ready. For soft checks, log a warning from a bean instead.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that crash-looping before readiness is the right failure for a mandatory value, but that cross-field or environment checks need a custom Validator or @AssertTrue. For warn-only cases use an ApplicationListener/InitializingBean logging WARN and a metric. Tests binding with invalid values. Distinguishes: invalid means cannot work (fail), suspicious means works but risky (warn, for example pool size above DB limit). Adds failure analysers so messages are actionable, and avoids validation that calls remote services at startup.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Establishes the policy centrally: all properties classes validated, startup failures alerting via deployment health, and a limited, documented list of soft checks. Uses a CI job that boots the app with prod-like config to catch bad values before release.

5 · Boot auto-configuration, starters and conditions

What @SpringBootApplication really does, and how to find out why a bean is (not) there.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: @SpringBootApplication = config + auto-configuration + component scan; conditions decide beans.
  • Must-know: ★ AutoConfiguration.imports · ★ @ConditionalOnMissingBean back-off · ★ --debug conditions report
  • Top pitfall: main class in the wrong package means beans are not scanned.
  • Version markers: Boot 1.2; 2.7 imports file replaces spring.factories
FeatureWhatSince
Starters ★ MUST KNOWCurated dependency descriptors: spring-boot-starter-web, -data-jpa, -security...Boot 1.0
@EnableAutoConfiguration + spring.factoriesOriginal registration mechanismBoot 1.0
@AutoConfiguration + AutoConfiguration.imports ★ MUST KNOWNew registration file under META-INF/spring/ (one class per line). spring.factories entry deprecated in 2.7 and no longer read for auto-config in 3.0Boot 2.7 / 3.0
