🔑 Key ideaAuto-config is just ordinary @Configuration classes, loaded late, guarded by conditions, mostly @ConditionalOnClass (is the library on the classpath?) + @ConditionalOnMissingBean (did the user already define one?). Define your own bean of that type and the default backs off. That is the whole trick.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe custom annotation processor + IntelliJ plugin for Splunk audit parameters is the same shape of problem as a starter: a cross-cutting convention packaged once. If the runtime half were shared across services it belongs in an auto-configuration with @ConditionalOnMissingBean so any team can override it.

Worksheet

What does @SpringBootApplication consist of, and how does auto-configuration decide what to create?
Answer

1. Model Answer

It is @SpringBootConfiguration (a @Configuration) + @EnableAutoConfiguration + @ComponentScan (with exclude filters for auto-config and type-exclusion). @EnableAutoConfiguration imports every class listed in AutoConfiguration.imports files on the classpath (spring.factories before 2.7); each class is filtered by conditions evaluated early from annotation metadata (@ConditionalOnClass is checked without loading the class via ASM). Surviving classes register beans, usually @ConditionalOnMissingBean, so user definitions win. See why: --debug or /actuator/conditions shows positive/negative matches.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Whether the candidate understands auto-configuration as conditional, ordered imports instead of magic.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says '@SpringBootApplication is a shortcut and Spring Boot auto-configures things'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: It combines @SpringBootConfiguration, @EnableAutoConfiguration, and @ComponentScan. Auto-config classes come from AutoConfiguration.imports (before 2.7 spring.factories), each filtered by conditions on classpath, beans and properties.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that class-level conditions are evaluated early from annotation metadata so classes not on the classpath are never loaded, that user beans win through @ConditionalOnMissingBean, and how to inspect decisions via --debug//actuator/conditions. Notes @ComponentScan default package scope pitfalls. Uses exclusions (exclude, spring.autoconfigure.exclude) sparingly, and checks the report when a bean is unexpectedly missing or present. Understands that classpath changes alone can switch behaviour, such as adding a dependency enabling a security or DB auto-config.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats dependency additions as behavioural changes: dependency-diff review on upgrades, a conditions-report snapshot test to catch unexpected auto-configs, and a curated BOM and starters policy.
How do you replace or disable one auto-configured thing, for example the default ObjectMapper or DataSourceAutoConfiguration?
Answer

1. Model Answer

  • Customise via properties (spring.jackson.*) or a Jackson2ObjectMapperBuilderCustomizer bean (keeps Boot’s defaults).
  • Replace by defining your own @Bean ObjectMapper (auto-config backs off through @ConditionalOnMissingBean; you lose Boot’s defaults like FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES=false unless you configure them).
  • Disable: @SpringBootApplication(exclude = DataSourceAutoConfiguration.class) or spring.autoconfigure.exclude=.... Excluding by class name works even if the class is not on the classpath.

Prefer a customizer to a replacement bean.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knowing the right override level: customise, replace, or exclude, and the consequences of each.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Defines their own ObjectMapper bean and wonders why dates or unknown properties behave differently, or excludes auto-config blindly.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Customise via spring.jackson.* or Jackson2ObjectMapperBuilderCustomizer to keep Boot defaults; replace by defining your own ObjectMapper bean (auto-config backs off); disable with exclude on @SpringBootApplication or the property.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Warns that replacing loses Boot defaults such as FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES=false, registered modules, and consistent serialisation in MVC, messaging and clients, so in most cases a customizer is right. Verifies by serialising sample payloads in a test. Rule: customise first, replace last, exclude only when you really supply the whole thing. Notes that excluding DataSourceAutoConfiguration also affects JPA, Flyway and health indicators, so check the conditions report afterwards. Multiple ObjectMapper beans cause injection ambiguity (@Primary).
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises the JSON contract in a shared starter with a customizer so every service serialises identically, and tests the contract with golden files. Documents that replacing core beans needs an architectural reason.
How do you write your own starter / auto-configuration in Boot 3 and what changed from 2.x?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Two artifacts by convention: acme-spring-boot-autoconfigure (the config + @ConfigurationProperties) and acme-spring-boot-starter (empty jar with dependencies). Mark the config @AutoConfiguration (2.7), register in META-INF/spring/org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.AutoConfiguration.imports. In 2.x you used META-INF/spring.factories key org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.EnableAutoConfiguration; Boot 3.0 ignores that key. Rules: proxyBeanMethods=false, never component-scan, @ConditionalOnMissingBean on every bean, order with before/after, name the properties prefix after the starter, test with ApplicationContextRunner, and add the configuration processor for metadata. Other spring.factories keys (ApplicationListener, EnvironmentPostProcessor) still live there.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Experience building reusable Boot infrastructure and awareness of the packaging conventions and the 2.x to 3.x registration change.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'create a spring.factories file with your configuration class'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Two modules by convention: autoconfigure (config + properties) and starter (dependencies only). Use @AutoConfiguration and register in META-INF/spring/org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.AutoConfiguration.imports; in 2.x it was the EnableAutoConfiguration key in spring.factories, which Boot 3 no longer reads for this.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds correct conditions (@ConditionalOnClass, @ConditionalOnMissingBean), optional dependencies, the configuration processor for metadata, @AutoConfiguration(before/after) ordering, and testing with ApplicationContextRunner. Pitfall: an un-registered file means nothing happens and no error. Considers the compatibility contract: supporting both Boot 2.7 and 3.x needs both registration files, and API removals break consumers. Provides failure analysers, health indicators and metrics from the start.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Governs it as a platform product: ownership, versioning and deprecation policy, BOM, automated consumer upgrade PRs, and documentation. Challenges whether it must be a starter or could be a plain library with explicit wiring.
What is the difference between @ConditionalOnBean and @ConditionalOnMissingBean and what is the ordering trap?
Answer

1. Model Answer

They test the bean factory state at the time the condition is evaluated, not the final state. In user @Configuration classes the registration order is undefined enough that results are unreliable; in auto-configurations they are reliable because auto-configs are processed after user configs and ordered explicitly with @AutoConfiguration(after=...). So: use them only in auto-config (or with a clear ordering guarantee), and use @ConditionalOnProperty/@Profile in application config.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understanding of condition evaluation timing and why bean-presence conditions are only trustworthy in auto-configuration.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Treats @ConditionalOnBean as 'if the bean exists' without recognising timing.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Both test the bean factory state at evaluation time, not the final state. In user configs registration order makes results unreliable; in auto-configs it works because they process after user configs and are ordered via @AutoConfiguration(after=...).
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Gives the trap: a @ConditionalOnBean in a user config evaluated before the target bean is registered silently returns false, and the bean disappears with no error. Detects using the conditions report. Fixes: move into auto-configuration, add explicit ordering, or use @ConditionalOnProperty or plain wiring. Notes @ConditionalOnMissingBean has the mirror problem causing duplicate beans, and that specifying types explicitly avoids accidental matching. Prefers property-based conditions for application code.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Establishes a rule that bean conditions belong only in auto-configuration libraries, flagged in review for application code, with ApplicationContextRunner tests required for any conditional.
Startup is slow and the context loads far more than you need. How do you find out what is being auto-configured and trim it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. --debug or /actuator/conditions → list positive matches; remove starters you do not use (a transitive spring-boot-starter-data-redis or Actuator’s metrics-export adds beans and connection attempts).
  2. BufferingApplicationStartup (Boot 2.4) + /actuator/startup shows step timings per bean/phase.
  3. spring.main.lazy-initialization=true (Boot 2.2): faster boot, but failures move to first use and the first request pays latency; exclude critical beans with @Lazy(false).
  4. Narrow component scan; spring.jpa.open-in-view irrelevant; set spring.jmx.enabled=false (default false since 2.2).
  5. Class data sharing (Boot 3.3), AOT/native (3.0), or CRaC (3.2) for the big wins (see Packaging).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Performance diagnosis skills for startup: measuring before trimming, and knowing the trade-offs of the common levers.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'turn on lazy initialization' or 'remove unused dependencies' without measuring.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Use --debug or /actuator/conditions to list matches, remove unused starters, use BufferingApplicationStartup and /actuator/startup for per-bean timings, and spring.main.lazy-initialization=true for faster boot.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that lazy init moves cost and errors to the first request and hides wiring failures, so it suits dev/CLI use more than latency-sensitive prod. Other levers: exclude auto-configs, proxyBeanMethods=false, narrower component scan, heavy @PostConstruct work, class data sharing/AOT, and Hibernate's schema work. Verifies with before/after startup timelines. Separates JVM warm-up, classpath scanning, and I/O at start (connecting to Redis, Flyway). Points out that startup affects autoscaling reaction time and rollout speed.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a startup budget per service tracked in CI with the startup endpoint, and a platform base with CDS/AOT or native evaluated by cost-benefit. Treats a growing dependency list as a cost review item.

6 · Spring Web MVC: request flow, controllers, content negotiation

DispatcherServlet, handler mapping, argument resolvers, filters vs interceptors.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: DispatcherServlet maps a request to a handler, resolves arguments, converts bodies.
  • Must-know: ★ request flow · ★ filter vs interceptor · ★ content negotiation / HttpMessageConverter
  • Top pitfall: @RequestBody can be read once; wrong Accept gives 406.
  • Version markers: Spring 3.0 REST; 4.3 composed mappings
FeatureDetailSince
@RestController ★ MUST KNOW@Controller + @ResponseBodySpring 4.0
@GetMapping etc. composed annotationsShortcuts for @RequestMapping(method=...)Spring 4.3
ResponseEntity ★ MUST KNOWControl status, headers and bodySpring 3.0
Content negotiationAccept header, produces/consumes; query-param strategy removed as default in 5.xcore
PathPatternParser defaultFaster matching; trailing slash match off in 6.0; /** mid-pattern not allowedBoot 2.6 / Spring 6.0
@RequestBody, @PathVariable, @RequestParam, @RequestHeaderBindingcore
Functional endpoints (RouterFunction)Alternative to annotationsSpring 5.2 (MVC)
API versioning (version attribute)First-class versioning in mapping (verify details in release notes)Spring 7.0
⚠ PitfallFilters vs interceptors: a servlet Filter sees every request (including static content and error dispatches), runs before the DispatcherServlet, can wrap request/response, and is where Spring Security lives. A HandlerInterceptor runs only for requests matched to a handler, knows the HandlerMethod, has preHandle/postHandle/afterCompletion. Exceptions inside filters are not handled by @ControllerAdvice.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceRetail-platform-style services typically propagate a correlation/audit id: a servlet Filter (or Boot’s observation filter) puts it into MDC for every request, including error responses, whereas an interceptor would miss failures raised before handler mapping.

Worksheet

Walk through what happens from an HTTP request arriving at Tomcat until your @RestController method returns JSON.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Tomcat acceptor/worker thread → servlet filter chain (Boot registers e.g. CharacterEncodingFilter, ObservationFilter, Spring Security’s FilterChainProxy) → DispatcherServlet.doDispatch → HandlerMapping (RequestMappingHandlerMapping) finds the handler method + interceptors → HandlerAdapter invokes it: HandlerMethodArgumentResolvers build arguments (@RequestBody uses a HttpMessageConverter and then validation) → method runs → HandlerMethodReturnValueHandler writes the result with a message converter chosen by Accept/produces (Jackson) → interceptors’ postHandle/afterCompletion → back through the filters. Any exception goes to HandlerExceptionResolvers (ExceptionHandlerExceptionResolver → @ControllerAdvice, then ResponseStatusExceptionResolver, then default).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: End-to-end understanding of the servlet/MVC request pipeline, which supports debugging of filters, converters and error handling.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'request hits the controller, the controller returns JSON' with no mention of DispatcherServlet or message converters.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Walks through Tomcat worker thread, filter chain, DispatcherServlet.doDispatch, HandlerMapping, HandlerAdapter with argument resolvers, interceptors, controller invocation, return value handler with HttpMessageConverter (Jackson) writing the body.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Places exception handling (HandlerExceptionResolver, @ControllerAdvice), interceptor pre/post/afterCompletion, and security filters in the flow, and states what runs before the DispatcherServlet and so is outside MVC error handling. Shows where to set a breakpoint (doDispatch) for debugging. Uses the model to locate bugs: 415/406 come from converters or negotiation, 400 on bad JSON from HttpMessageNotReadableException before the handler, and filter exceptions bypass @ControllerAdvice. Knows thread-per-request and where a blocked worker pool shows up.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Uses it to set platform-wide boundaries: where tracing, auth, logging and error mapping live so behaviour is consistent across services, and documents the pipeline in onboarding material.
Servlet Filter vs HandlerInterceptor vs @ControllerAdvice: which for authentication, request logging with the handler name, and exception translation?
Answer

1. Model Answer

NeedUseWhy
AuthenticationFilter (Spring Security)Must run before and independent of MVC; protects non-MVC resources
Log handler name/timingInterceptor (or Observation)Has the HandlerMethod; afterCompletion even on exception
Exception to HTTP mapping@ControllerAdviceResolves exceptions thrown from handlers; filter exceptions need AuthenticationEntryPoint/HandlerExceptionResolver delegation

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Choosing the correct cross-cutting mechanism for each concern based on where in the pipeline it runs and what it can see.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Picks one tool for everything, often a filter for all three, or says they are 'basically the same'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Authentication belongs in a filter (Spring Security), request logging with handler name in an interceptor (or Observation), and exception translation in @ControllerAdvice.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains why: filters run before MVC and cover non-MVC resources; interceptors have the HandlerMethod and afterCompletion even on exceptions; @ControllerAdvice only sees exceptions thrown from handlers, so filter-level errors need AuthenticationEntryPoint / AccessDeniedHandler. Mentions the cost of reading the request body in a filter (needs caching wrapper). Adds pitfalls: multiple interceptors and ordering, async dispatch behaviour, and double-logging. Prefers Micrometer Observation for timing and tracing rather than hand-rolled logging.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises a shared starter owning auth, correlation ids and error format, so service teams do not reinvent them, with tests that check consistent 401/403/500 shapes across services.
POST /orders should return 201 with a Location header. Show it. How do you pick the right status codes for create/update/delete/conflict/validation?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Return ResponseEntity.created(uri).body(dto) (or @ResponseStatus(HttpStatus.CREATED)). Conventions: create 201 (+Location), update 200 or 204, delete 204 (idempotent: second delete also 204 or 404, document it), validation failure 400 (or 422 if the team chooses semantic errors), unauthenticated 401, forbidden 403, not found 404, optimistic-lock/duplicate 409, precondition (If-Match ETag mismatch) 412, rate limit 429, upstream failure 502/503/504. Errors should be a consistent body: RFC 7807 ProblemDetail (see next section).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: REST semantics and API usability: correct status codes, Location header, and the discipline to be consistent.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Returns 200 for everything, or 201 without a Location header, and picks 400 for any error.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Returns ResponseEntity.created(uri).body(dto). Create 201 with Location, update 200/204, delete 204, validation 400 (or 422), 401, 403, 404, and 409 for conflicts.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Builds the URI with ServletUriComponentsBuilder/UriComponentsBuilder rather than string concat, explains idempotency of PUT/DELETE and what second delete returns, and distinguishes 401 from 403. Tests with MockMvc asserting status and header. Rule: choose one convention per class of error and document it. Handles duplicates with an Idempotency-Key for POST, 409 for optimistic-lock/duplicate conflicts, and 202 for async creation with a status resource. Considers behind-proxy URL generation (forwarded headers).
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Publishes an API style guide with a status-code matrix, enforced via contract tests/OpenAPI linting (Spectral) in CI, so clients see consistent behaviour across teams.
Trailing slashes and path matching changed in Spring 6. What breaks on migration?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Spring Framework 6.0 removed trailing-slash matching by default: @GetMapping("/users") no longer matches /users/ (404). Boot 2.6 had already switched MVC to PathPatternParser (also affects Actuator and Springfox, which broke). Mid-pattern ** (/a/**/b) is rejected. Fix by redirecting at the edge or adding both paths; the global opt-in (configurePathMatch with setUseTrailingSlashMatch) is deprecated. Add a regression test for each public path and for clients that append a slash (API gateways, old SDKs).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Migration awareness: what Spring 6's path-matching changes break and how to handle them without hacks.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'URLs are matched differently now' and discovers 404s in production.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Spring 6 removed trailing-slash matching by default, so /users no longer matches /users/; Boot 2.6 had moved to PathPatternParser, affecting Actuator and Springfox; mid-pattern ** is rejected.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Gives fixes: redirect at the edge/gateway, map both paths explicitly, or update clients, and flags that re-enabling globally is deprecated. Finds affected endpoints by running contract/integration tests and checking access logs for 404s on slash URLs before cutover. Notes that gateways, health checks, and external callers (partners with hard-coded URLs) are the real risk, so a staged rollout with 404 monitoring on trailing-slash paths is safer than assuming.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Runs migration as a programme: inventory of callers via access logs, gateway normalisation rule, an OpenRewrite/automated pass, a deprecation notice to consumers, and a rollback plan.
How does content negotiation pick JSON vs XML vs CSV, and how do you version an API (URI vs header vs media type)? What does Spring 7 add?
Answer

1. Model Answer

The ContentNegotiationManager reads the Accept header (path-extension and ?format= strategies are off by default since 5.3/6.0 for security), intersects with the handler’s produces, and picks a HttpMessageConverter that can write the return type; failure → 406. Versioning: URI (/v1) is simple, cache- and gateway-friendly, the most common; header (X-API-Version) or media type (application/vnd.acme.v2+json) keep URLs stable but are harder to test and cache (needs Vary). Spring Framework 7.0 adds built-in API versioning support in the request mapping (version resolver by path segment, header, query param or media-type parameter; configured through WebMvcConfigurer) (verify exact API in release notes). Whatever you choose: additive changes only within a version, deprecate with Deprecation/Sunset headers.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: API design judgement: content negotiation mechanics and a justified API versioning approach, plus awareness of newer framework support.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'send Accept: application/json' and 'put /v1 in the URL' with no discussion of alternatives or 406.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: ContentNegotiationManager reads Accept, intersects with produces, and picks a converter, otherwise 406; extension and ?format= strategies are off by default. Versioning options: URI, header, media type; URI is simplest and cache/gateway friendly.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Compares trade-offs: URI versioning is visible and easy to route but proliferates; header/media-type is cleaner REST but harder to test and cache (Vary). Prefers additive, backward-compatible changes to avoid versions at all. Notes Spring Framework 7 adds first-class API versioning support but check the exact configuration in the release docs before relying on it. Adds deprecation signalling (Deprecation/Sunset headers), consumer-driven contract tests, and metrics per version to know when it is safe to retire.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines an organisation-wide versioning and deprecation policy with a gateway enforcing it, a fixed support window, and tooling that reports consumers on old versions.

7 · Validation and error handling (@ControllerAdvice, ProblemDetail RFC 7807)

Return one consistent, safe error shape for every failure.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: One consistent, safe error shape for every failure.
  • Must-know: ★ @Valid vs @Validated · ★ @RestControllerAdvice · ★ ProblemDetail
  • Top pitfall: unhandled ConstraintViolationException becomes a 500.
  • Version markers: Spring 6.0 ProblemDetail; 6.1 HandlerMethodValidationException

Sample problem+json

⚠ PitfallNever return exception messages/stack traces from SQL or internals. Boot’s server.error.include-message/include-stacktrace/include-binding-errors default to never (since 2.3); keep it that way.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceA consistent error contract matters in the HR/payroll style integrations (payroll outbound, Kafka eventing): the consumer must tell a retryable fault (503) from a business rejection (422/409). Mapping optimistic-lock failures to 409 (the retail platform uses optimistic locking, no pessimistic locks) gives clients the right retry semantics.

Worksheet

How do you implement global exception handling in Spring MVC?
Answer

1. Model Answer

A @RestControllerAdvice class with @ExceptionHandler methods; Spring picks the most specific exception type (closest in the hierarchy). Extending ResponseEntityExceptionHandler gives you ready-made mapping of Spring’s own exceptions (MethodArgumentNotValidException, HttpMessageNotReadableException, NoHandlerFoundException...) to ProblemDetail from 6.0. Scope with basePackages/annotations. Order multiple advices with @Order. Log once at the right level (4xx at debug/info, 5xx at error with stack and trace id). Exceptions from filters (including Security) are outside this mechanism.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Practical error-handling architecture in MVC and awareness of the built-in base class.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Wraps controller methods in try/catch blocks and returns strings.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: A @RestControllerAdvice with @ExceptionHandler methods, most specific exception wins; extending ResponseEntityExceptionHandler maps Spring's own exceptions to ProblemDetail.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains ordering when several advices exist (@Order), the distinction between domain exceptions mapped to statuses and unexpected ones logged at ERROR and returned as generic 500, and that filter-level exceptions are not covered. Tests with MockMvc. Avoids leaking internals, adds trace ids, logs once at the right level (4xx at WARN or lower, 5xx with stack trace), and avoids catch-all handlers swallowing framework exceptions into 500.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Ships the advice in a common library so all services return one error format, with contract tests, and reviews exception hierarchies to prevent each team inventing its own.
What is RFC 7807 / ProblemDetail and how do you enable it in a Boot 3 app?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Standard JSON error format application/problem+json with members type, title, status, detail, instance plus extensions. Spring 6.0 added ProblemDetail and ErrorResponse; any @ExceptionHandler can return a ProblemDetail. Boot 3.0: spring.mvc.problemdetails.enabled=true (and spring.webflux.problemdetails.enabled) registers a ProblemDetailsExceptionHandler for MVC exceptions. Benefits: one machine-readable contract for all services, type URI is a stable error code; for i18n, MessageSource keys problemDetail.title.* are supported. It is RFC 9457 now (compatible).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows the standard error format and how Boot/Spring wire it, plus its limits.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it is a standard JSON error format' but cannot say which fields or how to enable it.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: RFC 7807 application/problem+json with type, title, status, detail, instance plus extensions. Spring 6 added ProblemDetail/ErrorResponse; Boot 3 enables handling via spring.mvc.problemdetails.enabled=true.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Shows returning ProblemDetail from an @ExceptionHandler, adding properties via setProperty, and that the Boot property only covers Spring's built-in exceptions, not your domain ones. Notes content type and verifies with MockMvc. Points out type should be a stable documented URI, and default about:blank hides meaning. Adds trace id extension. Recognises RFC 9457 supersedes 7807 with compatible shape.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes it the organisation-wide error contract via a shared advice, documented error catalogue, and OpenAPI schema so client generation and support share one vocabulary.
Difference between @Valid and @Validated; how do you validate @PathVariable/@RequestParam and what exception results?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@Valid (Jakarta) triggers cascading validation of a bean (request body, nested objects). @Validated (Spring) additionally supports validation groups and, on a class, enables method-level validation through an AOP proxy (MethodValidationPostProcessor). Body validation failure → MethodArgumentNotValidException (400). Simple parameter constraints (@PathVariable @Min(1) long id) before 6.1 required @Validated on the controller and produced ConstraintViolationException (map it to 400 yourself or you get 500). Since Spring 6.1 controllers get built-in method validation producing HandlerMethodValidationException (400) without class-level @Validated. Notice the proxy implication: class-level @Validated makes the controller a CGLIB proxy.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Distinguishes bean-level and method-level validation mechanics, and knows which exceptions result.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Treats @Valid and @Validated as interchangeable and does not know why @Min on a @PathVariable is ignored.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: @Valid cascades bean validation; @Validated adds groups and, on a class, method validation via an AOP proxy. Body failures raise MethodArgumentNotValidException; parameter constraints raise ConstraintViolationException (handled to 400).
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes that without class-level @Validated simple-parameter constraints are not enforced, and that a proxy needs the call to cross the bean boundary. Spring 6.1 introduced built-in method validation raising HandlerMethodValidationException for controllers. Tests for each path. Warns that the exception type differs by Spring version, so one advice must handle both or the migration yields 500s. Careful with validation in service layers versus controllers.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets one validation approach per layer in the guidelines with shared advice mapping every exception to the same ProblemDetail, and regression tests across upgrades.
How do you design the error contract for a public API: what goes in type/title/detail, how do you avoid leaking internals, and what about correlating with logs?
Answer

1. Model Answer

type = stable documented URI per error class (client logic keys off it, not the message); title short and constant per type; detail instance-specific but safe for the caller (no SQL, host names, class names); status mirrors HTTP; extensions: traceId (from MDC/Observation so support can find the Splunk/ELK trail), errors[] for field-level validation. Log the full exception server-side exactly once; return an opaque 500 with a trace id for unexpected failures. Localise via MessageSource. Keep a contract test for each documented problem type so a refactor cannot change an error silently; document in OpenAPI with a shared ProblemDetail schema. Retryability hints: Retry-After header for 429/503.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: API design maturity: error contract as a product for clients, balanced against security and operability.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Returns the exception message and sometimes stack trace in the body, or a bare {error: "failed"}.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: type is a stable URI per error class, title constant, detail safe and instance-specific, status mirrors HTTP, extensions like traceId and errors[] for field-level validation.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that clients should branch on type/code not message, no SQL, hostnames or class names in detail, and traceId from MDC/Observation lets support find logs. Localisation and message stability are considered. Adds that validation details can leak enumeration info, and rate-limit or generic messages for auth endpoints. Logs the full cause server-side only.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Maintains an error catalogue with owners, versioned, linted in CI, and links error rates by type to SLO dashboards so error classes are measurable.
A 500 appears for malformed JSON and a stack trace shows in the response of one endpoint but not another. How do you debug the inconsistency?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Likely causes: (1) the first is thrown by a HttpMessageConverter before the handler; ResponseEntityExceptionHandler handles HttpMessageNotReadableException → 400, but a custom @ExceptionHandler(Exception.class) without it extending the base maps it to 500; (2) two @ControllerAdvice beans with no @Order: the first matching wins; (3) the leaking endpoint is outside MVC (a servlet, Actuator, or a Filter that throws) so it falls through to Boot’s BasicErrorController//error with server.error.include-stacktrace or devtools set; (4) a profile enabling include-stacktrace=always. Reproduce with MockMvc, print HandlerExceptionResolver order (HandlerExceptionResolverComposite), and add a test asserting the response of each failure path.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Systematic debugging of inconsistent error behaviour across endpoints, hypothesis-driven rather than random.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add a try/catch' or 'check the logs' without a method.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Suspects the converter failing before the handler, a catch-all @ExceptionHandler(Exception.class) mapping HttpMessageNotReadableException to 500, multiple @ControllerAdvice without @Order, and that the leaking endpoint is outside the advice or has a local handler.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Checks advice scope (basePackages, assignableTypes), server.error.include-stacktrace, the BasicErrorController fallback, and reproduces with MockMvc. Verifies by breakpoint in doResolveHandlerMethodException. Considers Boot's error page path for filter-originated errors and a different servlet context or gateway adding stack traces. Fixes at the root with a single advice and a regression test.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Prevents recurrence through a shared error-handling library, a contract test covering malformed JSON/validation/unknown route on every service, and a security scan checking responses for stack traces.

8 · HTTP clients: RestTemplate, WebClient, RestClient and @HttpExchange

Outbound calls fail more often than inbound ones: timeouts and retries are design decisions.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Outbound calls fail more often than inbound; timeouts and retries are design decisions.
  • Must-know: ★ RestClient (blocking) vs WebClient · ★ timeouts always set · ★ @HttpExchange interfaces
  • Top pitfall: default RestTemplate/JDK timeouts are infinite or very long.
  • Version markers: RestClient 6.1; @HttpExchange 6.0
ClientStyleStatusSince
RestTemplateBlocking, template methodsMaintenance mode since 5.0; formal deprecation planned for 7.1 and removal in 8 (verify in release notes)Spring 3.0
WebClient ★ MUST KNOWNon-blocking/reactive, fluent; works with MVC too (.block())Active; needs spring-webfluxSpring 5.0
RestClient ★ MUST KNOWBlocking fluent API (WebClient-like), same converters as RestTemplateRecommended synchronous clientSpring 6.1 / Boot 3.2
@HttpExchange interfaces ★ MUST KNOWDeclarative client generated as a proxy over RestClient/WebClient/RestTemplateActiveSpring 6.0
@ImportHttpServices / client group registryRegister many HTTP interface clients with shared config (verify details)NewSpring 7.0 / Boot 4.0
ClientHttpRequestFactorySettings, spring.http.client.*Boot-level timeout/factory configBoot 3.4 (verify property names)
🔑 Key ideaAlways set connect timeout and read (response) timeout. Defaults are infinite or very long on several factories, and one slow dependency then exhausts your Tomcat/virtual-thread/pool capacity: that is how a downstream blip becomes your outage.
⚠ PitfallRestTemplate/RestClient default SimpleClientHttpRequestFactory (JDK HttpURLConnection) has no pooling and no timeouts by default. For production use the Apache HttpClient 5 or JDK HttpClient factory with a PoolingHttpClientConnectionManager (max total/per route), connection TTL, and both timeouts. Apache HttpClient 4 support was removed in Spring 6.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe fork-and-join aggregation (parallel calls to several backends with graceful degradation on partial failure) needs exactly this: a bounded per-call timeout, a bounded pool and a per-dependency fallback, so one slow service degrades one section of the response rather than the whole response.

Worksheet

RestTemplate vs WebClient vs RestClient: which would you choose today and why?
Answer

1. Model Answer

For a Spring MVC (blocking) service on Boot 3.2+: RestClient: fluent API, same HttpMessageConverters and ClientHttpRequestInterceptors as RestTemplate, easy migration, no reactive dependency. WebClient when you need streaming, reactive composition (Mono.zip fan-out) or you are on WebFlux; it is the only one that does non-blocking I/O. RestTemplate is in maintenance mode: only in legacy code. Put a declarative @HttpExchange interface on top whichever you use, so business code does not depend on the client. On virtual threads (Java 21) blocking RestClient scales well, which narrows WebClient’s advantage to streaming.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Currency with Spring's HTTP client options and the judgement to choose by workload, not fashion.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'WebClient is the new one so use it' or keeps RestTemplate by default.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: For blocking MVC on Boot 3.2+, RestClient: fluent, shares converters and interceptors with RestTemplate, no reactive dependency. WebClient for streaming/reactive composition or WebFlux; RestTemplate is in maintenance mode.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Warns that WebClient .block() in an MVC app brings Reactor with little benefit and needs care on threads. Mentions building RestClient from RestClient.Builder so Boot customizers and observability apply, and that the timeouts are configured on the underlying request factory. Notes with virtual threads, blocking clients scale well, reducing the case for reactive clients. Migration of RestTemplate is incremental since RestClient.create(restTemplate) reuses config.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises on one blocking client in the platform starter with timeouts, TLS, tracing and retry guidance preconfigured, bans new RestTemplate usage via ArchUnit, and plans the removal timeline.
Show how @HttpExchange works and what you must still configure yourself.
Answer

1. Model Answer

You declare an interface with @HttpExchange/@GetExchange/@PostExchange methods using the same argument annotations as controllers; HttpServiceProxyFactory creates a JDK proxy that translates calls to an adapter (RestClientAdapter, WebClientAdapter, RestTemplateAdapter). It gives no resilience: timeouts, pooling, auth headers (interceptor / ClientRequestFilter), retries and error mapping (defaultStatusHandler) still live in the underlying client. Exceptions are the client’s (RestClientResponseException / WebClientResponseException). Spring 7 / Boot 4 add a registry and @ImportHttpServices to avoid per-interface factory boilerplate (verify in release notes).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knowledge of declarative HTTP interfaces and honest understanding of what they do not provide.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says '@HttpExchange is like Feign, it handles everything'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Declare an interface with @GetExchange/@PostExchange; HttpServiceProxyFactory creates a JDK proxy over an adapter (RestClientAdapter, WebClientAdapter). It does not give timeouts, pooling, auth, retries.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Shows the setup: build RestClient with base URL, interceptors and request factory timeouts, then create the proxy and register it as a bean. Error mapping uses status handlers. Tests with MockRestServiceServer. Highlights that resilience (circuit breaker, retry) is added with annotations or wrappers around the proxy, and observability comes through the underlying client. Argues one client per downstream with its own timeouts.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines a shared factory in the platform library so every service-to-service client has consistent timeouts, auth propagation and metrics, and generates interfaces from OpenAPI where possible.
Design the timeout and retry policy for a call to a downstream service in a request path with a 2 s SLA.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Budget: the whole request has 2 s, so per-call timeout must be a fraction: e.g. connect 200-500 ms, read 800 ms, at most one retry only for idempotent operations (GET, PUT, or POST with an idempotency key) and only on transient causes (connect timeout, 502/503/504, 429 with Retry-After), with exponential backoff + jitter, and a retry budget so that retries across callers cannot multiply load (3 layers × 3 attempts = 27x). Combine with a circuit breaker (stop calling an unhealthy host) and a bulkhead (cap concurrent calls per dependency), and a fallback (cached/stale/default) when partial results are acceptable. Never retry non-idempotent calls blindly: a read timeout does not mean the server did not process it. Emit metrics per attempt (http.client.requests).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Resilience design under a latency budget: timeouts, retries, idempotency and avoiding retry storms.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add a retry of 3 and a 30 s timeout' and ignores the SLA and idempotency.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Divides the 2 s SLA into per-call budgets (connect 200-500 ms, read 800 ms), at most one retry for idempotent calls on transient failures with backoff and jitter, and a retry budget.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains retries multiply load (3 layers each retrying 3 times gives 27x), so retry at one layer only, respect Retry-After, and combine with a circuit breaker and bulkhead. POST only with idempotency keys. Verifies with fault-injection and p99 metrics. Considers total deadline propagation so retries stop when remaining budget is too small, and hedged requests where appropriate. Tracks retry rate and timeout rate as metrics with alerts.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises defaults in the shared client starter, with SLO-driven budgets per dependency tier, chaos testing in staging, and a design review checklist for any new downstream call.
Your service calls 4 backends per request. How do you do it in parallel with a blocking Spring MVC app, and how do you degrade gracefully?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Fan out with CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(call, boundedExecutor) (or virtual threads from Java 21, or WebClient Mono.zip), each with .orTimeout(800, MILLISECONDS) (Java 9) and .exceptionally(ex -> fallback) returning an “absent” marker; join with allOf; build the response from whatever succeeded and flag partials ("degraded": ["recommendations"] or a header) rather than failing everything. Mark which dependencies are critical (fail the request) vs optional (degrade). Use a dedicated bounded executor per dependency (bulkhead), propagate MDC/security context with a TaskDecorator, and cancel outstanding futures when the deadline passes. Test with MockServer delays.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Concurrency and graceful degradation design: parallel fan-out with bounded resources and partial results.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Calls the four backends sequentially, or uses parallelStream() and unbounded threads.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Uses CompletableFuture.supplyAsync with a bounded executor (or virtual threads), orTimeout, exceptionally fallbacks, allOf join, and builds a partial response flagged as degraded.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains choosing which dependencies are critical versus optional, propagating MDC/trace context across threads, sizing the pool, and avoiding the common pool. Cancels outstanding work when the deadline passes. Tests with a slow stub. Adds bulkheads per backend so one slow service cannot exhaust the pool, metrics on degraded rates, and notes structured concurrency/virtual threads simplify this. Deals with partial failure semantics for clients.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes degraded-response contract explicit in the API, defines per-backend SLOs and fallback policies with product owners, and provides a shared fan-out helper in the platform library.
Intermittent “Connection reset” / “Premature close” errors against an internal service after idle periods. What is happening?
Answer

1. Model Answer

A load balancer or the server closes idle keep-alive connections (e.g. after 60 s) while your pool still holds them; the next request reuses a stale connection and fails. Fixes: set the pool’s connection TTL / max idle below the peer’s idle timeout (Reactor Netty maxIdleTime, Apache setTimeToLive, evictExpiredConnections), validate on lease (validateAfterInactivity), retry once for idempotent calls, and check Hikari-like max-lifetime semantics on the DB side. Confirm with a packet capture or by correlating errors with the first request after idle.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Network-level production debugging: connecting intermittent errors to idle keep-alive mismatches.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'network issue, retry it' or blames the downstream service.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: The LB or server closes idle keep-alive connections while the client pool still holds them, so the next reuse fails. Fix by setting pool max idle/TTL below the peer's idle timeout, validating on lease, and evicting expired connections.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Confirms by correlating failures with idle gaps, packet capture or LB idle-timeout settings, and checks the pool configuration (Reactor Netty maxIdleTime, Apache setTimeToLive, evictExpiredConnections). Retries are safe only for idempotent requests. Notes defaults often differ per layer (client pool, service, LB, NAT gateways with 350 s timeouts), and the pattern is worst after quiet periods like overnight. Adds metrics for pool leases and resets.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Documents a platform rule: client idle time must be lower than every hop's idle timeout, set as defaults in the shared client config, with a runbook and a test environment simulating idle-closing proxies.

9 · Spring Data JPA: queries, N+1, projections, locking, batching

The persistence bugs that actually page you: lazy loading, N+1, OSIV, missing batches.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Spring Data JPA: derived queries, projections, locking and batching.
  • Must-know: ★ N+1 fixes (fetch join, EntityGraph) · ★ @Version optimistic lock · ★ OSIV and lazy loading
  • Top pitfall: lazy loading in the view layer and N+1 queries.
  • Version markers: Spring Data 1.x; Hibernate 6 with Boot 3
