🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe retail platform’s concurrency control uses optimistic locking and no pessimistic locks: a @Version column, a conflict becomes ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException, mapped to HTTP 409 (or retried for internal callers). Benefit: no row locks held across user think-time or remote calls, no deadlocks; cost: conflict handling and retry code.

Worksheet

What is the N+1 select problem and how do you detect and fix it in Spring Data JPA?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Loading N parents then lazily touching a collection/association issues one extra query per parent: 1 + N round trips. Detect: spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.generate_statistics=true, logging.level.org.hibernate.SQL=debug (or datasource-proxy / p6spy), a test asserting statement count (Hibernate Statistics), APM traces showing repeated identical queries. Fix per case: @EntityGraph or JOIN FETCH for “always need it” (watch cartesian products with two collections: use @BatchSize/default_batch_fetch_size for the second), DTO projections when you do not need entities, and keep associations LAZY (@ManyToOne defaults to EAGER: set it LAZY explicitly). Pitfall: JOIN FETCH + pagination makes Hibernate paginate in memory (warning HHH90003004): page ids first, then fetch.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Persistence performance fundamentals: recognising N+1 and choosing a fix with an understanding of the trade-offs.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Defines it as 'many queries' and answers 'use EAGER fetch', which makes it worse.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Loading N parents then lazily touching an association issues N extra queries. Detect with SQL logging, Hibernate statistics, datasource-proxy/p6spy, a statement-count test, or APM traces. Fix with fetch join, @EntityGraph, batch fetching, or DTO projections.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains fetch join with pagination pitfalls (in-memory pagination warning HHH000104) and multiple bag fetch exceptions, and that @BatchSize or default_batch_fetch_size is a good general fix. EAGER does not eliminate it. Rule: LAZY by default, fix per use case. Prevents regressions with statement-count assertions in tests and slow-query monitoring. Uses projections for read-only listing endpoints to avoid loading entities at all.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes it an organisation guardrail: a test utility asserting query counts in integration tests, a default batch-size setting in the platform config, database-side query dashboards, and training from real incidents.
Interface projection vs DTO projection vs entity: when do you use each? What about dynamic projections?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Entities when you will modify and save. Interface (closed) projections select only the declared properties (SQL narrows to those columns); open projections (@Value("#{...}")) load the whole entity. Record/class DTO projections use constructor expressions (select new com.acme.OrderRow(o.id, o.status) or derived queries): immutable, type-safe, no proxies. Dynamic projection: <T> List<T> findByStatus(Status s, Class<T> type) picks the shape per caller. Projections are read-only, skip the persistence context (no dirty checking), so they are cheaper for list endpoints. Native queries work with interface projections via aliases.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows that projection choice changes the SQL and the persistence-context cost, not just the return type.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'projections return fewer fields' and 'use DTOs for read-only'. Cannot say whether an interface projection actually narrows the SELECT, and treats entities as the default for everything.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains closed interface projections select only declared columns, record DTOs use constructor expressions, and entities are for modify-and-save. Knows dynamic projections via a generic Class<T> parameter.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Calls out that open projections with @Value("#{...}") load the whole entity and silently lose the benefit, and that proxies of interface projections are slower than records. Verifies with show_sql or a SQL log that the column list narrowed. Rule: entity only when mutating, record DTO for hot read paths, closed projection for simple reads.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a team convention (read side uses records/DTO queries, entities only inside command handlers) and backs it with an ArchUnit rule forbidding entities in controller signatures. Ties it to cost: fewer columns and no dirty-checking snapshots lower DB I/O and heap churn, which shows up in p99 and GC metrics. Questions whether the read model should be a separate query model when joins get complex.
Explain optimistic vs pessimistic locking in JPA. How does @Version work and how do you handle the failure?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Optimistic: @Version column; Hibernate issues update ... set ..., version=version+1 where id=? and version=?; zero rows updated → OptimisticLockException wrapped by Spring as ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException. No DB locks held, high throughput when conflicts are rare. Pessimistic: @Lock(PESSIMISTIC_WRITE) → SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, serialises writers, risk of lock waits, deadlocks and held connections; fine for short critical sections with high contention (counters, inventory decrement).

Handling: for user-facing edits return 409 with the current state (or use ETag/If-Match with the version); for internal commands retry the whole transaction (re-read, re-apply) with bounded attempts and jitter; the retry must wrap the @Transactional method from outside (a retry inside the failed transaction is pointless). Expose the version in the DTO so clients send it back. Bulk JPQL updates bypass @Version unless you increment it explicitly.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Trade-off thinking between optimistic and pessimistic locking, including what happens when the conflict actually occurs.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'optimistic uses a version column, pessimistic locks the row' and stops. Has no plan for the exception and would just let the 500 bubble up.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes the update ... where id=? and version=? mechanics, zero rows updated leading to ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException, and PESSIMISTIC_WRITE mapping to SELECT ... FOR UPDATE. Mentions retrying or returning 409.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Chooses by contention: optimistic when conflicts are rare, pessimistic for hot rows where retries would thrash, with lock timeouts and consistent lock ordering to avoid deadlocks. Retries by re-reading in a new transaction (not inside the failed one) with a bounded count, and surfaces 409 plus ETag/version to clients on user-facing edits. Tests it with two concurrent threads or a latch.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises on version columns exposed as ETags with If-Match across services so lost updates are handled uniformly, and documents when pessimistic or queue-based serialisation is allowed. Tracks conflict rate and retry exhaustion as metrics so a rising rate signals a contention design problem rather than a tuning problem. Considers whether a hot row should be redesigned (append-only, partitioning, atomic SQL update) instead of locked.
What is Open Session In View, why is it on by default, and why do most teams turn it off?
Answer

1. Model Answer

OpenEntityManagerInViewInterceptor keeps the EntityManager (and its DB connection after first use) open for the entire web request so lazy associations can be loaded in the controller/serialisation. Boot logs a warning because it defaults to true. Problems: the connection is held through view rendering and any remote calls → pool starvation under load; lazy loads in JSON serialisation hide N+1; transactional boundaries become unclear. Turn it off (spring.jpa.open-in-view=false), fetch what you need in the service layer (graph/projection) and return DTOs; then accidental lazy access throws LazyInitializationException in tests, which is the point.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Awareness of a hidden default that holds DB connections far longer than expected, and the judgement to disable it responsibly.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'OSIV lets lazy loading work in the view' and has no idea Boot enables it or that it affects the pool. Fixes LazyInitializationException by switching to EAGER.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains OpenEntityManagerInViewInterceptor keeps the EntityManager for the whole request, that Boot warns because spring.jpa.open-in-view defaults to true, and that it risks pool starvation.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes the connection is held across serialisation and remote calls, so slow downstreams exhaust Hikari under load, and lazy loads in the view cause hidden N+1. Turns it off, then fixes the resulting LazyInitializationException with fetch joins, @EntityGraph or DTO projections, and checks pool pending metrics before and after.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes open-in-view=false a platform default in the shared starter and adds a test or startup check to enforce it. Rolls it out per service by first logging lazy loads outside transactions, then fixing the offenders, rather than flipping it blindly. Frames the real fix as an explicit read-model boundary, with connection-hold time as an SLO-relevant metric.
Batch inserting 100k rows is slow with saveAll. What do you check and change?
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. IDENTITY id generation disables JDBC batching (Hibernate must insert to get the id). Use SEQUENCE with allocationSize=50 and a matching DB sequence INCREMENT BY 50 (Hibernate pooled optimizer), or assign ids yourself.
  2. hibernate.jdbc.batch_size=50, order_inserts=true (and order_updates), and for Postgres reWriteBatchedInserts=true on the JDBC URL.
  3. Process in chunks: saveAll(chunk); em.flush(); em.clear(); every 500-1000 rows to cap the persistence-context memory and dirty-checking cost; commit per chunk so a failure does not roll back everything.
  4. If you do not need entities: JdbcTemplate.batchUpdate/JdbcClient, or Postgres COPY, which is an order of magnitude faster; or Spring Batch for restartability.
  5. Check the statement count and the DB side (indexes/triggers that slow inserts).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Systematic performance diagnosis of ORM bulk writes: knowing which settings silently disable JDBC batching.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'use saveAll' or 'increase the batch size' and perhaps 'use native SQL'. Does not know IDENTITY ids defeat batching.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Identifies IDENTITY generation as blocking batching, switches to SEQUENCE with allocationSize=50 matching the DB increment, and sets hibernate.jdbc.batch_size, order_inserts and Postgres reWriteBatchedInserts=true.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Also flushes and clears the persistence context every batch to avoid memory growth and dirty-check cost, and checks that allocationSize matches the sequence increment. Verifies batching with Hibernate statistics or datasource-proxy logging that shows batch execution counts, and benchmarks against JdbcTemplate.batchUpdate or COPY when 100k rows is still too slow.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Decides per workload whether JPA is the right tool at all: bulk loads go through JDBC batch, COPY or Spring Batch with restartability, while JPA handles transactional domain writes. Puts a batching assertion in integration tests so someone switching back to IDENTITY fails CI. Considers ingest windows, lock and WAL impact on a shared database, and throttling.

10 · Transactions: propagation, isolation, rollback rules and the self-invocation trap

@Transactional is proxy-based AOP: most “it did not roll back” bugs are about that.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: @Transactional is proxy AOP; most "no rollback" bugs are about that.
  • Must-know: ★ propagation REQUIRED vs REQUIRES_NEW · ★ rollback only on unchecked by default · ★ self-invocation bypasses proxy
  • Top pitfall: catching the exception inside the method prevents rollback.
  • Version markers: Spring 1.2 / 3.1 @EnableTransactionManagement
PropagationBehaviourUse
REQUIRED ★ MUST KNOW (default)Join existing, else start newNormal service method
REQUIRES_NEW ★ MUST KNOWSuspend outer tx, start an independent one (commits/rolls back separately; needs a second connection)Audit log that must persist even if business tx rolls back
SUPPORTS / NOT_SUPPORTEDJoin if present / run without tx (suspends)Read helpers / non-transactional calls
MANDATORY / NEVERMust be inside / must not be insideGuard rails
NESTEDJDBC savepoint within the outer tx (works with JdbcTemplate; with JPA/Hibernate the savepoint is used but entities already loaded are NOT reverted)Partial rollback in JDBC code
IsolationPreventsNotes
READ_UNCOMMITTEDNothingRare
READ_COMMITTEDDirty readsDefault for PostgreSQL, Oracle; each statement sees committed data
REPEATABLE_READ+ non-repeatable readsMySQL InnoDB default; Postgres snapshot (no phantoms in PG)
SERIALIZABLE+ phantomsPostgres SSI may abort with serialization failure: retry
🔑 Key ideaRollback rule: by default only unchecked exceptions (RuntimeException, Error) roll back. A checked exception commits unless @Transactional(rollbackFor = Exception.class). Also: if an inner REQUIRED method throws and the outer catches it, the shared tx is already marked rollback-only → outer commit throws UnexpectedRollbackException.
⚠ PitfallSelf-invocation: this.inner() inside the same bean bypasses the proxy, so the @Transactional on inner() is ignored. Same for private/final methods, non-Spring-managed objects and (by default) protected/package-private methods in older versions (public-only until 6.0; since 6.0 class-based proxies also support protected and package-private). Fixes: move method to another bean, inject self (@Lazy), TransactionTemplate, or AspectJ mode.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceFor the hourly batch jobs and payroll extracts, commit per chunk (not one giant transaction) so a failure at row 90,000 keeps the first 89,000 chunks and a restart resumes. With Kafka eventing in the HR systems, publish events from committed state (transactional outbox or @TransactionalEventListener(AFTER_COMMIT)) so consumers never see events for rolled-back data.

Worksheet

What are the default rollback rules of @Transactional? Show how to roll back on a checked exception.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Rolls back on RuntimeException and Error; commits on checked exceptions. Use @Transactional(rollbackFor = Exception.class) (or a specific class), noRollbackFor to exclude, or TransactionAspectSupport.currentTransactionStatus().setRollbackOnly(). Common mistake: catching the exception inside the method and swallowing it, so the proxy sees a normal return and commits.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows the exact default rollback contract and the ways it is silently bypassed.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it rolls back on exceptions'. Believes checked exceptions also roll back, or that adding @Transactional makes everything atomic.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: States rollback on RuntimeException and Error, commit on checked, and shows rollbackFor = Exception.class. Mentions noRollbackFor and setRollbackOnly().
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Warns that catching and swallowing the exception makes the proxy see a normal return and commit, and that a nested @Transactional that throws marks the shared transaction rollback-only, causing UnexpectedRollbackException at the outer commit. Writes a test that asserts the row is absent after a checked exception.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Proposes a team convention: either a custom composed annotation with rollbackFor = Exception.class or avoid checked exceptions in service layers, so behaviour is uniform. Adds a review checklist item and a lint or ArchUnit rule for bare @Transactional on methods that declare checked exceptions. Explains why the default exists (EJB heritage) to help newcomers.
Why does @Transactional not work when called from another method of the same class? List all the cases where it silently does nothing.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Spring applies it through a proxy (JDK or CGLIB) that wraps the bean; a call via this goes straight to the target and never passes through the TransactionInterceptor. Same family: (1) self-invocation, (2) private/final/static methods (can’t be overridden), (3) methods of an object created with new, (4) wrong @Transactional (javax/jakarta.transaction works but lacks some attributes), (5) called in @PostConstruct (proxy not yet applied), (6) non-public methods on pre-6.0 frameworks, (7) @EnableTransactionManagement missing (Boot auto-configures it), (8) exception swallowed or checked exception without rollbackFor, (9) another thread: the transaction is thread-bound, so work in @Async/CompletableFuture/parallel streams is outside it, (10) the transaction manager is the wrong one in multi-datasource apps. Diagnose: logging.level.org.springframework.transaction.interceptor=TRACE or TransactionSynchronizationManager.isActualTransactionActive().

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands the proxy mechanism well enough to enumerate silent no-op cases rather than just naming self-invocation.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it is a Spring limitation, move the method to another class'. Cannot list other cases or explain the proxy.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains the JDK or CGLIB proxy wraps the bean, so this.method() bypasses the TransactionInterceptor. Lists private/final/static methods, objects created with new, wrong annotation import, and non-public methods on interface proxies.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that @Transactional on a @PostConstruct or during bean init may run before the proxy exists, that checked rollback rules and wrong propagation also surprise, and that a non-Spring-managed thread or @Async hop changes the transaction. Proves it with a debugger (AopUtils.isAopProxy), TransactionSynchronizationManager.isActualTransactionActive() or tx logging at TRACE. Fix by extracting a bean, not injecting self.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats it as a recurring defect class: standardises on transaction boundaries at application-service level, adds ArchUnit rules for annotation placement, and enables TRACE tx logging in integration tests. Evaluates AspectJ weaving as a migration option only if self-invocation is unavoidable, weighing the build complexity. Teaches it via a short internal doc with the failing test as an example.
REQUIRES_NEW: when to use it and what are the dangers?
Answer

1. Model Answer

It suspends the current transaction and uses a second connection with an independent commit. Use for work that must persist regardless of the outer outcome (audit/attempt log, idempotency-key reservation, sequence allocation). Dangers: (1) pool deadlock: each thread holds one connection and needs another; with pool size 10 and 10 concurrent callers all wait forever (Hikari connectionTimeout errors). Keep pool > max concurrency × 2 or avoid. (2) Data inconsistency: inner commit stays even if outer rolls back. (3) The inner tx cannot see uncommitted outer changes (isolation) → surprises with foreign keys/locks (self-deadlock on a row the outer locked). (4) Only works across beans (proxy).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Judgement about when a second independent transaction is justified and awareness of its operational dangers.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'REQUIRES_NEW starts a new transaction' and suggests it as a fix whenever something rolls back unexpectedly.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains it suspends the outer transaction and uses a second connection with an independent commit; good for audit logs, idempotency reservations. Mentions it can break atomicity.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Identifies pool deadlock: each thread holds one connection and waits for another, so with pool size N and N concurrent callers everything stalls. Also notes the inner commit survives an outer rollback, lock waits against rows the outer already holds, and visibility differences. Mitigates with a pool sized above concurrency, short inner work, and a test that saturates the pool.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Restricts its use through review guidance and wrapper abstractions (an audit publisher) rather than scattered annotations. Monitors Hikari pending threads and connection timeout as alerts to catch the deadlock pattern early. Prefers designs that avoid it entirely, such as an outbox row in the same transaction, and documents the exceptions.
Explain the isolation levels and what Postgres/MySQL/Oracle give you by default. When would you raise it, and what is the cost?
Answer

1. Model Answer

READ_COMMITTED (Postgres, Oracle default): no dirty reads, but non-repeatable reads and phantoms possible. REPEATABLE_READ (MySQL InnoDB): consistent snapshot within the tx; in Postgres it is snapshot isolation (prevents phantoms too) and may throw serialization failures on write conflicts (SQLSTATE 40001). SERIALIZABLE: strongest; Postgres uses SSI and aborts transactions on dependency cycles → you must retry. Raise it only for a proven anomaly (write skew, e.g. two doctors going off-call); usually cheaper alternatives: @Version, SELECT FOR UPDATE on the specific row, or a unique constraint. Cost: aborts/retries and lower concurrency. Set it per method, @Transactional(isolation = REPEATABLE_READ); not all JPA providers support changing isolation per tx on JpaTransactionManager without a dialect (HibernateJpaDialect does).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Precise knowledge of isolation anomalies and engine-specific behaviour, plus cost-aware decision making.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Recites the four levels and the anomaly table but thinks defaults are the same across databases and says 'use SERIALIZABLE to be safe'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Knows READ_COMMITTED is default for Postgres and Oracle, REPEATABLE_READ for MySQL InnoDB, and describes dirty, non-repeatable and phantom reads. Notes Postgres REPEATABLE_READ is snapshot isolation.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that higher levels trade throughput for correctness and in Postgres can throw serialization failures (SQLSTATE 40001) that need retry logic. Prefers targeted fixes (SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, unique constraints, optimistic versions) over globally raising isolation. Reproduces the anomaly in a two-session test before changing anything.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a default of READ_COMMITTED plus explicit constraints, with SERIALIZABLE allowed only for named invariants and wrapped in a standard retry helper. Tracks serialization failure and retry rates as metrics and documents per-engine differences for teams on mixed databases. Challenges the premise by asking which invariant is actually at risk.
How do you make “update the DB and publish an event/message” reliable? Compare @TransactionalEventListener, the outbox pattern and Kafka transactions.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Dual write problem: commit then crash → no event; publish then rollback → phantom event. Options: @TransactionalEventListener(phase = AFTER_COMMIT) (4.2): event fires only after commit, but if the process dies right after commit (or the send fails) the event is lost; fine for best-effort (cache eviction, notifications). Note AFTER_COMMIT runs when the tx resources are still bound: a new write needs REQUIRES_NEW. Transactional outbox: insert the event into an outbox table in the same transaction; a relay (poller or CDC e.g. Debezium) publishes to Kafka at-least-once and marks it sent; consumers dedupe by event id. Guarantees no loss, adds latency and a relay to operate. Kafka transactions (transaction-id-prefix, KafkaTransactionManager) give atomic multi-topic writes + offset commit (consume-transform-produce) but do not make DB + Kafka atomic (best-efforts 1PC chaining at most). For DB→Kafka, choose the outbox.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Reasoning about the dual-write problem and choosing a delivery guarantee that matches the business need.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'publish the event after saving' or 'use @Transactional on the method that calls Kafka'. Assumes a DB transaction covers the broker.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes the dual write problem and compares @TransactionalEventListener(AFTER_COMMIT) (event lost on crash), the outbox pattern (atomic row, relay publishes at-least-once) and Kafka transactions (atomic only within Kafka).
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Picks the outbox for must-not-lose events, with idempotent consumers because delivery is at-least-once, and notes AFTER_COMMIT runs after the transaction resources may be gone so DB work needs REQUIRES_NEW. Weighs relay options: polling vs CDC (Debezium), ordering per aggregate key, and lag. Tests by killing the process between commit and publish.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides the outbox as a shared library or platform capability with a standard schema, relay, retention and dead-letter handling so teams do not reinvent it. Monitors outbox lag and oldest-unsent age as SLO signals, and plans migration of existing best-effort listeners by criticality. Sets an architecture rule: any cross-system state change uses the outbox unless explicitly waived.
readOnly = true: what does it actually do?
Answer

1. Model Answer

It is a hint: Spring sets the JDBC connection read-only (driver/DB may optimise or reject writes, Postgres rejects), and with Hibernate sets flush mode to MANUAL (skips dirty checking, so less CPU and no accidental updates, and snapshots are not kept for read-only entities in some paths). It does not prevent writes to non-transactional resources, and with a plain DataSource some drivers ignore it. Combine with AbstractRoutingDataSource to send reads to replicas, accounting for replication lag (read-your-writes).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows what readOnly really does and does not guarantee instead of treating it as a safety switch.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'readOnly makes the transaction read-only so you cannot write' and uses it everywhere as a performance magic flag.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains it is a hint: sets the JDBC connection read-only and Hibernate flush mode to MANUAL, which skips dirty checking. Notes Postgres rejects writes.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Points out that it does not stop writes to non-transactional resources and that behaviour depends on driver and database; MySQL and others may only optimise. Uses it on query services to save CPU and enable routing to read replicas, while watching replica lag and read-your-writes anomalies. Verifies with a test attempting a write.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines a convention (class-level readOnly on query services, explicit write transactions elsewhere) and uses it as the hook for read/write datasource routing. Documents the consistency implications of replica reads and monitors replica lag. Avoids claiming it as a security control.

11 · AOP and proxies: how @Transactional, @Async and @Cacheable really work

Declarative features are advice around a proxy created by a BeanPostProcessor.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Declarative features are advice around a proxy created by a BeanPostProcessor.
  • Must-know: ★ JDK vs CGLIB proxies · ★ proxy-based limitations · ★ pointcut and advice
  • Top pitfall: final/private methods and this-calls are not advised.
  • Version markers: Spring 2.0 AspectJ support; Boot defaults to CGLIB (2.0)
AspectJDK dynamic proxyCGLIB proxy
Mechanismjava.lang.reflect.Proxy, implements the target’s interfacesGenerates a subclass of the target class (objenesis, no constructor call since 4.0)
RequirementTarget implements an interface; inject the interface typeClass not final, methods not final/private
DefaultSpring Framework (if interface exists)Spring Boot default: spring.aop.proxy-target-class=true since Boot 2.0
PitfallClassCastException injecting concrete classFinal classes (Kotlin classes are final: use the allopen plugin)
🔑 Key ideaSame machinery behind @Transactional (EnableTransactionManagement), @Async (@EnableAsync), @Cacheable (@EnableCaching), @Validated, @PreAuthorize (@EnableMethodSecurity), @Retryable and Resilience4j annotations. All share limitations: proxy only; self-invocation, private/final, wrong thread and early-init beans bypass it.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe custom annotation processor plus IntelliJ plugin for Splunk audit parameters solves at compile time what an @Around aspect solves at runtime. Be ready to compare: compile-time = no proxy limits, no runtime overhead, but build tooling; AOP = flexible but self-invocation/final-method caveats.

Worksheet

Explain JDK dynamic proxy vs CGLIB. Which does Spring Boot use by default and why does it matter?
Answer

1. Model Answer

JDK proxy implements the bean’s interfaces; you can only inject by interface. CGLIB subclasses the class, so it works without interfaces but not on final classes/methods. Boot sets spring.aop.proxy-target-class=true (since 2.0) so class-based proxies are used even when interfaces exist, which prevents ClassCastException when someone injects the concrete type. Since Spring 4 CGLIB is repackaged in spring-core and instantiates via Objenesis (constructor not called twice). Consequences: keep beans non-final, avoid heavy work in constructors (the subclass may be instantiated without it), and fields accessed directly on a proxy see the proxy’s empty fields: always use getters.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands proxy types and their practical consequences for injection, final classes and startup behaviour.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'JDK proxy uses interfaces, CGLIB uses classes' with no view on which Boot defaults to or why it matters.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains JDK proxies implement interfaces so injection must be by interface, CGLIB subclasses so final classes and methods cannot be proxied. Knows Boot defaults proxy-target-class=true since 2.0.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the default prevents ClassCastException when someone injects the concrete class, and that final methods are silently not intercepted under CGLIB, while Kotlin classes need the allopen plugin. Verifies with AopUtils.isCglibProxy or by printing getClass(). Rule: keep beans non-final, inject interfaces or concretes consistently.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes the Kotlin allopen/spring plugin and non-final rules part of project templates, and considers AOT and native-image implications where proxies are generated at build time. Adds a lint check for final annotated methods. Treats the class-vs-interface question as a team convention to avoid debates and mixed behaviour.
@Async, @Cacheable and @Transactional “don’t work”. Give the checklist you go through.
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. Is the feature enabled? @EnableAsync / @EnableCaching (Boot auto-configures caching only when cache libs/@EnableCaching exist; transactions are auto-enabled).
  2. Is the call crossing a bean boundary (not this.x())?
  3. Is the method public and non-final, class non-final?
  4. Is the bean Spring-managed (not new)?
  5. Annotation on the interface vs class with JDK proxies, and the right import.
  6. Bean created too early (a BeanPostProcessor dependency or @PostConstruct), logged as “not eligible for auto-proxying”.
  7. Return type: @Async must return void/Future/CompletableFuture; the exception of a void method goes to AsyncUncaughtExceptionHandler.
  8. Proxy order conflicts (@Order on aspects).

Verify with AopUtils.isAopProxy(bean) and the bean class in /actuator/beans ($$SpringCGLIB$$ suffix).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Systematic debugging of annotation-driven features that silently do nothing.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'maybe you forgot the annotation' and tries restarting or adding annotations randomly.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists enablement (@EnableAsync, @EnableCaching), crossing a bean boundary instead of this.x(), public non-final methods, Spring-managed beans, and annotation on interface vs class.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Orders the checklist by likelihood and verifies each step: log the bean class to confirm a proxy, check the thread name for @Async, enable cache and tx debug logging, and look for a custom executor or advisor order conflict. Notes @Async needs a proper executor and returns void or Future/CompletableFuture. Builds a minimal failing test.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Turns the checklist into a shared runbook and a test utility that asserts a bean is proxied and the feature is active. Adds a startup check for missing enablement and an ArchUnit rule against self-invocation patterns. Reduces the class of problem by keeping cross-cutting concerns in a few clearly named platform annotations.
Write an @Around aspect for timing/audit and explain pointcut designators. When prefer AspectJ weaving?
Answer

1. Model Answer

See the example: @Around advice receives ProceedingJoinPoint; you must call proceed() and rethrow. Designators: execution(...) (method signature), within(...) (type), @annotation(...), @within, args, bean(name) (Spring only). Spring AOP only supports method execution on Spring beans through proxies. Use AspectJ (compile-time or load-time weaving) for advising constructors, field access, non-Spring objects, private methods or self-invocation; cost is build/agent complexity and GraalVM-native friction. Order multiple aspects with @Order (lower = outermost); a retry aspect must be outside the transaction aspect, otherwise it retries inside the already-failed tx.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Practical AOP skills and knowing the limits of Spring's proxy-based AOP versus AspectJ weaving.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Writes @Around but forgets to call proceed() or swallows exceptions; cannot explain execution vs within.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Writes @Around with ProceedingJoinPoint, calls proceed() and rethrows. Lists execution, within, @annotation, @within, args, bean. Knows Spring AOP is method-execution on beans only.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Uses try/finally for timing, preserves the return value and exception type, avoids logging sensitive arguments, and uses Micrometer instead of ad hoc logging. Knows aspect ordering with @Order and that proxies cannot advise self-calls or private methods. Chooses AspectJ only for non-bean objects, field access, constructors or self-invocation.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides a custom annotation (for example for timing/audit) in the platform library instead of ad hoc pointcuts, with tests and metrics naming conventions. Keeps pointcuts narrow to limit hidden behaviour and startup/performance cost. Prefers explicit code or Micrometer @Observed where it is clearer, and documents when AspectJ weaving's build complexity is justified.
Why must a retry wrapper sit outside @Transactional, and how do you guarantee ordering of Spring Retry / Resilience4j / Tx advice?
Answer

1. Model Answer

If the transaction advice is outermost and retry is inside, each retry happens within the same rolled-back-only transaction; a second attempt can never succeed after a DB error (tx marked rollback-only or connection broken). Retry must wrap a new transaction per attempt. Ordering: set the advice order explicitly (@EnableTransactionManagement(order=...); Resilience4j has resilience4j.*.*AspectOrder properties; for Spring Retry check the order attribute of your version) because lower value = outer. Simplest robust design: put the retry on a facade method (non-transactional) that calls a separate @Transactional bean, or use RetryTemplate around TransactionTemplate explicitly so the nesting is visible in code.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understanding of advice ordering and why retry must create a fresh transaction per attempt.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'just add @Retryable' on the transactional method and does not see a problem.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains that if the transaction is outermost, retries run inside a transaction that is already rollback-only or has a broken connection. Retry must wrap the transaction. Sets order explicitly.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Specifies the ordering (retry or Resilience4j outermost with higher precedence than transaction advice) and verifies it with a test that fails the first attempt and shows a new transaction on the second. Limits retries to transient errors, adds backoff and jitter, and ensures the operation is idempotent. Notes ordering between retry, circuit breaker and bulkhead.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Centralises resilience composition in one starter with documented, tested advice order so teams cannot misplace it. Sets rules on which exceptions are retryable and monitors retry rate and exhaustion to catch retry amplification against shared dependencies. Challenges retrying DB deadlocks blindly without idempotency.

12 · Spring Security fundamentals: filter chain, DSL, method security, CSRF, CORS

Everything is a servlet filter chain; configuration is a SecurityFilterChain bean.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Security is a servlet filter chain configured by a SecurityFilterChain bean.
  • Must-know: ★ filter chain order · ★ authorizeHttpRequests DSL · ★ CSRF/CORS for APIs
  • Top pitfall: WebSecurityConfigurerAdapter is removed; CORS must run before auth.
  • Version markers: Spring Security 5.7 SecurityFilterChain; 6.0 lambda DSL
FeatureDetailSince

🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe CSAC project used Angular + Spring Boot with an enterprise identity-management product and role-based access control; in modern Security terms that is an oauth2ResourceServer chain with roles mapped from the token to authorities and enforced by @PreAuthorize on services. Field-level AES-256/RSA encryption of SSN/PII is a separate, defence-in-depth layer: authorisation decides who may see data, encryption protects the data at rest. (The retail platform did similar with KMS at the gateway so persistence never stores plaintext.)

Worksheet

How do you configure Spring Security in Spring Boot 3? What happened to WebSecurityConfigurerAdapter?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Declare one or more SecurityFilterChain beans taking HttpSecurity and returning http.build(); supply AuthenticationManager/UserDetailsService/PasswordEncoder as beans. WebSecurityConfigurerAdapter was deprecated in 5.7 and removed in 6.0 (Boot 3.0). Use the lambda DSL (authorizeHttpRequests(a -> ...)), requestMatchers instead of antMatchers/mvcMatchers, and @EnableMethodSecurity instead of @EnableGlobalMethodSecurity. Ignore static resources with permitAll in the chain rather than WebSecurityCustomizer.ignoring() (ignored requests skip all headers/protections).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Currency with Spring Security 6 configuration and understanding of why the old adapter pattern was removed.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Describes extends WebSecurityConfigurerAdapter, which is removed, or copy-pastes an old snippet with antMatchers.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Declares SecurityFilterChain beans using HttpSecurity and http.build(), with lambda DSL, authorizeHttpRequests and requestMatchers. Knows the adapter was deprecated in 5.7 and removed in 6.0.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Defines multiple ordered chains with securityMatcher, exposes UserDetailsService, PasswordEncoder as beans, and uses @WebMvcTest with security tests (with(jwt()), @WithMockUser). Watches for the default-deny rule ordering, and for the 6.x change that authorization filter applies to all dispatcher types.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Wraps common security defaults in a shared module so each service only declares routes and scopes, and tracks Spring Security major upgrades with an automated migration recipe (OpenRewrite). Adds tests that assert the secure-by-default posture, such as anyRequest denied. Keeps config readable so reviewers can audit it.
What is CSRF, when can you disable it, and how does CORS differ?
Answer

1. Model Answer

CSRF: a malicious site makes the victim’s browser send a state-changing request that automatically carries the victim’s cookie credentials. Protect with a synchroniser token/double-submit (Security default for session apps) and SameSite cookies. If authentication is a bearer token in the Authorization header (JWT), browsers do not attach it automatically, so CSRF protection is unnecessary and can be disabled for that stateless chain. CORS is a browser policy relaxing the same-origin rule for cross-origin JS calls: the server declares allowed origins/methods/headers (preflight OPTIONS). It is not an authentication mechanism and does not protect against non-browser clients. Configure CORS in Security (.cors()) so preflights are answered before authentication; configuring only in MVC @CrossOrigin gets blocked by Security with 401 on preflight.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Precise understanding of what CSRF and CORS each protect and when disabling CSRF is legitimate.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'disable CSRF for REST APIs' without explaining why, and confuses CORS with a security feature that blocks attackers.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Defines CSRF as the browser auto-attaching cookie credentials to a forged request, defended by tokens and SameSite. Says CSRF can be disabled for stateless bearer-token APIs, and CORS is a browser relaxation of the same-origin policy.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the condition: disable only when no ambient credentials (cookies, basic auth) are used. Warns that session cookies with a JWT mix need CSRF kept on, that CORS is not an access control (curl ignores it), and that wildcard origin with credentials is invalid. Tests preflight and cross-origin behaviour from a real browser.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines an organisational rule: cookie-authenticated endpoints keep CSRF on; token APIs may disable it with a code comment and review sign-off. Centralises CORS policy in the gateway or a shared config with explicit origins per environment. Includes these cases in security review checklists and automated scans.
Explain authentication vs authorization in the filter chain, and hasRole vs hasAuthority.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Authentication filters build an Authentication (principal + GrantedAuthority list) via AuthenticationManager → AuthenticationProviders and store it in SecurityContextHolder (thread-local; propagated by DelegatingSecurityContextExecutor to async tasks). Authorisation (AuthorizationFilter + method security) decides on that object. hasRole("ADMIN") checks authority ROLE_ADMIN (prefix added automatically); hasAuthority("ROLE_ADMIN") is the literal. JWT scopes map to SCOPE_xxx. Failures: unauthenticated → AuthenticationEntryPoint (401), authenticated but denied → AccessDeniedHandler (403).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understanding of the authentication flow into SecurityContext and the hasRole/hasAuthority prefix subtlety.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'authentication is who you are, authorization is what you can do' and thinks hasRole('ADMIN') and hasAuthority('ADMIN') are identical.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes filters building Authentication through AuthenticationManager and providers, stored in the thread-local SecurityContextHolder; authorization is evaluated afterward. Knows hasRole adds the ROLE_ prefix.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Flags that thread-local context does not propagate to @Async or new threads unless DelegatingSecurityContextExecutor is used, and that mismatched prefixes with JWT scope claims (SCOPE_) cause 403s. Debugs with security debug logging and checking the authority list actually mapped. Differentiates 401 and 403 correctly.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines a consistent authority naming scheme (roles vs scopes vs permissions) across services and documents the claim-to-authority mapping in the shared library. Adds tests that assert authorities per token type. Discourages roles as a proxy for fine-grained permissions as the system grows.
Compare URL-based authorisation and method security. How do you do tenant-/owner-based (row-level) authorisation?
Answer

1. Model Answer

URL rules are coarse (role/scope per route); method security (@PreAuthorize) protects the service regardless of which controller/Kafka listener/scheduler calls it, and supports SpEL on arguments and return values. Do both: URL for broad perimeter, method for business rules. Owner/tenant checks: @PreAuthorize("@perm.canRead(authentication, #id)") delegating to a bean, @PostAuthorize("returnObject.owner == authentication.name") (loads first, then denies: avoid for expensive/side-effecting reads), or @PostFilter (inefficient on large lists: filter in the query instead via :#{authentication.name} SpEL in @Query with SecurityEvaluationContextExtension/@AuthenticationPrincipal parameter). Method security is proxy-based: same self-invocation limits. Unit-test with @WithMockUser/jwt() post-processor.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Layered authorisation design including object-level checks, not just role checks.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'use @PreAuthorize with roles' and treats URL rules as sufficient; has no approach for owner or tenant checks.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains URL rules are coarse and method security protects the service regardless of caller, and uses @PreAuthorize("@perm.canRead(authentication, #id)") for owner checks.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes that @PostAuthorize loads data before denying and that list endpoints need the filter pushed into the query (tenant predicate) because per-row checks do not scale. Considers Hibernate filters or tenant-aware repositories, tests with cross-tenant negative cases, and avoids leaking existence with 403 vs 404.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes tenant scoping structural (a mandatory tenant predicate in repositories, plus RLS in the database as defence in depth) so developers cannot forget it. Adds negative cross-tenant integration tests to the template and an ArchUnit check on repository access. Reviews the policy model for centralisation, such as a policy engine, if rules grow.
Design the security for a service with a browser SPA, mobile app, and service-to-service callers. What chains, token types and CSRF/CORS decisions?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Separate SecurityFilterChains by securityMatcher/@Order: (1) /api/** for SPA/mobile: stateless, OAuth2 resource server accepting JWTs from the IdP (Authorization Code + PKCE for SPA/mobile; no tokens in localStorage if avoidable, prefer BFF pattern with an HttpOnly SameSite session cookie and the token held server-side, then CSRF protection on); (2) /internal/** for services: client-credentials JWT or mTLS (x509) with narrow scopes; (3) /actuator/** on a separate management port, open for probes only, the rest authenticated/network-restricted. CORS only for the SPA origin; short-lived access tokens + refresh rotation; validate iss, aud, exp; map roles/scopes to authorities; audit logging of authz denials; rate limiting at the gateway. Failure modes to discuss: IdP/JWKS outage (cache the keys, bounded), clock skew (JwtTimestampValidator default 60 s), token revocation (short TTL, introspection for opaque tokens).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Holistic security design that separates client types and picks tokens, CSRF and CORS deliberately.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Proposes 'JWT for everything' and stores the token in localStorage, with CSRF disabled everywhere.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Defines separate SecurityFilterChains by matcher and order: stateless resource-server chain for APIs, Authorization Code with PKCE for SPA and mobile, client credentials for service-to-service, and mentions a BFF with an HttpOnly cookie.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains why BFF keeps tokens out of the browser and then requires CSRF and SameSite for the cookie chain, while bearer chains disable CSRF. Specifies audience and scope per caller, short-lived access tokens, refresh rotation, and CORS only for the origins that need it. Tests each chain independently with the wrong token type.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats this as a platform reference architecture: a shared auth starter, a gateway enforcing coarse policy, standard token lifetimes, and threat-model documentation. Plans rollout and key rotation, auditing, and incident revocation (short TTL, introspection for sensitive APIs). Reviews the design with security and publishes decision records.

13 · OAuth2 / OIDC, JWT resource server, mTLS (x509) and an external IdP

Validate tokens, map claims, secure service-to-service calls.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Validate JWTs as a resource server, map claims, secure service-to-service calls.
  • Must-know: ★ resource server JWT validation · ★ scope/authority mapping · ★ mTLS/x509
  • Top pitfall: trusting an unvalidated issuer or audience.
  • Version markers: Security 5.1 resource server; Boot 3.1 SSL bundles
ConceptDetailSince
OAuth2 Resource Server (JWT) ★ MUST KNOWspring-boot-starter-oauth2-resource-server; validates signature (JWKS), exp, issSecurity 5.1 / Boot 2.1
issuer-uri vs jwk-set-uriissuer-uri: OIDC discovery of the JWKS (done lazily on first token with Boot’s auto-configured decoder) and issuer validation; jwk-set-uri: keys only, no discoveryBoot 2.1
JwtAuthenticationConverter ★ MUST KNOWMap claims (scope, roles, groups) to authoritiesSecurity 5.2
OAuth2 Client / oauth2LoginAuthorization Code flow for server-side web appsSecurity 5.0
Opaque token introspectionoauth2ResourceServer().opaqueToken()Security 5.2
x509 client-cert auth ★ MUST KNOWhttp.x509(...) with server.ssl.client-auth=needSecurity 3.x / Boot 1.0
SSL bundlesspring.ssl.bundle.jks.*/pem with hot reloadBoot 3.1
DPoP / token exchange / OAuth 2.1Newer sender-constrained tokens (verify availability)Security 6.5+ (verify)
🔑 Key ideaA resource server never sees the user’s password. It receives a bearer JWT, checks signature (JWKS key by kid), iss, exp/nbf, and (you add) aud, then converts claims to authorities. The IdP (for example a corporate identity server or API-manager key manager) issues tokens; the service only needs its issuer/JWKS URL.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe Attestation Authority (x509 / HSM) and the mTLS/JWT items on the resume map directly: mTLS authenticates the calling service (client cert chain, CN/SAN to principal, short-lived certs, revocation), JWT carries the user or app identity and scopes. Typical L5 answer: use both: mTLS for transport-level service identity, JWT for end-user context, and authorise on JWT scopes.

Worksheet

How does Spring Boot validate an incoming JWT as a resource server? What properties do you need?
Answer

1. Model Answer

With spring-boot-starter-oauth2-resource-server and spring.security.oauth2.resourceserver.jwt.issuer-uri (or jwk-set-uri), Boot builds a NimbusJwtDecoder: on first use it fetches the JWKS and caches keys; BearerTokenAuthenticationFilter extracts the token from the Authorization: Bearer header; decoder verifies the signature using the key matching kid, checks exp/nbf (60 s skew) and the issuer; JwtAuthenticationConverter turns claims into authorities (scope/scp → SCOPE_x). Invalid token → 401 with WWW-Authenticate: Bearer error=.... You must add an audience validator yourself, otherwise any token from the same issuer for another API is accepted.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows what Boot actually does at JWT validation time and the minimal configuration plus its failure modes.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add the security starter and a secret key' and does not know about JWKS or issuer validation.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Names spring-boot-starter-oauth2-resource-server and issuer-uri or jwk-set-uri. Describes NimbusJwtDecoder fetching and caching JWKS, BearerTokenAuthenticationFilter, signature verification by kid, and exp/iss validation.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds audience validation (not default), clock skew, mapping scopes or custom claims to authorities via a JwtAuthenticationConverter, and startup behaviour with issuer-uri (discovery call at first use/startup). Considers JWKS endpoint availability and cache refresh. Tests with the jwt() MockMvc post-processor and an invalid token.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides a shared validator configuration (issuer, audience, algorithms, skew) so all services validate identically, and monitors 401 rates per reason. Plans for key rotation and IdP outages through caching policy and runbooks. Reviews whether opaque introspection is better for revocation-sensitive APIs.
Access token vs ID token vs refresh token; JWT vs opaque token. Which do you send to an API?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Access token: authorises API calls (sent to the resource server). ID token (OIDC): proves who logged in to the client; never send it to an API. Refresh token: obtains new access tokens; stays on the client/BFF, rotated, never sent to APIs. JWT access tokens are self-contained (stateless validation, can’t be revoked before exp, keep short-lived, carry minimal claims); opaque tokens require introspection (/introspect, a network hop, but instantly revocable). Choose JWT for scale and low latency, opaque when revocation or hiding claims matters; cache introspection results briefly.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Clear understanding of token types and their audiences, and the JWT versus opaque trade-off.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Treats all tokens as interchangeable 'JWTs' and sends the ID token to the API.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: States access tokens go to APIs, ID tokens prove login to the client only, refresh tokens obtain new access tokens and stay with the client/BFF. Notes JWTs are self-contained and cannot be revoked before expiry.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Chooses based on revocation and exposure needs: JWT with short TTL for scale, opaque with introspection (cached) when immediate revocation or hiding claims matters. Mentions audience and scope validation, refresh token rotation, and not putting sensitive data in JWT payloads. Verifies by decoding sample tokens.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines org-wide token policy (lifetimes, audience conventions, what claims may appear) and a revocation strategy for incidents. Weighs the latency and availability cost of introspection against risk by API tier. Documents the model for engineers so ID tokens are never used as API credentials.
You integrate with an enterprise identity server as IdP. What do you configure and what usually goes wrong?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Configure: issuer-uri or jwk-set-uri, the audience (client id) check, claim mapping for roles/groups (some IdPs emit roles in claims configured via the service provider’s claim mapping, often roles or groups, sometimes as comma-separated string instead of array, so write a custom converter), token signing alg (RS256) and TLS trust for the IdP host (corporate CA in the truststore). Typical failures: iss mismatch (the IdP issuer differs from the discovery URL, or trailing slash/port/tenant domain), startup failures when the IdP is unreachable if you build the decoder yourself with JwtDecoders.fromIssuerLocation (eager discovery; Boot’s auto-configured decoder is lazy; or use jwk-set-uri + explicit issuer validator), self-signed certs (PKIX path building failed), clock skew, and API Manager gateways issuing a different JWT (backend JWT with X-JWT-Assertion header) than the user’s OAuth token. (Details vary by IdP product and version: verify against your deployment.)

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Real-world IdP integration experience, including claim mapping and trust issues that appear outside the happy path.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'set the issuer URL and it works' with no knowledge of IdP-specific claim shapes or TLS trust.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Configures issuer or JWKS URI, audience check, claim mapping for roles or groups, RS256, and TLS trust for the IdP host. Notes IdP roles may be comma-separated strings requiring a custom converter.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Lists typical failures: internal CA missing from the truststore, issuer string mismatch (trailing slash, hostname behind proxy), clock skew, roles claim not enabled in the service provider, and key rotation. Debugs by decoding the token and calling the JWKS and discovery endpoints from the pod. Adds a test using a captured token shape.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Abstracts IdP differences behind a claims-mapping adapter in the shared library so switching IdPs is configuration, and documents the onboarding checklist. Adds health checks and alerts for JWKS reachability and token validation failure rates. Plans for an IdP migration without service rewrites.
How would you implement service-to-service auth with mTLS in Spring Boot, and what are the operational costs?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Server: server.ssl.bundle with the server keystore, server.ssl.client-auth=need and a truststore containing the CA that issues client certs; Security http.x509() extracts the principal from the subject CN/SAN and a UserDetailsService maps it to authorities. Client: RestClient/WebClient built with an SSL bundle (SslBundles Boot 3.1+; RestClientSsl/WebClientSsl helpers in 3.2, verify) presenting its client cert. Costs: certificate issuance/rotation (short-lived certs, SSL bundle reload in 3.1+ avoids restarts), CRL/OCSP revocation checking, cert expiry alerts, TLS terminated at ingress breaks end-to-end mTLS unless the proxy forwards the verified cert (X-Forwarded-Client-Cert) which then must be trusted only from the proxy, harder debugging (handshake failures show no HTTP response). A service mesh (mTLS by sidecar) can offload it.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows how to implement mTLS in Boot and honestly accounts for operational costs.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'enable SSL on both sides' and does not know about client-auth, truststores, or certificate rotation.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Configures server.ssl.bundle with client-auth=need, a truststore with the issuing CA, x509 principal extraction and a client built with an SSL bundle (Boot 3.1+).
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Covers certificate rotation without restart (SSL bundle reload), expiry monitoring, CN vs SAN mapping, handling ingress/mesh termination, and debugging handshake failures with -Djavax.net.debug=ssl. Weighs against a service mesh providing mTLS transparently. Tests with an invalid client cert expecting handshake failure.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Prefers platform-managed identity (mesh or SPIFFE-style short-lived certs) over per-app keystores, and justifies when app-level mTLS is still needed. Owns cert lifecycle: automation, expiry alerts, and incident runbooks. Budgets the operational cost (PKI, onboarding, debugging complexity) against the threat model.
JWT validation passes locally but you get 401 in the cluster only after a few hours. What do you investigate?
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. Key rotation: IdP rotated signing keys; cached JWKS lacks the new kid. Nimbus refreshes on unknown kid (rate-limited), check the JWKS cache/refresh behaviour and that egress to the JWKS URL is allowed.
  2. Clock skew between pods/nodes (NTP) causing nbf/exp failures.
  3. Token TTL shorter than the caller’s cache: caller reuses an expired token.
  4. Proxy/TLS: truststore missing a renewed intermediate CA.
  5. Different iss via an internal vs external hostname.

Turn on logging.level.org.springframework.security=DEBUG (the reason is logged: “Jwt expired at…”, “signed JWT rejected: Another algorithm expected / no matching key(s)”), decode the failing token’s header/claims, and compare with the IdP’s JWKS.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Production debugging instinct for time-dependent authentication failures.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'the token must be invalid' and regenerates it, or restarts the pod, without a hypothesis.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Suggests key rotation with a stale JWKS cache, clock skew between nodes, token TTL shorter than the caller's cache, and proxy or TLS problems reaching the JWKS endpoint.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Correlates the failure time with the IdP rotation schedule and token expiry, checks the exact validation error (kid unknown, exp, iss, aud), and tests JWKS fetch from the pod including egress and proxy. Checks NTP drift, and the caller's token caching and refresh logic. Reproduces by rotating keys in a test IdP.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Turns the incident into a systemic fix: standard clock sync, JWKS cache settings, caller-side refresh-on-401 with backoff, and dashboards with 401 reasons by service. Writes a runbook and adds a rotation game-day to staging. Pushes for observable auth failure categories rather than a generic 401.

14 · Testing: @SpringBootTest, slices, MockMvc, Testcontainers, MockServer, TDD

Fast slice tests for logic, a few hermetic integration tests for wiring.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Fast slice tests for logic, a few hermetic integration tests for wiring.
  • Must-know: ★ @WebMvcTest/@DataJpaTest slices · ★ Testcontainers and MockServer · ★ @MockitoBean vs @MockBean
  • Top pitfall: @SpringBootTest everywhere makes the suite slow and flaky.
  • Version markers: Boot 1.4 slices; Boot 3.1 @ServiceConnection; Framework 6.2 @MockitoBean

@SpringBootTest ★ MUST KNOWWhole context (random port optional)End-to-end wiring, few of themBoot 1.4

⚠ PitfallContext caching: Spring caches the application context across tests keyed by configuration (profiles, @MockBean set, properties). Every unique combination = new context start (seconds). Many different @MockBean/@DirtiesContext sets are the #1 reason test suites become slow. Standardise on a few base configs.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe retail platform’s hermetic component tests used containerised Postgres + MockServer + Kafka sidecars: the Boot 3.1 equivalent is Testcontainers with @ServiceConnection for Postgres/Kafka and a MockServer container for downstream HTTP, so the same suite runs on a laptop and in Jenkins/GitHub Actions without shared environments. Combined with the performance-baseline-and-regression process, tests can pin latency budgets for the fork-and-join aggregation.

Worksheet

@SpringBootTest vs @WebMvcTest vs @DataJpaTest: when do you use each?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@WebMvcTest: only the web layer (controllers, @ControllerAdvice, filters, converters, Security config) with MockMvc; collaborators mocked. Milliseconds-to-seconds. @DataJpaTest: JPA slice with an in-memory or container DB, transactional per test, schema from Flyway/Hibernate; test queries, mappings and constraints, preferably on the real DB engine (Testcontainers) because H2 differs (dialect, JSON, locking, sequences). @SpringBootTest: full context for wiring/integration and a handful of end-to-end paths (webEnvironment = RANDOM_PORT with TestRestTemplate/WebTestClient). Pyramid: many plain unit tests, some slices, few @SpringBootTest.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Choosing the cheapest test scope that still proves the behaviour, with awareness of what slices exclude.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Uses @SpringBootTest for everything because 'it tests the whole app'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains @WebMvcTest loads only the web layer with MockMvc and mocked collaborators, @DataJpaTest loads the JPA slice with a transactional test, and @SpringBootTest loads the full context.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes @DataJpaTest should run against the real DB engine via Testcontainers rather than H2 to catch dialect and constraint differences, and that slices exclude your security or custom beans unless imported. Uses a test pyramid: many unit tests, some slices, few full-context tests.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines a testing strategy and base classes per layer, with a budget for full-context tests and CI timing tracked as a metric. Standardises Testcontainers use and slice conventions in a template. Reviews tests in code review for scope creep and slow suites before they grow.
What does @ServiceConnection do, and what did we do before it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Before: @Container static PostgreSQLContainer pg + @DynamicPropertySource to push spring.datasource.url/username/password from the container into the Environment. Boot 3.1 adds ConnectionDetails beans: annotating a Testcontainers field (or a @Bean in a @TestConfiguration) with @ServiceConnection creates the right JdbcConnectionDetails/KafkaConnectionDetails/... bean from the container type, and the auto-configuration prefers it over properties. It also powers spring-boot-docker-compose (3.1) and dev-time SpringApplication.from(App::main).with(TestcontainersConfig.class).run(args). Add @RestartScope + spring-boot-devtools to keep containers across restarts. Reuse containers (withReuse(true)) locally for speed.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows modern Testcontainers wiring in Boot and the problem it replaces.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it connects the container to Spring' without being able to describe the previous manual wiring.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains the old @DynamicPropertySource approach and that Boot 3.1 @ServiceConnection creates ConnectionDetails beans from the container automatically.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes it removes brittle property names, works with @TestConfiguration beans, supports many container types, and enables SpringApplication.from(...).with(...) for local dev with Testcontainers. Warns about container reuse and lifecycle with the context cache. Still uses @DynamicPropertySource for unsupported cases.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes the pattern the standard in the test template, shares a single container configuration between tests and local development, and measures its effect on suite time. Plans the upgrade path off legacy wiring across repos with an automated recipe. Documents how to add new container types.
Your suite takes 25 minutes because of context startups. How do you speed it up?
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. Count distinct contexts (logging.level.org.springframework.test.context.cache=DEBUG prints hits/misses). Remove stray @DirtiesContext, unify @MockBean/@MockitoBean sets into a shared abstract base or @TestConfiguration, stop varying properties per class.
  2. Convert most @SpringBootTest into slices or plain unit tests.
  3. Share one static container per JVM (singleton container pattern or withReuse) rather than per class.
  4. Parallelise: junit.jupiter.execution.parallel.enabled only for tests that are context- and DB-safe (separate schemas/data), or shard across CI agents.
  5. Lazy init in tests (spring.main.lazy-initialization=true), exclude unneeded auto-configs, disable open ports.
  6. Use the Gradle/Maven test cache and keep Docker images pre-pulled in CI.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Systematic test-suite performance diagnosis centred on Spring's context cache.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'run tests in parallel' or 'buy a bigger CI machine' without measuring.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Counts distinct contexts using cache logging, removes @DirtiesContext, unifies @MockBean sets, converts @SpringBootTest to slices or unit tests, and shares one container.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Identifies what makes cache keys differ (properties, mock beans, profiles, @Import) and fixes them with a shared base class. Measures before and after with context-start counts and per-class times, and parallelises only after fixing shared state. Targets the largest startup costs first.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a CI duration SLO and a dashboard of slowest tests, adds a check on context count per module, and puts test-structure guidance in the template. Considers module-level splitting and test selection to shorten feedback. Treats slow feedback as a team productivity cost and justifies the investment.
How do you apply TDD to a Spring Boot feature? Show the red-green-refactor loop for a new endpoint.
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. Red: write a @WebMvcTest asserting POST /orders returns 201 + Location and 400 for invalid payload; it fails (404).
  2. Green: smallest controller + service stub passing it; then a unit test for the domain rule (pure Java, no Spring), then implementation.
  3. Add a repository test (@DataJpaTest on Testcontainers) driven by the query you need.
  4. Refactor with the tests as a safety net; finally one @SpringBootTest happy path.

Tips: test behaviour not implementation (avoid verifying every mock call), keep domain logic in plain objects so most TDD cycles run in milliseconds, use ApplicationContextRunner for config logic, and add contract tests (OpenAPI/Spring Cloud Contract/Pact) for consumers.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Practical TDD skill applied at the right test levels rather than ritual.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'write tests first' but writes a full controller first and the tests afterward, or only uses @SpringBootTest.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes red with a failing @WebMvcTest for 201 and 400, green with minimal code, a pure-Java domain unit test, a repository test, then refactor.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Drives outside-in, keeps unit tests free of Spring, and uses the slice test for contract (status, headers, validation, error body). Refactors under green tests and adds one end-to-end test with Testcontainers. Recognises when TDD is less valuable, such as exploratory spikes.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Promotes the practice through pairing, templates and review norms, not mandates, and measures effects via defect escape rate and change failure rate. Adds contract tests for API consumers. Keeps the test suite fast so the loop stays practical.
Flaky integration tests: they pass locally and fail in CI with Kafka/Postgres containers. What are the usual causes and fixes?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Causes: (1) async assertions with fixed Thread.sleep: use Awaitility (await().atMost(10, SECONDS).untilAsserted(...)); (2) shared state between tests (rows, topics, consumer group offsets): unique topic/group per test or truncate in @BeforeEach; (3) container readiness: wait strategies, CI cold image pulls, port mapping and Docker-in-Docker networking (TESTCONTAINERS_HOST_OVERRIDE); (4) timing assumptions (time zones, Instant.now()): inject Clock; (5) test order dependency or parallelism sharing a context; (6) CI CPU starvation making short timeouts fail. Fix at the root: deterministic waiting, isolated data, injected clock, and quarantine + ticket for genuinely flaky tests rather than retries that hide real race conditions.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Diagnosing nondeterminism in integration tests with a methodical view of timing, shared state and environment.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'rerun the pipeline' or 'add Thread.sleep(5000)' and calls it flaky.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists fixed sleeps replaced by Awaitility, shared state between tests, container readiness and cold CI image pulls, and port or network differences.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Isolates state with unique topics, groups and truncation, uses wait strategies and health checks, fixes ordering assumptions, and captures logs and container output on failure. Reproduces by running the test in a loop under CPU constraint. Quarantines with a tracked ticket rather than ignoring.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats flakiness as a metric with an owner: tracks flake rate per test, enforces a quarantine policy, and provides shared test utilities (Awaitility defaults, container base). Aligns CI resources with local conditions and caches images. Prevents erosion of trust in CI.

15 · Messaging and events: Spring for Apache Kafka, ApplicationEvents, @Async

Reliable consumption, error handling, transactions and in-process events.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Reliable Kafka consumption, error handling and in-process events.
  • Must-know: ★ at-least-once and idempotency · ★ DefaultErrorHandler + DLT · ★ @TransactionalEventListener
  • Top pitfall: auto-commit with slow processing loses or duplicates messages.
  • Version markers: Spring Kafka 2.x; 2.8 DefaultErrorHandler
FeatureDetailSince
@KafkaListener + KafkaTemplate ★ MUST KNOWAnnotation-driven consumers via ConcurrentMessageListenerContainerSpring Kafka 1.x
DefaultErrorHandler ★ MUST KNOWRetries with BackOff, then recoverer (DLT). Replaces SeekToCurrentErrorHandlerSpring Kafka 2.8
