🔑 Key ideaKafka delivery is at-least-once by default with Spring: process → commit offset. Therefore consumers must be idempotent (dedupe by event id / natural key / upsert). Exactly-once is only for Kafka-to-Kafka (transactions + read_committed), never for “Kafka + DB” without an idempotent write.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe HR systems moved from Oracle + MongoDB to Kafka event-driven flows, and a leave-management system (4,000+ leave types) used Kafka eventing: the questions to be ready for are consumer idempotency, ordering (key by employee id so one employee’s events stay on one partition), DLT handling for poison messages and replay, and publishing events from committed state (outbox).

Worksheet

How does @KafkaListener work and what are the key settings (group id, concurrency, ack mode)?
Answer

1. Model Answer

A KafkaListenerAnnotationBeanPostProcessor registers a ConcurrentMessageListenerContainer per listener; it spawns concurrency consumer threads, each assigned partitions by the group coordinator (effective parallelism ≤ partition count; extra threads idle). Messages are handed to your method; AckMode (BATCH default: commit after each poll batch is processed; RECORD, MANUAL, MANUAL_IMMEDIATE) controls offset commits. Boot sets enable.auto.commit=false in the container (the container commits). Tune max.poll.records and max.poll.interval.ms: if processing a batch exceeds the interval the consumer is kicked out of the group and rebalances (duplicate processing). Ordering is per partition only.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understanding of the Kafka listener container model and what each setting means for parallelism and delivery semantics.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says '@KafkaListener consumes messages from a topic' and sets concurrency higher to go faster.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains the container per listener, concurrency threads each assigned partitions, parallelism bounded by partition count, group id for load sharing, and AckMode (BATCH default, RECORD, MANUAL).
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Covers at-least-once behaviour and the need for idempotent handlers, enable.auto.commit=false managed by the container, max.poll.records versus max.poll.interval.ms, and deserialiser errors. Verifies partition assignment with logs or kafka-consumer-groups and tests ordering per key.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides shared listener configuration with sensible defaults (error handler, DLT, observability) and capacity rules (partitions vs consumers). Defines message contracts and schema evolution policy. Monitors lag and processing time per consumer group as an SLO.
A poison message makes the consumer fail repeatedly. How do you handle errors, retries and dead letters in Spring Kafka?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Use DefaultErrorHandler (2.8+) with a BackOff (e.g. 4 retries exponential) and a DeadLetterPublishingRecoverer; it seeks back and re-polls the failed record (blocking that partition during retries: keep the total backoff short), then publishes to <topic>.DLT with headers (original topic, partition, offset, exception) and commits past it. Classify: non-retryable (deserialisation, validation) go to the DLT immediately via addNotRetryableExceptions; deserialisation failures need ErrorHandlingDeserializer or the container loops forever. For long delays without blocking the partition use @RetryableTopic (retry topics with delays) at the cost of reordering. Operate the DLT: alert on depth, a tool to replay after the fix, retention long enough.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designing error handling for poison messages that preserves throughput and traceability.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'catch the exception and log it' or retry forever, blocking the partition.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Uses DefaultErrorHandler with a BackOff and DeadLetterPublishingRecoverer to publish to a DLT with headers. Notes retries block the partition.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Classifies exceptions into retryable and non-retryable (deserialisation, validation go straight to DLT), keeps total backoff below max.poll.interval.ms, and uses ErrorHandlingDeserializer. Considers non-blocking retry topics for long delays, and DLT replay tooling. Verifies with a test that sends a bad record.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises DLT naming, headers, retention, and a replay process with ownership and alerts on DLT growth. Defines which failures must page and which are triaged. Evaluates retry-topic ordering trade-offs and data-privacy concerns of dead-lettered payloads.
Explain exactly-once in Spring Kafka. Can you make a DB write and a Kafka publish atomic?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Kafka EOS = idempotent producer + transactions: transaction-id-prefix turns on KafkaTransactionManager; a listener container with a transaction manager does consume-process-produce atomically (sends + offset commit in one Kafka tx); downstream consumers need isolation.level=read_committed. It guarantees atomicity only within Kafka. Not atomic with a database: chaining a DataSourceTransactionManager + Kafka tx is best-effort 1PC (the DB can commit and the Kafka commit fail, causing redelivery). Practical answer: make processing idempotent (unique key/upsert, dedupe table) so at-least-once redelivery has no effect, and use the outbox for DB→Kafka. Costs of Kafka tx: latency, transactional.id management, zombie fencing.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Precise understanding of what exactly-once covers and its limits, especially at the DB boundary.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'enable idempotence and we have exactly-once end to end', including writes to the database.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains idempotent producer plus transactions, transaction-id-prefix, consume-process-produce atomically in Kafka, and read_committed downstream. States it is atomic only within Kafka.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Says a DB write and Kafka publish cannot be atomic and uses the outbox or idempotent consumers (dedupe keys) to get effectively-once. Notes chained transaction managers only give best-effort ordering of commits. Mentions transactional producer fencing and the throughput cost. Tests crash points.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets the guideline: assume at-least-once, design idempotency, and use Kafka transactions only for Kafka-to-Kafka pipelines. Documents the guarantee per pipeline for consumers. Challenges requests for exactly-once by asking what duplication would actually cost.
@EventListener vs @TransactionalEventListener vs @Async: what are the semantics and failure modes?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@EventListener: synchronous in the publisher’s thread and transaction; a listener exception propagates to the publisher (and rolls back its tx). @TransactionalEventListener: bound to the tx phase (AFTER_COMMIT default, AFTER_ROLLBACK, AFTER_COMPLETION, BEFORE_COMMIT); if no transaction is active the event is dropped unless fallbackExecution=true. After-commit listener failures cannot roll back the committed tx and are only logged; and DB writes in it need REQUIRES_NEW. @Async moves it to an executor: caller returns immediately; exceptions go to AsyncUncaughtExceptionHandler; context (MDC, security) is lost without a TaskDecorator; events can be lost on shutdown unless executor is configured with setWaitForTasksToCompleteOnShutdown. For anything that must not be lost use the outbox/Kafka instead of in-memory events.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows event semantics (thread, transaction, phase) and the failure modes when they are misunderstood.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Thinks events are always asynchronous and that listeners cannot affect the publisher.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: States @EventListener is synchronous in the publisher's thread and transaction, exceptions propagate, @TransactionalEventListener binds to phase with AFTER_COMMIT default, and is dropped without a transaction unless fallbackExecution.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Covers @Async listeners losing the transaction and security context, exceptions being lost without an async handler, and AFTER_COMMIT needing REQUIRES_NEW for DB writes. Tests listener behaviour for no-transaction and rollback paths. Rule: events for in-process decoupling, not durable messaging.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines when to use in-process events versus outbox/messaging, and documents default thread pools, error handling and observability for async listeners. Reviews event chains that cause hidden coupling. Adds tests ensuring critical events are not dropped by phase misuse.
Consumer lag grows and rebalances keep happening. How do you diagnose and fix it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. Check max.poll.interval.ms vs batch processing time: slow downstream (DB, HTTP) per record × max.poll.records exceeds the interval → member evicted → rebalance storm. Lower max.poll.records, add timeouts to downstream calls, or process asynchronously with care.
  2. Parallelism: partitions ≥ consumer threads; concurrency × pods ≤ partitions; add partitions (breaks key ordering during growth).
  3. Rolling deploys cause rebalances: use static membership (group.instance.id) and cooperative sticky assignor (CooperativeStickyAssignor).
  4. GC pauses/CPU throttling in K8s exceeding session.timeout.ms/heartbeat.
  5. Hot partitions from skewed keys.
  6. Metrics: kafka.consumer via Micrometer (records-lag-max), consumer-group describe (kafka-consumer-groups --describe).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Production diagnosis of consumer lag and rebalance storms using Kafka's timing contracts.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add more consumers' or 'increase partitions' without checking why they rebalance.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Checks processing time against max.poll.interval.ms, lowers max.poll.records, adds downstream timeouts, and matches concurrency and pods to partitions.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Reads lag per partition for skew and hot keys, rebalance logs to see eviction reasons, and separates heartbeat/session timeouts from poll interval. Uses cooperative sticky assignment and static membership to reduce churn. Verifies fixes with lag and rebalance-count metrics under load.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Builds standard dashboards and alerts (lag growth rate, rebalance rate, processing latency) and capacity planning for partitions. Sets team guidelines for downstream timeouts and bulkheads so slow dependencies cannot stall consumers. Runs a post-incident review that changes defaults.

16 · Caching: @Cacheable, Caffeine, Redis and stampedes

A cache is a consistency trade-off with an operational cost.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: A cache is a consistency trade-off with an operational cost.
  • Must-know: ★ @Cacheable key/TTL · ★ eviction and invalidation · ★ cache stampede
  • Top pitfall: self-invocation and missing TTL give stale or no caching.
  • Version markers: Spring 3.1 abstraction; 4.1 JCache
Annotation / featureMeaningSince
@EnableCaching + @Cacheable ★ MUST KNOWCache result by key; skip method on hitSpring 3.1
@CacheEvict, @CachePut, @CachingInvalidate, update, combineSpring 3.1
@Cacheable(sync = true) ★ MUST KNOWOnly one thread computes a missing key (single-JVM stampede protection)Spring 4.0
CacheManager backendsCaffeine (spring.cache.caffeine.spec), Redis (spring.cache.redis.time-to-live), EhCache, JCacheBoot 1.3+
@CacheConfig, SpEL key/condition/unlessKey design, conditional cachingSpring 3.1
Cache metricscache.gets{result=hit|miss} in MicrometerBoot 2.0
⚠ Pitfall@Cacheable is proxy-based: self-invocation, private methods and a missing @EnableCaching silently skip it. Cache keys must be stable and complete (include tenant/locale/user if results vary!). Cached objects must be effectively immutable: callers mutate the shared instance in a local cache.
💡 TipStampede / thundering herd (many requests miss at once, all hit the DB): sync=true (per JVM), Caffeine refreshAfterWrite (serve stale while reloading async), TTL jitter (random ±10%) so keys do not expire together, request coalescing, negative caching with short TTL for “not found”, and a distributed lock only if recompute is really expensive.

Worksheet

How does @Cacheable work, and what are the common reasons it does not cache?
Answer

1. Model Answer

An CacheInterceptor advice around the proxy computes the key (default: params via SimpleKeyGenerator), looks it up in the Cache from the CacheManager; on a miss it invokes the method and stores the result. Not caching because: self-invocation, @EnableCaching missing, private/final method, key collisions or always-different keys (e.g. a timestamp/object without equals), unless/condition evaluating to skip, TTL too short, null results excluded, or cached by value and mutated. Verify with the Micrometer cache.gets hit/miss counters.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands the caching proxy mechanism and diagnoses why it silently fails.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it caches the method result' and does not know about keys or proxy limits.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains CacheInterceptor computing the key, looking in the CacheManager cache, and invoking the method on a miss. Lists self-invocation, missing @EnableCaching, private or final methods, and bad keys.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that null results, mutable cached objects, unbounded caches and missing TTLs cause problems, and that default keys ignore context not in parameters. Verifies hit rate with cache metrics and debug logging. Specifies unless, condition and explicit eviction.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides cache conventions (key format, TTL, naming, metrics) in the platform, and checks them in review. Tracks hit ratio and staleness as operational metrics. Questions whether caching is masking a slow query that should be fixed.
Caffeine vs Redis: how to choose, and can you combine them?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Caffeine (in-process): nanosecond access, no network, no serialisation, bounded by heap; each pod has its own copy so invalidation across pods is eventual (use short TTL or publish evictions). Redis: shared across pods, consistent invalidation, survives restarts, larger capacity; cost is a network hop (~0.5-1 ms), serialisation, an extra component that can fail (set timeouts, fallback to source). Combine as L1 (Caffeine, small TTL) + L2 (Redis): Spring has no built-in two-level manager; use a composite or a library, and broadcast evictions (Redis pub/sub or Kafka). Rule of thumb: reference data that changes rarely → Caffeine; per-user/shared mutable → Redis or no cache.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Trade-off thinking between local and distributed caches, including consistency.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Redis is faster and better' or 'Caffeine is simpler' without describing the trade-off.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Caffeine is in-process with no network or serialisation but per-pod copies; Redis is shared and consistent with network cost and an extra component. Mentions combining them as L1/L2.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Quantifies the cost (sub-microsecond versus roughly a millisecond hop plus serialisation), handles L1 invalidation via short TTL or pub/sub, and sets size bounds and eviction. Plans for Redis outage fallback and tests behaviour. Chooses by data shape: reference data local, shared mutable data in Redis.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides a standard two-level cache abstraction with metrics, and decision guidance by consistency requirement. Considers cost of Redis capacity and failure domains, and runs failure drills. Questions whether a cache is needed or whether data should be reshaped or precomputed.
What is a cache stampede and how do you prevent it in a Spring service?
Answer

1. Model Answer

When a hot key expires (or the cache restarts) hundreds of concurrent requests all miss and hit the DB/downstream, which may collapse, and then the retries make it worse. Prevention in layers: (1) @Cacheable(sync=true) serialises computation per key in the JVM (not across pods; Redis cache manager supports sync with a lock); (2) Caffeine refreshAfterWrite returns the old value while one thread reloads; (3) jittered TTLs; (4) pre-warm on startup/deploy; (5) stale-while-error: serve stale if reload fails (circuit breaker + fallback); (6) rate limit/bulkhead the origin; (7) for cross-pod, a short distributed lock or single-flight via Redis SET NX PX. Mention that sync=true limits you to one cache and no unless.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands thundering-herd failure and layered mitigation.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'increase TTL' or 'cache more' and does not see concurrent misses as the problem.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes many concurrent misses hitting the DB on expiry, and prevention with @Cacheable(sync=true), Caffeine refreshAfterWrite, and TTL jitter.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes sync=true locks only per JVM unless the Redis manager supports locking, so cross-pod stampedes need distributed locks or request coalescing, stale-while-revalidate and pre-warming. Adds circuit breakers and rate limits on the origin. Load-tests expiry of a hot key to confirm.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats it as a platform-level protection: default jitter, refresh-ahead and origin protection in shared cache library. Sets alerts on origin load at cache expiry and on cache restarts, and plans warm-up in deployments. Reviews which keys are hot via metrics.
You cache user-specific data in a shared Redis cache and see one tenant seeing another’s data. How could that happen and how do you prevent it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Key missing the tenant/user (@Cacheable("profile") with key = id only while ids are tenant-scoped, or the default key ignoring a Locale/Principal that is not a parameter but read from SecurityContext inside the method). Prevent: build keys explicitly with SpEL including tenant (#root.target.tenant() or a KeyGenerator using the current tenant), use per-tenant cache names/prefixes (CacheKeyPrefix), never cache results that depend on implicit thread-local state without capturing it in the key, add an integration test that calls with two tenants, and keep cache TTL short for authorisation-dependent data. Same hazard exists with @Async executors reusing threads with stale thread-locals.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Security-sensitive reasoning about cache key design and tenant isolation.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Redis had a bug' or 'clear the cache' without considering key construction.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Identifies keys missing the tenant or user, or context read from SecurityContext inside the method rather than as a parameter, and prevents it with explicit SpEL or a KeyGenerator including the tenant.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Prefixes keys per tenant, avoids caching user-specific data in shared caches when possible, and adds a test with two tenants using the same ids. Includes cache names and versioned prefixes to prevent cross-environment collisions. Treats it as a data-leak incident: purge, audit access, notify.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Enforces tenant-aware caching by providing a mandatory tenant-scoped cache wrapper and banning the raw annotation via ArchUnit. Adds cross-tenant tests to the template and a security review step. Runs incident handling and disclosure processes.

17 · Scheduling and Spring Batch

Run jobs on time, once across a cluster, and process big data in restartable chunks.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Run jobs on time, once across a cluster, and process big data in restartable chunks.
  • Must-know: ★ @Scheduled runs on every node · ★ ShedLock/CronJob · ★ Spring Batch chunk and restart
  • Top pitfall: N pods = N executions of the same scheduled job.
  • Version markers: Spring 3.0 @Scheduled; Batch 5 with Boot 3
FeatureDetailSince
@Scheduled (fixedRate, fixedDelay, cron) ★ MUST KNOWDefault scheduler is single-threaded; spring.task.scheduling.pool.size to raise (Boot 2.1)Spring 3.0
@Scheduled(cron, zone)Always set zone (DST!)Spring 4.0
ShedLock ★ MUST KNOWLibrary: @SchedulerLock(name, lockAtMostFor, lockAtLeastFor) via JDBC/Redis lock table so one pod runs the job3rd party

🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe resume lists hourly batch jobs and payroll outbound extracts. Strong talking points: chunked, restartable processing with a unique JobParameters key per run (idempotent re-run), skip vs fail policy for bad records, only one instance running across pods (ShedLock or a K8s CronJob with concurrencyPolicy: Forbid), reconciliation counts (read/written/skipped) emitted as metrics, and how the file is delivered (atomic rename after write so the consumer never reads partial files).

Worksheet

How do @Scheduled fixedRate, fixedDelay and cron differ? What is the default threading?
Answer

1. Model Answer

fixedRate: start-to-start interval (runs can overlap only if you use an async scheduler; with the default single thread, a long run delays the next). fixedDelay: end-to-start (waits after completion), safest for jobs that must not overlap. cron: calendar expression (6 fields incl. seconds; set zone). initialDelay postpones the first run. The default TaskScheduler has one thread: one slow job blocks all others; Boot’s spring.task.scheduling.pool.size (2.1) raises it. Exceptions in a task are logged and the schedule continues. Needs @EnableScheduling.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows scheduling semantics and the default single-thread trap.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says all three 'run periodically' and is unaware of the single scheduler thread.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: fixedRate is start-to-start, fixedDelay end-to-start, cron is a calendar expression with a zone. Notes the default scheduler is single-threaded and initialDelay.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that a long job delays all other scheduled tasks, so configures a ThreadPoolTaskScheduler, picks fixedDelay for non-overlapping work, sets the cron zone for DST, and handles exceptions so the task keeps running. Observes with thread names and metrics.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides a standard scheduler config and a job wrapper with metrics, timeouts and logging. Recommends moving heavy or critical jobs to a dedicated scheduler or batch system. Documents ownership and alerts for missed runs.
You run 6 replicas and a @Scheduled job must run once. Options and trade-offs?
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. ShedLock: @SchedulerLock acquires a row/key in a shared store (JDBC/Redis/Mongo); lockAtMostFor must exceed worst-case run time (else a second pod starts while first runs), lockAtLeastFor protects against clock skew/very fast runs. Not a scheduler: no retry, no history.
  2. Kubernetes CronJob (concurrencyPolicy: Forbid) running the app (or a batch image) per run: clean isolation, history, retries; slower startup.
  3. Leader election (Spring Integration / K8s lease) for a single active scheduler.
  4. Quartz clustered (JDBC store): full-featured, heavier.
  5. Make the job idempotent anyway: locks fail (GC pause longer than lockAtMostFor).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Distributed scheduling trade-offs: lock-based, external scheduler, or leader election.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'only run it on one pod' or 'use a flag', without a mechanism.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes ShedLock with lockAtMostFor and lockAtLeastFor, Kubernetes CronJob with concurrencyPolicy: Forbid, and other options.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Sets lockAtMostFor above worst-case duration, knows ShedLock is not a retry or history system, and makes the job idempotent regardless since locks can fail. Weighs CronJob (isolation, resource control, startup cost) versus in-app lock (simplicity). Tests with several instances.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Chooses by criticality and visibility: central orchestration for important jobs with history, retries and alerts, in-app locks for lightweight ones. Standardises the approach, ownership and monitoring for missed or double runs. Avoids reinventing a scheduler.
Explain chunk-oriented processing in Spring Batch: reader, processor, writer, commit interval.
Answer

1. Model Answer

A step repeatedly reads items one at a time until chunk size items are collected, processes each (returning null filters the item), then passes the whole list to the writer inside one transaction, then commits and updates the step execution state in JobRepository. A failure rolls back only the current chunk. Readers must be restartable (paging reader with sort key, or cursor reader with saved state) and thread-safe if the step is multi-threaded. Larger chunk = fewer commits, higher memory and larger redo on failure (typical 100–1000). Alternatives: tasklet step for simple actions, partitioning (Partitioner + remote workers) and parallel steps for scale.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands Spring Batch's chunk model, transaction boundaries and restartability.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'read, process, write' with no idea about chunks or commits.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains reading item by item until chunk size, processing (null filters), one write per chunk within a transaction, then commit and JobRepository update. Failure rolls back only the chunk.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Chooses chunk size by measurement, uses restartable paging readers with a unique sort key, and avoids cursor readers across threads. Configures skip and retry policies and knows processors should be side-effect free. Observes with step execution metrics.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets standards for job design (idempotency, restart, parameters, metadata store) and monitoring. Decides when Spring Batch is warranted versus simple jobs or streaming. Plans capacity and scheduling around database load.
A batch job failed at row 90,000 of 100,000. How do you make it restartable and idempotent?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Spring Batch records each committed chunk’s read count in BATCH_STEP_EXECUTION_CONTEXT; restarting the same JobInstance (same identifying JobParameters) resumes from the last commit if the reader is restartable (saveState, JdbcPagingItemReader with unique sort key). Idempotency: writer does upsert or uses a unique key so a re-processed chunk (crash between write commit and metadata update is in the same tx, but external side effects are not) is harmless; no non-transactional side effects (emails, file append) in the writer without dedupe; write output to a temp file and rename at job end. For a clean rerun use a new JobParameter (RunIdIncrementer). Handle bad data with skip + a skip listener writing rejects to an error table instead of failing the whole run. Monitor with metrics and alert on skip count thresholds.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designing failure recovery and idempotency for long-running jobs.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'rerun the job from the start' and hopes duplicates do not matter.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Restarts the same JobInstance with identical identifying parameters so it resumes from the last committed chunk with a restartable reader; makes the writer an upsert or uses unique keys.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes the chunk in flight is reprocessed, so the write must be idempotent, and ensures reader state saving and a stable sort key. Adds skip and failure handling with a bad-row report, and tests by killing the job mid-run. Covers external side effects.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises restartable job templates and runbooks for failed jobs, with ownership, alerts and reconciliation reports. Considers partitioned steps and capacity impact on restart. Treats data correctness checks after reruns as part of the process.
What changed in Spring Batch 5 / Boot 3 that breaks existing job configs?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Builder factories removed: JobBuilderFactory/StepBuilderFactory → new JobBuilder(name, jobRepository), new StepBuilder(name, jobRepository); .chunk(n, transactionManager) takes the tx manager explicitly; @EnableBatchProcessing is no longer required and, when present, turns off Boot’s batch auto-configuration (so remove it or configure everything yourself); job parameters types and JobParameters identification rules changed (non-identifying, any type); new JobRegistry/DefaultBatchConfiguration to customise; Java 17 and Jakarta. Also the schema changed (new/removed columns in metadata tables): migrate with the provided scripts, and in Boot 3 spring.batch.jdbc.initialize-schema controls creation.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Has actually migrated a batch job across a major version and knows which API and infrastructure assumptions changed, not just that 'things were renamed'.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Spring Batch 5 needs Java 17 and some classes are deprecated' and cannot name the specific removals. Tries JobBuilderFactory first and is surprised it no longer compiles.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Names the removal of JobBuilderFactory/StepBuilderFactory in favour of new JobBuilder(name, jobRepository), and that .chunk(n, transactionManager) now takes the transaction manager explicitly. Mentions @EnableBatchProcessing is no longer needed.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that @EnableBatchProcessing actually switches off Boot's batch auto-configuration, so leaving it in silently changes behaviour (for example, JobLauncherApplicationRunner stops running jobs at startup). Also flags the batch metadata schema changes and JobParameter typing, and verifies by restarting a failed execution against a copy of the production metadata tables before cutover.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats it as a fleet problem: inventories batch services, publishes a migration recipe (OpenRewrite) and a reference job template, and adds a CI check that fails on @EnableBatchProcessing and the removed factories. Plans metadata-schema migration and in-flight job restartability as the real risk, sequenced with the Boot 3 upgrade rather than as a separate surprise.

18 · Resilience: Spring Retry, Resilience4j, timeouts, bulkheads and graceful degradation

Assume every dependency will be slow or down. Design what your service does then.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Assume every dependency will be slow or down; design the fallback.
  • Must-know: ★ timeouts first · ★ retry only idempotent calls with backoff · ★ circuit breaker and bulkhead
  • Top pitfall: retry storms amplify an outage.
  • Version markers: Resilience4j with Boot 2+; Framework 7 core retry (verify)
PatternTool in SpringWhat it protects againstSince
Timeout ★ MUST KNOWClient factory timeouts, @TimeLimiter, CompletableFuture.orTimeoutHung dependency exhausting threadscore
Retry ★ MUST KNOWSpring Retry @Retryable / RetryTemplate; Resilience4j @Retry; Spring Framework 7 core @Retryable + @EnableResilientMethods (verify)Transient faultsSpring Retry 1.x; core 7.0

🔑 Key ideaAspect nesting in Resilience4j (outermost to innermost): Retry ( CircuitBreaker ( RateLimiter ( TimeLimiter ( Bulkhead ( function ) ) ) ) ). So the circuit breaker sees each retry attempt as a separate call, and the retry stops being useful when the breaker is open (it fails fast with CallNotPermittedException, which you normally do not retry).
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe retail platform’s fork-and-join aggregation with graceful degradation on partial failure is exactly the diagram: parallel calls, per-branch timeout and fallback, critical vs optional branches, partial response flagged to the caller. Expect “how do you test it” (MockServer with delayed/500 responses), “how do you observe it” (per-branch timer + degraded counter), and “what is the thread/pool limit”.

Worksheet

How does @Retryable work and what are the pitfalls?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@EnableRetry adds an advisor (proxy) so a method throwing the listed exceptions is re-invoked per maxAttempts with a @Backoff; after exhaustion a matching @Recover method runs or the last exception propagates. Pitfalls: proxy rules (self-invocation does not retry); retrying non-idempotent operations; no jitter/cap → synchronised retry storms; retries stacked at several layers multiply; @Retryable inside a @Transactional method retries inside the same tx (see AOP section); blocking sleep holds a thread (bad on a small pool, fine on virtual threads); retry only on transient exceptions via retryFor/noRetryFor (the attribute was include/value before spring-retry 2.0). Spring Framework 7 brings equivalent annotations into core (verify in release notes).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands that @Retryable is proxy-based AOP and can reason about when retrying is safe or harmful.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'annotate the method with @Retryable(maxAttempts=3) and it retries'. Calls it from another method in the same class and cannot explain why nothing retries.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains that @EnableRetry adds an advisor, so only calls through the proxy are retried; describes @Backoff and the @Recover fallback. Notes self-invocation does not work.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that retries need idempotent operations, a retry-only-on-transient exception list, exponential backoff with jitter and a cap, and awareness of stacking with a retrying HTTP client or Kafka consumer. Mentions that retry inside an open @Transactional holds the connection and that they would verify with a test that counts invocations and a metric on attempts.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a rule that retries live at exactly one layer per call chain and are expressed through a shared platform wrapper with budget limits, not ad hoc annotations. Enforces it with ArchUnit or code review checklist, tracks retry-rate dashboards and alerts, and notes the migration path as retry support moves into Spring Framework 7 core.
Explain the circuit breaker states and the settings that matter.
Answer

1. Model Answer

CLOSED: calls pass, results recorded in a sliding window (count or time based). When failure rate (or slow-call rate) ≥ threshold after minimumNumberOfCalls, it goes OPEN: calls fail fast with CallNotPermittedException for waitDurationInOpenState. Then HALF_OPEN: a few trial calls (permittedNumberOfCallsInHalfOpenState); success rate decides close vs reopen. Tune: what counts as failure (recordExceptions/ignoreExceptions: a 404 or business rejection should not trip it), slow-call threshold (latency is failure too), window sized for traffic (too small = flapping, too large = slow to react), per-dependency instances. Expose state via Actuator health/metrics and alert on OPEN. A breaker without a meaningful fallback just changes the error.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Can model a circuit breaker as a state machine and tune the knobs against real traffic rather than defaults.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Recites 'closed, open, half-open' as 'working, broken, testing' and says 'it stops calls when there are failures'. Cannot name any configuration property.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes the sliding window, failure-rate threshold after minimumNumberOfCalls, fail-fast with CallNotPermittedException for waitDurationInOpenState, and the half-open trial calls deciding close or reopen.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds slow-call rate as a trigger, count vs time-based windows and why low-traffic services need a small minimumNumberOfCalls, and that business exceptions (4xx) must be ignored or they trip the breaker. Verifies by fault-injecting a slow downstream and watching the state-transition metrics and events.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines per-dependency breaker defaults in a shared starter, with dashboards for state transitions and alerts on open duration. Warns about breakers hiding root cause, pairs them with timeouts and bulkheads, and documents which fallbacks are acceptable per domain so teams do not invent unsafe defaults.
Design graceful degradation for an aggregation endpoint that calls 4 backends. How do you test and observe it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Classify each backend: critical (no response without it → 503 with Retry-After) vs optional (omit/cached/default). Per branch: timeout from a total latency budget (e.g. P99 budget 2 s: branches at 800 ms), breaker + bulkhead, fallback. Response includes a degraded marker (header or field) so clients can render “unavailable” instead of wrong data; never present a fallback as fresh. Avoid cascading: bounded executor per dependency; shed load rather than queue unboundedly. Test: MockServer/WireMock scenarios (delay, 500, connection drop, slow-drip body) per branch asserting the response and latency, plus a load test showing p99 stays bounded with one dependency dead. Observe: timer per dependency, counters fallback.used, breaker state gauge, alert on degraded ratio. Be explicit about consistency: a degraded response must not be cached as if complete.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Design thinking for partial failure: classifying dependencies, defining degraded contracts, and proving the design works under fault.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add a circuit breaker and a fallback to each call'. Returns an empty list or a default silently, so clients cannot tell data is missing.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Separates critical from optional backends, gives each call a timeout from the total latency budget, adds breaker and bulkhead plus fallback, and signals degradation to clients with a header or field.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Calls branches in parallel with bounded concurrency, returns 503 with Retry-After only when a critical dependency fails, and tests with fault injection (WireMock delays/errors, Toxiproxy) asserting both the body and the degraded marker. Observes via per-branch outcome metrics, fallback counters and an SLO on degraded-response rate.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Gets product to agree on what 'degraded' means per field and publishes it as an API contract. Runs periodic game days or chaos tests in staging, and reuses the pattern as a platform library so each aggregation service gets the same budgets, metrics and dashboards.
Bulkhead and timeouts: how do they prevent a slow dependency from taking down the whole service?
Answer

1. Model Answer

A slow dependency with no timeout holds request threads; with 200 Tomcat threads and 200 concurrent requests blocked on it, the whole service (including health checks and unrelated endpoints) stops responding. Timeouts bound the hold time; a bulkhead bounds how many threads/permits the dependency may use (semaphore for blocking calls, thread-pool bulkhead with queue), so the rest remain available; combine with breaker to stop calling at all. Size by Little’s law: concurrency = throughput × latency (200 rps × 0.2 s = 40 concurrent; allow ~2x headroom). Separate executors/pools per dependency and a separate management port/thread pool for health. On virtual threads the thread cost disappears but the downstream limit and pool (DB, HTTP connections) still need bulkheads (Semaphore/@ConcurrencyLimit).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands thread exhaustion as the actual mechanism of cascading failure and how timeouts and bulkheads bound it.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'use Resilience4j bulkhead and timeouts to isolate failures' without explaining what resource is being protected.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains that without timeouts, all 200 Tomcat threads block on the slow dependency and even health checks fail. Timeouts bound hold time; a bulkhead caps how many threads/permits one dependency can use (semaphore or thread-pool type).
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that timeouts must be set at every layer (connect, read, pool acquire) with the inner shorter than the outer, that semaphore bulkheads suit blocking calls without extra threads while thread-pool bulkheads add queueing, and that you size them from Little's law. Verifies with a load test against a latency-injected dependency, watching thread-busy and rejected-call metrics.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes timeout and bulkhead defaults a platform concern: client builders come with sane timeouts, and a lint or review rule rejects clients without them. Ties the numbers to SLOs and error budgets, and frames virtual threads as moving the bottleneck to the semaphore, DB pool and downstream, not eliminating it.
Retries made the outage worse. Explain retry storms and how you prevent them.
Answer

1. Model Answer

When a dependency slows, each caller times out and retries; with N layers × M attempts the load multiplies (3 layers × 3 attempts = 27x), turning degradation into collapse; synchronised retries without jitter arrive in waves. Prevent: retry at one layer only (usually the edge/nearest caller), cap attempts (1-2), exponential backoff + full jitter, retry budgets (e.g. retries ≤ 10% of requests), honour Retry-After, do not retry on breaker-open or 4xx, deadline propagation so deep calls do not retry after the caller gave up, and load shedding at the server (429/503 quickly). Verify with a chaos/load test that injects latency.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Reasons about load amplification across layers and applies disciplined retry policy.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'retries help with transient errors, so add more retries'. Does not connect retries to the outage getting worse.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains multiplication: 3 layers x 3 attempts yields 27x load, and that synchronised retries without jitter arrive in waves. Fixes: retry at one layer, cap attempts, exponential backoff with full jitter.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds retry budgets (for example retry only when retries are under about 10 percent of requests), honouring Retry-After, not retrying on timeouts of non-idempotent calls, and pairing retries with breakers and load shedding. Confirms from traces that one user request produced many downstream spans, and verifies the fix with a load test.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises a single retry policy and owner per call path across teams, with gateways or service mesh as the one retry layer. Adds incident-review guidance, dashboards for retry ratio, and platform defaults so the amplification cannot be silently reintroduced.

19 · Observability: Actuator, Micrometer, Observation API, tracing, structured logging

Metrics, traces and logs correlated by trace id; health probes for Kubernetes.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Metrics, traces and logs correlated by trace id; health probes for K8s.
  • Must-know: ★ Actuator health groups · ★ Micrometer Observation · ★ trace id in logs
  • Top pitfall: exposing all actuator endpoints publicly.
  • Version markers: Boot 2.0 Micrometer; Boot 3.0 Micrometer Tracing

Worksheet

Liveness vs readiness vs startup probes in Spring Boot on Kubernetes: what should each check?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Liveness: “is the process stuck beyond repair?” → failure restarts the pod. It must be internal only (livenessState, deadlock detection) and never depend on the DB/Kafka: a DB outage would restart every pod and prolong the outage. Readiness: “can I take traffic now?” → failure removes the pod from Service endpoints; may include DB/broker health, and Boot flips it to REFUSING_TRAFFIC during graceful shutdown. Startup probe protects slow starts from liveness kills. Boot 2.3 added the probe groups and AvailabilityChangeEvent; management.endpoint.health.probes.enabled=true (auto when running on K8s). Put them on the management port.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows how Kubernetes probes interact with application health and the cost of a wrong liveness check.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'liveness checks if the app is up, readiness checks if it is ready' and points both at /actuator/health.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Liveness means 'restart me' and must be internal only; readiness means 'send me traffic' and may include dependencies; startup protects slow boots. Mentions Boot's livenessState/readinessState probe groups.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that a liveness check depending on the DB restarts every pod in a DB outage and prolongs it, whereas readiness tied to the DB removes pods from endpoints. Covers startup probe thresholds, readiness flipping to REFUSING_TRAFFIC on shutdown, and verifies with a chaos test that kills the DB and watches pod restarts.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides standard Helm/chart probe settings and Boot property defaults in a platform template, with a rule that readiness lists only dependencies a pod cannot serve without. Reviews incidents where probes caused restarts and publishes the guidance in the runbook.
What is the Observation API and how does it relate to Micrometer and Sleuth?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Micrometer 1.10 / Spring 6.0 introduced Observation: you instrument once (Observation.start/observe) and registered ObservationHandlers turn it into a timer metric, a tracing span, and log context. Spring MVC, WebClient/RestClient, JDBC (with datasource-micrometer), Kafka (spring.kafka.template.observation-enabled) are instrumented through it in Boot 3. Spring Cloud Sleuth is not used with Boot 3: its tracing moved to Micrometer Tracing with a bridge (OpenTelemetry or Brave) + a reporter (OTLP/Zipkin). Metric names changed (e.g. http.server.requests is now the observation’s timer, http.client.requests for outbound), and tags must be low-cardinality; high-cardinality values (user id, sku) go in the span only.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows the modern Spring observability model and migration path from Sleuth.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Sleuth does tracing and Micrometer does metrics' and treats them as separate things.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Observation API instruments once and ObservationHandlers produce timers, spans and log context. Sleuth is replaced by Micrometer Tracing in Boot 3; MVC, RestClient/WebClient, Kafka and JDBC are covered through it.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that clients only propagate trace context when built from Boot's builders, that you add a bridge (OTel or Brave) and exporter, and that sampling and low-cardinality vs high-cardinality key values matter. Verifies by checking a trace across two services and the matching timer metric.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines a company-wide convention for custom observation names and key values, ships it as a starter, and uses the migration from Sleuth to unify metrics, traces and logs under OpenTelemetry. Watches cost of sampling and cardinality as a budget item.
Metric cardinality: what goes wrong and how do you avoid it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Each unique tag-value combination creates a time series in the registry (memory in the app) and in Prometheus; tagging by user id, URL with ids (/orders/123), or exception messages grows unbounded and can OOM the app or the TSDB. Boot’s MVC metrics use the URI template (/orders/{id}), so keep using templates; for unmatched paths it reports NOT_FOUND. Rules: tag only bounded enums (status class, method, outcome, dependency name); use MeterFilter.maximumAllowableTags or deny to cap; put identifiers in traces/logs. Percentiles: prefer publishPercentileHistogram (aggregable server-side) over client-side percentiles (not aggregable across pods).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Production awareness of metrics cost: unbounded tags as a memory and bill risk.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'too many metrics are slow' and suggests adding more memory. Tags by user id without noticing.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Each unique tag combination creates a time series; tagging by user id, raw URL or exception message grows unbounded. Use URI templates and bounded values.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds concrete detection (series counts in Prometheus, MeterFilter.maximumAllowableTags, deny filters), notes unmatched paths report NOT_FOUND for this reason, and moves high-cardinality detail to traces and logs. Verifies by checking top series per metric after a release.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats cardinality as a platform budget with per-service limits, alerts on series growth, and a registry-level MeterFilter in the shared starter. Includes a review checklist for new tags and teaches the metrics vs traces vs logs split.
How would you set up logging so you can trace one request across five services in Splunk/ELK?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Propagate trace context (W3C traceparent) through all clients (Observation does it for RestClient/WebClient/Kafka headers when instrumented beans are built from Boot’s builders), include traceId/spanId in every log line (MDC, enabled with tracing on the classpath), emit structured JSON (logging.structured.format.console=ecs in 3.4; earlier Logback + logstash-encoder), add service/env/version fields (logging.structured.json.add) and a business correlation id header for non-trace-aware callers. Keep PII out of logs (mask, allow-list fields; audit annotations should take explicit parameters). Return the trace id in error bodies (ProblemDetail extension). Log sampling for high-volume paths; retention per compliance. For async handoffs copy MDC with a TaskDecorator.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: End-to-end observability design across services, not just 'add logging'.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'log a correlation id' and perhaps 'use ELK'. Generates a UUID manually in each service.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Propagates W3C traceparent through clients, puts traceId/spanId in MDC on every log line, and emits structured JSON that Splunk/ELK can index.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that propagation only works for instrumented beans built from Boot's builders, that async hops and Kafka need context propagation configured, and uses ECS structured logging in Boot 3.4. Verifies by searching one traceId and confirming all five services show up, and watches for PII and log volume cost.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises the log schema and field names across teams, makes it the default in the platform starter, and links logs to traces in dashboards. Sets retention and sampling policy tied to cost, and adds a CI or runtime check for missing traceId.
Your app’s P99 latency doubled but CPU is flat and no errors. Walk through the diagnosis using Spring/Actuator tooling.
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. Which endpoints? http.server.requests by uri/outcome percentiles; trace one slow request to see the span breakdown (DB? downstream? queue?).
  2. Saturation: Hikari hikaricp.connections.pending/usage/acquire (pool starvation shows as acquire time), Tomcat tomcat.threads.busy, executor queue sizes, jvm.gc.pause, jvm.threads.states (blocked).
  3. Downstream: http.client.requests per host; breaker states; DB slow-query log/pg_stat_statements.
  4. Take 3 thread dumps 5 s apart (/actuator/threaddump or jcmd <pid> Thread.print): many threads WAITING in HikariPool.getConnection or Socket.read tells the story.
  5. Recent change? Compare against the performance baseline, check deployment, cache hit ratio, GC logs.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Systematic production debugging: narrowing a latency regression with metrics before guessing.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'check the logs and maybe add more CPU' or 'restart it'. Jumps to a cause without data.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Starts with http.server.requests percentiles by uri, traces a slow request to see the span breakdown, then checks saturation: Hikari pending/acquire time, Tomcat busy threads, executor queues, GC pauses.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes flat CPU plus doubled latency suggests waiting, not computing: pool starvation, a slow downstream, lock contention or GC pauses. Takes thread dumps to confirm, compares with the last deploy, and verifies the fix against the same dashboards.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Has a standard latency runbook and pre-built dashboards (RED plus saturation) in the platform, with exemplars linking metrics to traces. Runs a blameless review that adds the missing alert, and compares with deploy markers so regressions are caught earlier.

20 · Concurrency: virtual threads, @Async executors, WebFlux vs MVC

Pick the concurrency model deliberately and know what each one changes.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Pick the concurrency model deliberately.
  • Must-know: ★ virtual threads (spring.threads.virtual.enabled) · ★ MVC vs WebFlux · ★ @Async executor sizing
  • Top pitfall: blocking calls inside WebFlux event loop; pinned virtual threads.
  • Version markers: Java 21 / Boot 3.2 virtual threads
ModelHow it scalesGotchasSince
Thread-per-request (Tomcat, 200 threads) ★ MUST KNOWBlocking code; capacity = threadsThreads blocked on I/O waste capacity; tune server.tomcat.threads.maxServlet
Virtual threads ★ MUST KNOWspring.threads.virtual.enabled=true (Java 21+): Tomcat/Jetty request handling, @Async and scheduling executors use virtual threadsPool limits (Hikari) become the bottleneck; pinning on synchronized + blocking I/O (fixed in JDK 24, JEP 491); ThreadLocal-heavy codeBoot 3.2 / Spring 6.1
WebFlux / ReactorEvent loop, few threads, backpressureNever block the event loop; reactive drivers needed (R2DBC); steep debuggingSpring 5.0
@Async + ThreadPoolTaskExecutorOffload to bounded poolUnbounded queue; lost exceptions for void; context propagationSpring 3.0
TaskExecutor auto-config (applicationTaskExecutor)Boot defines a ThreadPoolTaskExecutor; spring.task.execution.pool.*Backs off if you define your own Executor (verify behaviour in your Boot version)Boot 2.1
🔑 Key ideaVirtual threads help I/O-bound blocking code (many concurrent waits). They do nothing for CPU-bound work, they do not make a slow database faster, and a connection pool of 10 still means 10 concurrent queries. They change the question from “how many threads?” to “how many concurrent resources?” → add explicit limits (semaphore/bulkhead/@ConcurrencyLimit in Spring 7).
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe retail platform’s fork-and-join aggregation is the classic use for a dedicated bounded executor (or virtual threads + a semaphore per dependency). With an active-active, high-traffic profile, the lesson is the same: measure with JMeter/YourKit before and after changing the threading model, and check pool saturation, not just CPU.

Worksheet

What do virtual threads change in a Spring Boot app, and how do you enable them?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Java 21 virtual threads are cheap JVM-scheduled threads that unmount from their carrier while blocked on I/O. In Boot 3.2+ set spring.threads.virtual.enabled=true: Tomcat/Jetty handle each request on a virtual thread, @Async and @Scheduled executors switch to virtual-thread-based ones. Blocking MVC code then handles tens of thousands of concurrent waits without a big platform thread pool. Not a free lunch: bounded resources (Hikari pool, HTTP connection pools, downstream limits) now cap you; synchronized blocks with blocking I/O pin the carrier on JDK 21–23; ThreadLocal per-request caches become memory heavy; CPU-bound work still needs bounded platform threads. Keep timeouts, keep bulkheads.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands what virtual threads change in the concurrency model and what they do not.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'virtual threads make everything faster' and 'just turn them on'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Virtual threads unmount from carriers while blocked on I/O; in Boot 3.2+ set spring.threads.virtual.enabled=true so Tomcat/Jetty, @Async and @Scheduled use them.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes this helps I/O-bound blocking code, not CPU-bound work, and that the thread cap disappears so DB pool and downstream limits become the bottleneck. Mentions pinning with synchronized on older JDKs and verifies with load tests and JFR pinning events.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Rolls out per service behind a flag with explicit concurrency limits at the DB pool and via semaphores, and compares against WebFlux or tuned platform threads for the workload. Writes a team guide with when not to use it.
WebFlux vs Spring MVC in 2025: how do you choose?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Default to MVC (simpler, debuggable, rich ecosystem, JDBC/JPA), now scaled by virtual threads. Choose WebFlux when: streaming/SSE/WebSocket with many long-lived connections, a gateway-like fan-out where non-blocking composition and backpressure matter, or the whole stack is reactive (R2DBC, reactive Mongo/Redis/Kafka). Costs: a different programming model, stack traces that lose causality, blocking calls accidentally stall the event loop (use BlockHound), JPA is not reactive. Mixed use is fine: MVC app using WebClient for outbound calls. Spring Cloud Gateway is WebFlux-based (MVC variant exists).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Architectural judgement, avoiding technology fashion: choosing WebFlux only for real reasons.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'WebFlux is faster and non-blocking so it is better'. Cannot name the cost.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Defaults to MVC and virtual threads; WebFlux for streaming/SSE/WebSocket, gateways and fully reactive stacks.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds costs: harder debugging, context propagation, blocking calls on event loops, smaller ecosystem for JDBC/JPA. Decides by measuring workload, connection counts and team skills.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a documented default (MVC) and an exception process for WebFlux. Considers hiring, onboarding and operational cost, and revisits as virtual threads and Boot evolve.
@Async is not working or silently drops errors and context. List the failure modes and fixes.
Answer

1. Model Answer

  1. Needs @EnableAsync and a proxy call (not this.x()); method public.
  2. Default executor: without Boot, SimpleAsyncTaskExecutor creates a new thread per task (unbounded). Boot supplies applicationTaskExecutor (core 8, unbounded queue by default!): define your own bounded one.
  3. Return void → exceptions go to AsyncUncaughtExceptionHandler only; return CompletableFuture so callers can handle failures.
  4. Context loss: SecurityContext, MDC, request attributes, and transactions do not propagate: use TaskDecorator/DelegatingSecurityContextAsyncTaskExecutor; the async method runs in its own transaction.
  5. Shutdown loses queued work: waitForTasksToCompleteOnShutdown + await seconds.
  6. Queue full → RejectedExecutionException: choose CallerRuns or reject consciously.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows @Async failure modes: proxies, executors and error handling.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add @Async and it runs in the background'. Does not know why exceptions vanish.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Needs @EnableAsync, a proxy call (not this.x()), a public method, and returns void sends exceptions to the uncaught handler only.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the default executor is unbounded or has an unbounded queue, so define a bounded one with a rejection policy, and that security/MDC/transaction context does not propagate without a TaskDecorator. Returns CompletableFuture and tests error paths and thread names.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Bans naked @Async through a platform executor configuration and ArchUnit rules, and exposes executor metrics. Prefers durable messaging for work that must not be lost and documents the trade-off.
Describe a production risk when enabling virtual threads on an existing Spring Boot service, and how you roll it out safely.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Risk: removing the thread cap lets concurrency jump from 200 to thousands, which floods the DB pool (threads queue on getConnection with 30 s timeout), downstream services, and memory (each in-flight request holds objects). Pinning (synchronized around blocking calls in older libraries, JDBC drivers, native calls) can deadlock-ish stall on JDK 21–23 (check -Djdk.tracePinnedThreads=full, JFR VirtualThreadPinned); ThreadLocal caches (e.g. per-thread buffers) multiply. Rollout: canary with a feature flag (property), load test against a staging DB first, add explicit concurrency limits (Hikari max, per-dependency semaphores, request-level limiter), monitor hikaricp.connections.pending, p99, and GC; verify libraries (Tomcat 10.1.x, driver versions) are updated; keep the ability to roll back via config only.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Risk assessment when changing a core runtime assumption in production.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'enable the flag, it is just config' with no discussion of risk.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Removing the thread cap can overload the DB pool and downstream; pinning can stall; memory rises with in-flight requests.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Rolls out with canary, bounds DB with pool size and semaphores, checks JFR for pinning, monitors pending connections, p99, memory, and has a rollback flag.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Writes the runbook, defines go/no-go metrics, upgrades JDK and drivers first, and sets platform guidance on which services qualify and shared limits.
How would you size Tomcat threads, Hikari pool and an @Async executor for a service doing 300 rps with 150 ms average DB-bound latency?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Little’s law: concurrency ≈ 300 × 0.15 = 45 in-flight requests on average; provision ~2x for variance → Tomcat threads.max ≈ 100 (default 200 fine); but the DB: if each request holds one connection for ~100 ms of that, the DB concurrency ≈ 30; Hikari maximum-pool-size ≈ 20-30 is plenty (small pools beat big pools: DB cores bound real parallelism; formula of thumb connections = core_count × 2 + spindles, per DB, divided across pods!). With 6 pods × 30 = 180 connections may exceed Postgres max_connections: use fewer per pod or PgBouncer. Executor: bounded, sized by the dependency’s capacity, not by CPU of the app. Validate by load test and look at pending connection metrics, not guesses.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Can turn a throughput and latency figure into concrete thread and connection numbers with Little's law, and knows that the DB, not the app, caps useful concurrency.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'set Tomcat threads high enough and make the Hikari pool the same size so nothing waits', or just keeps the default 200 threads and maximum-pool-size=10 without any arithmetic. Cannot explain where a number would come from.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Applies Little's law: 300 rps x 0.15 s = 45 in-flight requests, so server.tomcat.threads.max around 100 gives roughly 2x headroom. Derives roughly 30 concurrent DB connections from ~100 ms held per request and sets maximum-pool-size to 20-30, with a bounded @Async executor.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Stresses that a small pool beats a big one because DB cores bound real parallelism, and that the pool is per pod: 6 pods x 30 = 180 connections can blow past Postgres max_connections, so use fewer per pod or PgBouncer. Sizes the @Async executor by the downstream dependency's capacity with a bounded queue and a rejection policy, not by app CPU. Validates with a load test watching hikaricp.connections.pending and acquire time rather than guessing. Rule: derive, then measure, then adjust.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Turns the sizing into a team default: a platform-owned Boot starter ships a pool-sizing formula and a fleet-level connection budget (pods x pool <= ~70% of max_connections) that is checked at deploy time, so autoscaling cannot silently exhaust the DB. Adds an SLO-linked alert on pending connections and acquire time, and a capacity review whenever HPA max replicas changes. Uses the question to teach that 'more threads' is not capacity and to decide when PgBouncer or RDS Proxy is cheaper than a bigger database.

21 · Packaging and operations: layered jars, buildpacks, CDS, native, graceful shutdown

Ship small, start fast, stop cleanly.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Ship small, start fast, stop cleanly.
  • Must-know: ★ layered jars / buildpacks · ★ graceful shutdown · ★ native image trade-offs
  • Top pitfall: SIGTERM without graceful shutdown drops in-flight requests.
  • Version markers: Boot 2.3 layers + graceful; Boot 3.0 native GA

💡 TipJVM in containers: use -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=70-75 (heap is only part of memory: metaspace, thread stacks, direct buffers, code cache), set requests = limits for memory, avoid CPU limits that throttle GC/JIT threads (or set at least 2 CPUs), and enable -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError to a mounted volume. Java 17+ is container-aware by default.

Worksheet

How does graceful shutdown work in Spring Boot, and what do you configure on Kubernetes?
Answer

1. Model Answer

On SIGTERM Boot starts the shutdown phases: the readiness state flips to REFUSING_TRAFFIC, with server.shutdown=graceful the web server stops accepting new connections but lets in-flight requests finish up to spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase (default 30 s), then SmartLifecycle beans stop (Kafka containers), then beans are destroyed. Default is immediate (requests are cut). On K8s: pod removal and SIGTERM race with endpoint propagation, so add a preStop sleep (3-10 s) so the load balancer stops routing first, and set terminationGracePeriodSeconds ≥ preStop + phase timeout + margin. Verify with a load test during a rolling deploy: zero 5xx expected.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows the shutdown sequence Boot runs on SIGTERM and the Kubernetes race between endpoint removal and the signal, so deploys do not drop requests.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Spring Boot shuts down gracefully by default when Kubernetes stops the pod' and has never set server.shutdown. Believes in-flight requests are always finished.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains that with server.shutdown=graceful the web server stops accepting new connections and lets in-flight requests finish within spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase (30 s default). Notes readiness flips to REFUSING_TRAFFIC, then SmartLifecycle beans such as Kafka containers stop, then beans are destroyed.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Points out the default is immediate shutdown, so requests are cut unless configured. Adds the K8s race: SIGTERM and endpoint propagation happen concurrently, so a preStop sleep of 3-10 s lets the load balancer drop the pod first, and terminationGracePeriodSeconds must be at least preStop + phase timeout + margin. Verifies with a load test during a rolling deploy expecting zero 5xx. Rule: the grace period budget is always larger than the sum of its parts.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Packages this as a platform default (graceful shutdown, readiness group, preStop, grace budget) in the base Helm chart or starter, with a CI check that the numbers add up. Tracks 5xx and connection-reset counts during deploy windows as a release SLI. Also covers non-HTTP work: Kafka consumers and scheduled jobs that must finish or hand off before the grace period ends. Uses incidents of 502s at deploy time to drive adoption across teams.
What are layered jars and why do they help in Docker?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Boot splits the fat jar into layers ordered by change frequency (dependencies, spring-boot-loader, snapshot-dependencies, application). Extract them and COPY each layer separately in the Dockerfile: Docker’s layer cache re-uses the large unchanged dependency layers so CI pushes/pulls only the few KB of application code per build. Alternatives: Buildpacks (build-image) do this automatically with a reproducible, patched base and an SBOM; Jib builds layered images without Docker daemon. Launching the extracted layout with JarLauncher (or exploded) also starts slightly faster than the nested jar.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands Docker layer caching and how Boot's layered jar uses it to shrink build and deploy cost, plus the alternatives to hand-written Dockerfiles.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Copies the fat jar into the image with COPY app.jar and says layered jars 'make the image smaller'. Does not connect it to Docker layer cache invalidation.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains that Boot orders layers by change frequency (dependencies, spring-boot-loader, snapshot-dependencies, application). Extracting them and COPYing each layer separately means the large dependency layer is cached and only the small application layer is rebuilt and pushed.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Quantifies the benefit: CI pushes and pulls kilobytes of app code instead of a 100+ MB jar every build, which also speeds node pulls during autoscaling. Mentions the alternatives, Buildpacks (build-image) giving a patched base and an SBOM, and Jib building without a Docker daemon. Notes that running the extracted layout via JarLauncher starts slightly faster than the nested jar. Rule: use Buildpacks or Jib by default, hand-roll the Dockerfile only for special needs.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats image build as a supply-chain concern: standardise on one build path with a rebuilt-on-CVE base image schedule, SBOM output and image signing, rather than each team owning a Dockerfile. Measures build time, image pull time and registry storage cost to show the gain. Enforces via a shared CI template and a lint that rejects single-layer fat-jar images, and mentors teams on why dependency layers must not be invalidated by timestamps or version bumps.
Native image vs JVM: when would you choose GraalVM native for a Spring Boot service? What are the constraints?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Choose native for scale-to-zero/serverless, CLI tools, or very dense fleets where startup (tens of ms) and RSS matter. Constraints: closed-world analysis, so reflection, proxies, resources and serialization need hints (Spring’s AOT engine generates most; add RuntimeHintsRegistrar/@RegisterReflectionForBinding for yours); runtime conditions are fixed at build time (profiles that change beans, @ConditionalOnProperty evaluated at build); no dynamic class loading/agents (YourKit/JFR limited), longer builds (minutes, GBs of RAM), lower peak throughput than C2, proxyBeanMethods and library support matter. Test the native binary in CI (nativeTest). For a long-running latency-sensitive API on a stable fleet, plain JVM + CDS is usually the pragmatic choice.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Judges when GraalVM native is worth its build-time and runtime constraints versus tuned JVM, and can name the closed-world limitations concretely.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'native image is faster so we should use it everywhere' or 'it is just a compile flag'. Is unaware that reflection and proxies need extra configuration.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: States that native suits scale-to-zero, serverless, CLI tools and dense fleets because of tens-of-ms startup and low RSS. Lists the constraint of closed-world analysis, so reflection, proxies, resources and serialization need hints, most generated by Spring's AOT engine, with RuntimeHintsRegistrar or @RegisterReflectionForBinding for custom cases.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that conditions are fixed at build time, so profiles that change beans and @ConditionalOnProperty are evaluated once. Notes no dynamic class loading or agents (JFR and profiling are limited), builds take minutes and GBs of RAM, and peak throughput is lower than C2. Insists on running the native binary in CI (nativeTest). Rule: for a long-running latency-sensitive API on a stable fleet, plain JVM plus CDS is the pragmatic choice.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Frames it as a cost model: native saves compute only where startup or memory density dominates, and costs slower CI, harder debugging and library-compatibility risk. Would run a time-boxed pilot with measured cold start, RSS, p99 and build minutes, and compare against CDS/CRaC before committing. Sets a policy that a service must have native tests green and a documented hint ownership before it is allowed to ship native, and revisits the decision as library support matures.
Your pod is OOMKilled although heap usage looks fine. What do you check?
Answer

1. Model Answer

OOMKilled = the container exceeded its memory limit (kernel), not a Java OutOfMemoryError. Heap is only part: metaspace, thread stacks (200 threads × 1 MB), direct/NIO buffers (Netty, Kafka), code cache, GC structures, native libraries, mmap. With -Xmx equal to the limit there is no headroom. Fix: MaxRAMPercentage 70-75, enable Native Memory Tracking (-XX:NativeMemoryTracking=summary, jcmd <pid> VM.native_memory), cap -XX:MaxDirectMemorySize/-Xss, watch jvm.memory.used by area and process.rss in Prometheus, check for thread leaks (virtual-thread or executor growth) and glibc arena fragmentation (MALLOC_ARENA_MAX=2).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Distinguishes a kernel OOMKill from a Java OutOfMemoryError and can enumerate non-heap memory consumers and the tools to account for them.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'increase the memory limit' or 'there must be a heap leak', and is confused because heap graphs look fine. Treats OOMKilled and OutOfMemoryError as the same thing.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains OOMKilled means the container exceeded its limit and the kernel killed the process. Lists non-heap memory: metaspace, thread stacks (200 threads x 1 MB), direct/NIO buffers from Netty and Kafka, code cache, GC structures and native libraries.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes that -Xmx equal to the limit leaves no headroom, so set MaxRAMPercentage to 70-75. Diagnoses with -XX:NativeMemoryTracking=summary and jcmd <pid> VM.native_memory, caps -XX:MaxDirectMemorySize and -Xss, and watches jvm.memory.used by area plus process.rss in Prometheus. Checks for thread leaks from executors or virtual threads and glibc arena fragmentation (MALLOC_ARENA_MAX=2). Rule: limit minus heap must cover the measured non-heap total plus margin.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Builds a memory budget into the service template (heap %, metaspace, direct, stacks, headroom) and alerts on container working set versus limit, not only heap. Runs soak tests that watch RSS slope so native growth is caught before production. Records the runbook for OOMKilled triage and uses the postmortem to teach that dashboards showing only heap hide the real failure. Pushes platform defaults so teams do not hand-tune JVM flags per service.
How do you cut Spring Boot startup time for autoscaling pods without moving to native?
Answer

1. Model Answer

(1) AppCDS archive of app classes (Boot 3.3 documents extracting the jar and training run; JDK 19+ -XX:+AutoCreateSharedArchive); (2) trim auto-configs/starters and component scan; (3) lazy init for non-critical beans (spring.main.lazy-initialization) knowing first-hit cost; (4) avoid heavy work in @PostConstruct; (5) Spring 6.2 background bean initialisation (bootstrap = BACKGROUND on @Bean); (6) CRaC if your JDK supports it (3.2); (7) give the JVM enough CPU at start (CPU limit throttling is a big hidden cause); (8) readiness/startup probe tuned so pods are not killed during start. Measure with /actuator/startup.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows a prioritised, measurable toolbox for JVM startup time and the hidden CPU-throttling cause, rather than jumping to native.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Answers 'use GraalVM native' or 'add more replicas', or suggests removing a few dependencies without measuring. Has no way to say where the startup time goes.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists concrete levers: AppCDS (Boot 3.3 documents extracting the jar and a training run; -XX:+AutoCreateSharedArchive on JDK 19+), trimming starters and component scan, spring.main.lazy-initialization, and avoiding heavy @PostConstruct work.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds Spring 6.2 background bean initialisation (bootstrap = BACKGROUND on @Bean) and CRaC where the JDK supports it. Flags that CPU limit throttling at start is a big hidden cause, and that lazy init moves cost to the first request. Tunes startup and readiness probes so pods are not killed mid-start, and measures with /actuator/startup first. Rule: profile the startup steps, fix the top contributor, re-measure.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats startup as an autoscaling SLO: time-to-ready determines how much headroom is needed during a spike, so every second saved translates to lower baseline replicas. Keeps a startup-time budget per service tracked in CI from the /actuator/startup data, and rolls out CDS in the shared base image. Weighs lazy init against fail-fast behaviour in production. Decides native only if CDS and CPU fixes fall short.

22 · Boot 2 → 3 migration: Jakarta EE, Java 17, Hibernate 6

The largest breaking upgrade in Boot history; know the checklist.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: The largest breaking upgrade in Boot history: know the checklist.
  • Must-know: ★ javax to jakarta · ★ Java 17 baseline · ★ Hibernate 6 and removed properties
  • Top pitfall: third-party libs still on javax (springfox) break.
  • Version markers: Boot 3.0 (Nov 2022)

javax → jakarta ★ MUST KNOWjavax.servlet/persistence/validation/annotation/mail/transaction → jakarta.* (not javax.sql, javax.crypto, javax.net: those stay in the JDK)OpenRewrite recipes, check all libraries have Jakarta versions
