⚠ PitfallDo not jump straight from an old 2.x. Sequence: upgrade to the latest 2.7.x, fix all deprecation warnings (the 3.0 removals are the things deprecated in 2.x), then bump Java to 17 (still on 2.7), then migrate to Boot 3.0, then move forward one minor at a time (3.1, 3.2, ...).
🧑‍💻 From my experienceRetail-platform and Apple-scale services are long-lived; the same discipline applies to upgrades: performance baseline before the upgrade, regression comparison after (Hibernate 6 can change generated SQL and plans), and hermetic component tests (Postgres + MockServer + Kafka) as the safety net that catches serialization, SQL and security-behaviour changes.

Worksheet

What are the headline breaking changes when migrating Spring Boot 2.7 → 3.x?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Java 17 baseline; Jakarta EE 9/10 namespace (javax.* EE APIs → jakarta.*) which affects servlet, JPA, validation, JMS, mail; Spring Framework 6 (trailing slash, HttpHeaders, removed deprecated APIs); Spring Security 6 (no adapter, requestMatchers, CSRF deferred token); Hibernate 6 / Spring Data 3 (repository base changes, HQL strictness); auto-config registration file; Micrometer Tracing instead of Sleuth and renamed metrics; Batch 5; renamed properties; Apache HttpClient 5; Tomcat 10.1. Third-party libs must have Jakarta-compatible releases (springdoc 2, Springfox unsupported, Flyway, Lombok newer, Mockito/Byte Buddy for Java 17+).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Can enumerate the real breaking changes in a 2.7 to 3.x upgrade and the ecosystem coupling behind them, not just 'it is javax to jakarta'.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it needs Java 17 and the imports change from javax to jakarta' and stops there. Surprised by Spring Security or Hibernate changes during the upgrade.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists the headline changes: Java 17 baseline, Jakarta EE 9/10 namespace across servlet, JPA, validation, JMS and mail, Spring Framework 6, Spring Security 6 (no WebSecurityConfigurerAdapter, requestMatchers, deferred CSRF token), Hibernate 6 and Spring Data 3, Batch 5, Tomcat 10.1.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds the operational items: Micrometer Tracing replacing Sleuth, renamed metrics and properties, the new auto-configuration registration file, trailing-slash matching and Apache HttpClient 5. Stresses that third-party libraries need Jakarta-compatible releases (springdoc 2, Springfox unsupported, newer Lombok, Mockito/Byte Buddy for Java 17+) and that this usually decides the schedule. Rule: audit dependencies first, because they gate the upgrade.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats the upgrade as a program, not a version bump: produce a dependency compatibility matrix, stage 2.7.latest first, and list user-visible behaviour changes such as trailing slashes and renamed metrics that need dashboard changes. Estimates effort per service and sequences shared libraries before consumers. Records findings in a migration guide so later teams do not rediscover them.
How do you run the migration of a large multi-service estate safely?
Answer

1. Model Answer

(1) Inventory libraries (shared internal starters need Jakarta releases first: dual-publish 2.x and 3.x lines); (2) go to 2.7.latest + clean deprecations; (3) automate source changes (OpenRewrite) and review the diff; (4) pilot on one low-risk service, record the playbook; (5) use performance baselines + shadow/canary traffic since Hibernate 6 may change SQL/plans and Jackson/Spring behaviour might shift serialization; (6) contract tests for APIs and Kafka schemas; (7) migrate dashboards/alerts for renamed metrics and tracing; (8) roll out per service with feature-flagged rollback (previous image), and keep dependency BOM alignment (Spring Cloud release train compatible with the Boot line). Expect Hibernate sequence naming (hibernate_sequence → per-entity) issues in schema validation.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Plans a low-risk migration across many services: sequencing shared libraries, automation, canarying and rollback, rather than upgrading each repo ad hoc.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'upgrade each service one by one and fix compile errors'. Does not think about shared libraries, performance regressions or rollback.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes the steps: inventory libraries, move to 2.7.latest and clear deprecations, automate with OpenRewrite, pilot on a low-risk service, use contract tests, and roll out per service while keeping the previous image for rollback.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that shared internal starters must be dual-published for 2.x and 3.x lines first, and that Hibernate 6 may change SQL and plans, so performance baselines plus shadow or canary traffic are needed. Mentions migrating dashboards and alerts for renamed metrics and tracing, keeping the Spring Cloud release train aligned to the Boot line, and sequence naming (hibernate_sequence to per-entity) issues in schema validation. Rule: no service goes to 100% without a baseline comparison.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Runs it as a tracked programme with a dependency graph, a pilot playbook, a burn-down per team, and a deadline tied to end-of-support. Automates the diff with OpenRewrite recipes shared centrally and gates promotion on contract tests and SLO comparisons. Budgets for the dual-publish period of shared libraries and defines when the 2.x line is retired. Treats rollback as a requirement for each service and reviews regression incidents to refine the playbook.
javax to jakarta: which packages move and which stay? How do you find leftover usage?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Move: javax.servlet, persistence, validation, annotation (PostConstruct), transaction, mail, jms, websocket, ws.rs, inject (for CDI/JSR-330 depends on lib). Stay: JDK packages javax.sql, javax.crypto, javax.net.ssl, javax.security.auth, javax.xml.* (JAXB and javax.xml.bind were removed from the JDK in 11 and now jakarta.xml.bind). Find leftovers: grep imports, jdeps, compile errors, and runtime NoClassDefFoundError: javax/servlet/... from old third-party jars; the Eclipse Transformer or OpenRewrite can rewrite bytecode/sources; dependency tree check for libraries still on javax.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows exactly which javax.* packages are Jakarta EE and which are JDK, and how to hunt for stragglers at build time and runtime.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'replace all javax with jakarta' using find-and-replace, which breaks javax.sql, javax.crypto and javax.net.ssl.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Moved: javax.servlet, persistence, validation, annotation (@PostConstruct), transaction, mail, jms, websocket, ws.rs. Stay: JDK packages like javax.sql, javax.crypto, javax.net.ssl, javax.security.auth, javax.xml.*. Notes JAXB left the JDK in 11 and is now jakarta.xml.bind.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Finds leftovers with grep on imports, jdeps, compile errors and the runtime NoClassDefFoundError: javax/servlet/... from old third-party jars. Uses the Eclipse Transformer or OpenRewrite to rewrite sources or bytecode, and checks the dependency tree for libraries still on javax. Knows inject depends on the library in use. Rule: the dependency tree, not just your source, is where javax hides.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Adds a CI guard that fails builds when banned javax.* EE imports or old artifacts appear in the dependency tree, using an ArchUnit rule or a Maven enforcer ban. Runs the integration suite and a startup smoke test since some failures only surface at classload time. Keeps a registry of internal libraries with their Jakarta status, and uses it to prioritise vendor requests for updated releases.
What are the typical Hibernate 6 surprises after a Boot 3 upgrade and how do you detect them?
Answer

1. Model Answer

(1) Default sequence/ID naming: pre-6 used one hibernate_sequence (hibernate.id.new_generator_mappings); 6 uses <entity>_SEQ with allocationSize 50, so inserts fail or ids collide if the schema is not updated: set @SequenceGenerator explicitly or hibernate.id.db_structure_naming_strategy=legacy temporarily. (2) Stricter HQL: implicit joins, select new, missing aliases, positional parameters, ORDER BY rules. (3) @Type and custom type mappings reworked; JSON via @JdbcTypeCode(SqlTypes.JSON). (4) Different SQL (outer join ordering, pagination with fetch joins) that changes plans. (5) Dialect auto-detection (spring.jpa.database-platform often unnecessary now). Detect via startup schema validation (ddl-auto=validate), integration tests on the real DB engine, SQL logging diff of top queries, performance baseline comparison.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Anticipates the specific Hibernate 6 behaviour changes that cause data and performance incidents after an upgrade, and knows how to detect them before production.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Hibernate 6 is mostly compatible, so we just bump the version'. First learns about problems from production errors.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Names the main surprises: ID generation moves from one shared hibernate_sequence to <entity>_SEQ with allocationSize 50, stricter HQL (implicit joins, select new, aliases, positional parameters), reworked @Type mappings with @JdbcTypeCode(SqlTypes.JSON), and dialect auto-detection.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that sequence naming can cause failed inserts or colliding ids if the schema is not updated, fixed by explicit @SequenceGenerator or hibernate.id.db_structure_naming_strategy=legacy as a stopgap. Notes generated SQL can change outer join ordering and pagination with fetch joins, altering plans. Detects via ddl-auto=validate, integration tests on the real DB engine, a SQL log diff of top queries and performance baselines. Rule: test on the production engine, not H2.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats it as a regression-risk checklist: capture the top queries and their plans before, compare after, and block rollout on drift. Adds a canary stage with p99 and DB CPU comparison. Documents the explicit generator policy so new entities never rely on defaults, and enforces it in review or with an ArchUnit rule. Shares the findings across teams so each service does not repeat the same sequence incident.
The Spring Cloud / third-party BOM alignment: why do upgrades break at runtime with NoSuchMethodError and how do you prevent it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Mixed versions on the classpath (a library built against Spring 5 or an old Jackson/Netty) → binary incompatibility. Prevent: import the Boot BOM/dependency management only once, align the Spring Cloud release train with the Boot line, avoid overriding managed versions (ext[...]/properties) without a reason, run mvn dependency:tree/gradle dependencyInsight, use the Maven enforcer dependencyConvergence, and let CI fail on duplicates. Shared internal libraries should publish compatibility matrices.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands that NoSuchMethodError is a binary-compatibility symptom of mixed versions and knows build-time controls that prevent it.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it is a bug in the library, clear the Maven cache and retry' or 'just upgrade everything to latest'. Does not link it to mixed classpaths.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains that a library built against Spring 5 or an older Jackson or Netty is binary-incompatible with the newer classes at runtime. Prevention: import the Boot BOM once, align the Spring Cloud release train with the Boot line, and avoid overriding managed versions without cause.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Diagnoses with mvn dependency:tree or gradle dependencyInsight, and enforces with Maven enforcer dependencyConvergence, letting CI fail on duplicates. Notes that overriding via ext[...] or properties silently breaks the managed matrix, and that errors show only on the code path that touches the changed method. Rule: every version override needs a written reason and an expiry.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises dependency management in a company BOM or platform plugin so teams inherit a tested set, with automated PRs (Renovate) validating each bump. Requires shared internal libraries to publish compatibility matrices and be tested against supported Boot lines. Tracks runtime linkage errors as a metric to prioritise cleanup and teaches teams to read dependency trees as part of the upgrade review.
