Worksheet

What are the headline changes in Spring Framework 7 and Spring Boot 4? (verify in release notes)
Answer

1. Model Answer

Framework 7: Jakarta EE 11 baseline; Java 17+ (best on 21/25); JSpecify annotations for null-safety across APIs (replacing Spring’s own @Nullable semantics, better Kotlin/IDE support); built-in API versioning for MVC/WebFlux; @Retryable/@ConcurrencyLimit and @EnableResilientMethods moved into core; HTTP interface client registry (@ImportHttpServices); Jackson 3 support; removal of long-deprecated APIs; RestTemplate on a deprecation path. Boot 4: built on Framework 7, modular auto-configuration (technology-specific modules and starters, smaller classpaths), Jackson 3 as the default JSON library (tools.jackson packages), updated test support, removal of deprecated features. Details/names: verify before quoting.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows the headline direction of Spring Framework 7 and Boot 4 and, crucially, is honest about which details need verification.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Spring 7 is just a newer version with Java 21' or recites invented feature names with confidence. Cannot name a single concrete change.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists Framework 7 items: Jakarta EE 11, Java 17+ baseline, JSpecify null-safety, built-in API versioning, core @Retryable and @ConcurrencyLimit, HTTP interface client registry (@ImportHttpServices), Jackson 3 support. Boot 4: modular auto-configuration, Jackson 3 default (tools.jackson), updated test support.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Links each change to a practical effect: smaller classpaths from modular starters, tighter nullability tooling, fewer third-party dependencies for retry. Flags the removal of long-deprecated APIs and the deprecation path for RestTemplate. Explicitly says names and defaults must be verified in the release notes before quoting, and checks upgrade guides before planning. Rule: do not commit to a migration scope from memory.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Turns it into an adoption plan: a spike on a pilot service, a risk list (Jackson 3 packages, removed APIs, third-party compatibility), and a timeline relative to support windows for the current line. Uses the breaking changes to decide which in-house libraries to modernise first. Keeps a written decision record on what is adopted now versus deferred, and shares release-note summaries rather than letting folklore spread.
Why does JSpecify null-safety matter, and how would you adopt it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

JSpecify is a cross-tool standard (@NullMarked, @Nullable) so nullability is part of the API contract: Kotlin sees precise types, IntelliJ and NullAway (Error Prone) can flag NPE risks at build time, and Spring’s own APIs declare it. Adopt: add the jspecify dependency, mark packages @NullMarked (via package-info.java) so everything is non-null by default and only annotate the exceptions with @Nullable, enable NullAway in the build in warn mode first, then error mode per module. Benefit is fewer production NPEs and clearer public APIs; cost is annotation churn and generics/array subtleties (@Nullable goes on the type use).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands why null-safety as an API contract matters and can lay out an incremental adoption plan that does not stall delivery.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it is just annotations for nulls, we have Optional anyway'. Does not know the difference between a contract and a convention.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains JSpecify as a cross-tool standard (@NullMarked, @Nullable) making nullability part of the contract, which Kotlin, IntelliJ and NullAway can use. Mentions marking packages with @NullMarked in package-info.java so everything is non-null by default.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Describes adoption: add the jspecify dependency, annotate packages, then annotate only exceptions with @Nullable; run NullAway in warn mode first and move to error mode module by module. Notes cost: annotation churn and subtleties with generics and arrays, since @Nullable is a type-use annotation. Benefit is fewer production NPEs and clearer public APIs. Rule: start at the public API boundary and shared libraries, where the contract has the most value.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Rolls it out as a measured programme: baseline NPE incidents, enable warnings on all modules, ratchet to errors with a per-module allowlist so new code is protected without a big-bang change. Enforces through the build (NullAway in CI) rather than review comments. Prioritises shared client libraries and DTO modules, mentors teams on replacing defensive null checks with types, and tracks NPE exceptions in production as the outcome metric.
Compare Spring 7 built-in resilience annotations with Spring Retry and Resilience4j. When would you still pick Resilience4j?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Core @Retryable/@ConcurrencyLimit cover simple retry (with backoff, jitter, reactive-aware) and bulkhead concurrency limits with no extra dependency, consistent with Spring’s proxy model; Spring Retry the library becomes redundant for new code. Resilience4j still offers circuit breaker (stateful, sliding windows, slow-call rate), rate limiter, time limiter, rich metrics and event streams, and per-instance config. Pick R4J when you need breakers/rate limits and operational metrics; use core annotations for simple retry/limit. Either way the proxy limitations (self-invocation) and aspect ordering apply. (verify exact feature set in release notes)

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Compares Spring 7 core resilience support with Spring Retry and Resilience4j on what each actually provides, and picks by operational need.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Resilience4j and Spring Retry are the same thing' or 'Spring 7 replaces all of it', and picks by habit.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains core @Retryable and @ConcurrencyLimit cover simple retry with backoff and jitter and a concurrency bulkhead with no extra dependency. Spring Retry becomes redundant for new code, while Resilience4j still offers circuit breaker, rate limiter, time limiter and metrics.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Breaks it down: use R4J for breakers with sliding windows and slow-call rate, rate limiting, event streams and per-instance config; use core annotations for simple retry and limits. Notes both are proxy-based, so self-invocation and aspect ordering still apply (retry vs transaction vs breaker order matters). Says to verify the exact feature set in release notes. Rule: if you need to see and tune failure state operationally, choose R4J.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a platform policy: one resilience approach per concern, shared defaults for timeouts and backoff, and a ban on unbounded retries, since retry storms amplify outages. Requires metrics for breaker state and retry counts on dashboards and SLO alerts. Plans migration of legacy Spring Retry gradually rather than rewriting, and tests aspect ordering with a fault-injection test so changes in annotation behaviour do not hide.
Explain the HTTP service clients registry and API versioning additions at a conceptual level.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Registry: today each @HttpExchange interface needs manual HttpServiceProxyFactory wiring. Spring 7 lets you declare groups (@ImportHttpServices or a registrar) so client config (base URL, timeouts, auth, SSL bundle) is applied per group and interface beans are registered automatically; Boot 4 adds properties to configure groups. API versioning: the version attribute on mappings plus a configurable resolver (path segment, header, query param, media-type parameter), version parser and deprecation handler, replacing hand-rolled /v1 controllers and custom RequestConditions. Treat exact property and annotation names as “verify in release notes”.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Conceptual grasp of the declarative HTTP client registry and framework-level API versioning, with honesty about naming details.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'we already have RestTemplate/Feign, so this is just another client' and has never used @HttpExchange.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains that today each @HttpExchange interface needs manual HttpServiceProxyFactory wiring, and the registry groups interfaces (@ImportHttpServices or a registrar) so base URL, timeouts, auth and SSL bundle apply per group. Versioning adds a version attribute on mappings with a configurable resolver.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that resolvers can read a path segment, header, query param or media-type parameter, with a version parser and deprecation handler replacing hand-rolled /v1 controllers and custom RequestConditions. Boot 4 adds properties to configure groups. Says to verify property and annotation names in release notes. Rule: centralise client config per downstream, and version through one mechanism.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Frames both as standardisation wins: one client config per downstream with timeouts, auth and observability applied uniformly, and one versioning scheme instead of per-team conventions. Plans migration from RestTemplate and Feign incrementally, with ArchUnit rules banning unconfigured clients. Connects versioning to deprecation policy and per-client usage metrics so old versions can actually be removed.
How would you plan a Jackson 2 → Jackson 3 migration inside a Boot 4 upgrade?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Jackson 3 moves to tools.jackson packages (annotations stay in com.fasterxml.jackson.annotation), makes ObjectMapper immutable (use JsonMapper.builder()), changes defaults and uses unchecked exceptions (verify specifics). Plan: inventory custom ObjectMapper beans, modules and serializers; use Boot 4’s compatibility options (Jackson 2 support is deprecated/optional, verify), migrate shared libraries first, add golden-file serialization tests for every public DTO and Kafka message (field order, null handling, date formats, unknown properties) before and after, and canary. Highest risk: Kafka/JSON payloads read by other teams, so keep a compatibility test with real captured messages.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Plans a library-wide serialization migration around the risk that matters, which is wire-compatibility with other teams' consumers.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'change the imports to tools.jackson and run the tests'. Ignores that payloads read by other services could change.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Knows Jackson 3 moves to tools.jackson packages (annotations stay in com.fasterxml.jackson.annotation), makes ObjectMapper immutable (use JsonMapper.builder()), changes defaults and uses unchecked exceptions, and says to verify specifics.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Plans: inventory custom ObjectMapper beans, modules and serializers, use Boot 4's compatibility options if they exist (verify), migrate shared libraries first, and add golden-file serialization tests for every public DTO and Kafka message covering field order, null handling, date formats and unknown properties. Canary the rollout. Rule: the highest risk is JSON read by other teams, so keep tests with real captured messages.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats serialized formats as public contracts: a registry of all events and APIs with owners, consumer-driven contract tests, and a canary comparing produced payload bytes before and after. Staggers rollout so producers change only after consumers tolerate both shapes. Automates the code migration with OpenRewrite where available, and decides a deadline for removing Jackson 2 compatibility so dual support does not become permanent.

24 · Performance troubleshooting: startup, memory leaks, HikariCP, thread dumps

A repeatable method: measure, hypothesise, change one thing, re-measure.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: A repeatable method: measure, hypothesise, change one thing, re-measure.
  • Must-know: ★ thread dump / heap dump reading · ★ HikariCP pool sizing · ★ startup analysis
  • Top pitfall: changing several knobs at once.
  • Version markers: Boot 2.x Hikari default; JFR since JDK 11
SymptomFirst toolWhat to look for
Slow startup--debug, /actuator/startup (BufferingApplicationStartup, Boot 2.4)Slow beans, scanning, schema init, remote calls in @PostConstruct
Heap grows, GC thrashingjcmd <pid> GC.heap_dump file.hprof, -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError, Eclipse MATDominator tree, “Leak suspects”, GC roots of the biggest retained set
Requests hang, low CPUjcmd <pid> Thread.print x3 (/actuator/threaddump)Threads WAITING on HikariPool.getConnection, locks, sockets
High CPUProfiler (YourKit/async-profiler/JFR)Hot methods, regex, serialization, logging
DB slowpg_stat_statements/AWR, Hibernate statisticsN+1, missing index, full scans, lock waits
Latency spikesGC logs (-Xlog:gc*), jvm.gc.pauseFull GCs, safepoints, CPU throttling in containers

HikariCP essentials ★ MUST KNOW

PropertyDefaultGuidance
maximum-pool-size10Small. Start ~ (cores × 2) + spindles per DB, then divide by pod count; measure
connection-timeout30 sLower (2-5 s) so overload fails fast instead of piling threads
max-lifetime30 minBelow DB/LB idle or max connection age
minimum-idle= maxLeave default (fixed-size pool recommended)
leak-detection-threshold0 (off)Set e.g. 20 s in non-prod/while investigating
🔑 Key ideaPool size is a DB-side capacity decision, not “more threads need more connections”. A pool of 30 per pod × 20 pods = 600 connections: Postgres max_connections (typically 100-500) is exhausted and DB throughput drops from context switching. Use a smaller pool, a pooler (PgBouncer/RDS Proxy), and shorter transactions.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe resume has real stories for this section: the heap-dump memory-leak fix at California Student Aid Commission, AWR-based SQL tuning (45% faster), JMeter + YourKit tuning (30%), and the Attestation Authority 27 ms latency and 5x load tuning. Structure each as: symptom, measurement tool, root cause, fix, measured result, and the regression guard (baseline test) you added.

Worksheet

How do you find a memory leak in a Spring Boot service?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Confirm it is a leak: old-gen usage after full GCs trends up (jvm.memory.used{area=heap}, GC logs) rather than just a big working set. Capture a heap dump (jcmd <pid> GC.heap_dump, or /actuator/heapdump secured; HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError in prod), compare two dumps over time, open in Eclipse MAT: Leak Suspects, Dominator Tree (largest retained size), then Path to GC Roots excluding weak references to see what holds it (static collection, ThreadLocal, listener, cache). Fix the retention (bound/evict the cache, clear ThreadLocals, close resources), redeploy and watch the sawtooth flatten. Guard with a soak test and an alert on post-GC old-gen slope.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Follows an evidence-based leak investigation from confirming it is a leak through heap dump analysis to a verified fix, rather than guessing at GC tuning.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'increase the heap' or 'restart the pod every night', and cannot distinguish a large working set from a leak. Has never opened a heap dump.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Confirms a leak by old-gen usage after full GCs trending up. Takes a heap dump with jcmd <pid> GC.heap_dump or /actuator/heapdump, opens it in Eclipse MAT, and uses Leak Suspects and the Dominator Tree to find the largest retained size.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds comparing two dumps over time, and using Path to GC Roots excluding weak references to find what holds objects: a static collection, ThreadLocal, listener or cache. Secures the heapdump endpoint and sets HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError in production. Fixes by bounding or evicting the cache, clearing ThreadLocals or closing resources, then watches the sawtooth flatten. Rule: no fix is done until the post-GC old-gen slope is flat in a soak test.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Puts guardrails in place: an alert on post-GC old-gen slope, a soak test in the pipeline, and a runbook for capturing dumps safely, including PII handling since dumps contain data. Reviews common sources (unbounded caches, ThreadLocal in pooled threads, metric tags) in code review checklists. Uses incidents to standardise JVM flags and diagnostics across services, and trains the team on MAT so it does not depend on one expert.
Requests time out and logs show “HikariPool-1 - Connection is not available, request timed out after 30000ms”. What do you do?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Cause is one of: leaked/never-returned connections (missing close, a transaction stuck waiting on a remote call, @Transactional wrapping HTTP calls, open-in-view), slow queries holding connections too long, pool too small for real concurrency, or DB-side limit/locks. Steps: metrics hikaricp.connections.active/pending/acquire; thread dump to see who holds connections (threads in socketRead inside a tx?); leak-detection-threshold=20000 logs the stack of the acquiring code; DB side (pg_stat_activity state idle in transaction). Fixes: shorten transactions (move remote calls out), add timeouts (@Transactional(timeout), JDBC socketTimeout/statement timeout), fix leaks, set OSIV=false, and only then adjust pool size within DB limits.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Diagnoses connection pool exhaustion by distinguishing a leak, long-held connections, undersized pool and DB-side causes, using specific signals before touching the pool size.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'increase maximum-pool-size' immediately. Treats the timeout as a pool configuration problem only.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists the possible causes: leaked connections, transactions waiting on remote calls (@Transactional wrapping HTTP calls), open-in-view, slow queries, an undersized pool, DB limits or locks. Checks hikaricp.connections.active/pending/acquire metrics and takes a thread dump.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Uses leak-detection-threshold=20000 to log the acquiring stack, looks for threads in socketRead inside a transaction, and inspects pg_stat_activity for idle in transaction. Fixes by shortening transactions, moving remote calls out, adding @Transactional(timeout), JDBC socket and statement timeouts, setting OSIV to false, and only then adjusting the pool within DB limits. Rule: bigger pool is the last resort, not the first.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises defaults in the platform starter: leak detection on in non-prod, OSIV off, statement timeouts, and dashboards for pending threads and acquire time with alerts tied to the SLO. Adds a code-review rule against remote calls inside transactions, with an ArchUnit check where feasible. Documents the triage runbook and writes the postmortem to show that pool exhaustion is a symptom of held connections, not a sizing failure.
How do you size HikariCP, and why is a bigger pool often slower?
Answer

1. Model Answer

The DB can run only about cores × 2 queries in parallel effectively; beyond that connections just add context switching, lock contention and memory (Postgres forks a process per connection). Start with a small pool (10-20 per pod), measure throughput/latency while increasing; compute the fleet total: pods × pool ≤ ~70% of DB max_connections (leave room for admin, migrations, replicas). For many pods use PgBouncer (transaction mode; mind prepared statements/session state) or RDS Proxy. Lower connection-timeout so overload surfaces fast; set max-lifetime below infrastructure timeouts. With virtual threads the pool becomes the explicit concurrency limiter, so size it deliberately and let excess requests wait/fail fast.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands why DB throughput saturates at low connection counts, and sizes pools at fleet level with proxies and timeouts in mind.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'more connections means more throughput, so set the pool to 100 or 200'. Has no explanation for why that can slow things down.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains the DB can run only about cores x 2 queries effectively in parallel; more connections add context switching, lock contention and memory, and Postgres forks a process per connection. Starts with a pool of 10-20 per pod and measures while increasing.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Computes the fleet total: pods x pool <= ~70% of max_connections, leaving room for admin, migrations and replicas. Uses PgBouncer in transaction mode (mind prepared statements and session state) or RDS Proxy for many pods. Lowers connection-timeout so overload surfaces fast and sets max-lifetime below infrastructure timeouts. Rule: with virtual threads the pool is the explicit limiter, so size it deliberately and fail fast.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Owns a connection budget per database as a shared resource: each service is allocated a quota, autoscaling max is checked against it, and the platform enforces it in deploy review. Reviews it when HPA limits change, and monitors DB-side active versus idle connections. Evaluates proxy options on cost and failure modes (session state, prepared statements) and communicates that 'bigger pool' is a recurring anti-pattern.
Describe how you would read a thread dump to diagnose a hung service.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Take several dumps (jcmd Thread.print, kill -3, /actuator/threaddump). Group threads by state and top frame: many http-nio-*-exec threads in HikariPool.getConnection = connection starvation; in SocketInputStream.read/NioSocketImpl = slow downstream with missing timeout; BLOCKED on the same monitor = lock contention (find owner via “locked <0x…>”); jstack prints “Found one Java-level deadlock” with the cycle; RUNNABLE threads in the same hot method = CPU bound. Compare dumps: threads in the same place across dumps are stuck, not just busy. Use fastThread/IntelliJ analysers, then confirm with a profiler or JFR. Virtual threads need jcmd Thread.dump_to_file -format=json to see them.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Reads thread dumps systematically, grouping by state and top frame and comparing multiple dumps, to separate starvation, blocking, deadlock and CPU-bound situations.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'take a thread dump and look for errors' or glances at one dump and picks a random thread. Cannot map frames to causes.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Takes several dumps (jcmd Thread.print, kill -3, /actuator/threaddump) and groups threads by state and top frame. Many http-nio-*-exec threads in HikariPool.getConnection mean connection starvation; in SocketInputStream.read mean a slow downstream without timeouts.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Finds lock contention via BLOCKED threads and the owner shown as locked <0x...>, and reads jstack's 'Found one Java-level deadlock' cycle. Compares dumps: threads at the same place across dumps are stuck, not busy. Confirms with a profiler or JFR, and uses jcmd Thread.dump_to_file -format=json for virtual threads. Rule: one dump is a snapshot, three dumps are evidence.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Automates capture so dumps are taken on alert before the pod is restarted, through a sidecar or runbook script, and stored for analysis. Adds metrics that point at the cause earlier, such as pending connections and executor queue depth. Teaches the pattern of reading dumps in postmortems, and enforces timeouts on every outbound call so a hung service is the exception rather than the norm.
After a deploy, memory climbs until OOM every ~6 hours. You have a heap dump with 1.2 GB retained by a ConcurrentHashMap. How do you proceed?
Answer

1. Model Answer

In MAT, open the map in the Dominator Tree: which class owns it (a Spring bean field? a static? an @Cacheable-backed manual map?), what are keys (unique per request: session ids, timestamps, uncapped user input) and values. “Path to GC roots” shows the retaining bean. Correlate with the diff in the last deploy (new feature storing per-request state in a singleton; a metrics tag with unbounded values creating a Meter per URL; an in-memory idempotency map). Fix: bounded cache (Caffeine maximumSize/expireAfterWrite), move state to Redis/DB, or remove the retention; add a metric on the map size, a soak test in CI/staging, and consider -XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError so the pod restarts cleanly rather than limping.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Walks from a heap dump with a large ConcurrentHashMap to the owning bean, the key cardinality problem and a bounded fix, tying the cause to the last deploy.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'the map is too big so increase memory' or 'replace ConcurrentHashMap'. Does not ask who owns it or what its keys are.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: In MAT opens the map in the Dominator Tree to see which class owns it (a Spring bean field, a static, a manual cache) and what the keys and values are. Uses Path to GC Roots to find the retaining bean and compares with the diff in the last deploy.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Names likely culprits: per-request state stored in a singleton, a metrics tag with unbounded values creating a Meter per URL, or an in-memory idempotency map with session ids or timestamps as keys. Fixes with a bounded Caffeine cache (maximumSize, expireAfterWrite), moving state to Redis or the DB, or removing retention. Adds a metric on map size and a soak test, and -XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError so the pod restarts cleanly. Rule: any in-process map keyed by input needs a bound and a metric.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Prevents recurrence systematically: a code review rule against unbounded in-memory state in singletons, a cardinality guard on Micrometer tags, and a staging soak test that fails on heap slope. Correlates deploys with memory trends so regressions are caught within hours rather than at the 6-hour OOM. Makes the postmortem an artefact for the team and keeps a shared Caffeine configuration so caches have consistent bounds.

25 · System-design scenarios for Spring services

Idempotent endpoints, pagination, uploads, multi-tenancy, degradation: the L5 whiteboard set.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Idempotent endpoints, pagination, uploads, multi-tenancy and degradation: the L5 whiteboard set.
  • Must-know: ★ Idempotency-Key · ★ keyset pagination · ★ graceful degradation
  • Top pitfall: designing without stating consistency and failure modes.
  • Version markers: core design patterns
Idempotent POSTIdempotency-Key header + unique-key table; store status/response; 409 while in-flight; TTL 24 h
PaginationOffset for small admin lists; keyset/cursor (Window / ScrollPosition Spring Data 3.1) for large/changing data
File uploadMultipart limits; stream to object store; pre-signed URLs for big files; async scan; resumable
Multi-tenantTenant from JWT claim → TenantContext → discriminator column (@TenantId), schema, or DB per tenant
Graceful degradationCritical vs optional deps, per-branch timeouts/fallbacks, degraded flag, bulkheads
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThe retail platform’s optimistic locking, KMS-based field-level encryption at the gateway, and fork-and-join aggregation with graceful degradation are all ready-made “design” answers; the HR/payroll/Kafka systems supply the idempotency, outbox, batch and event-ordering stories.

Worksheet

Design an idempotent POST /payments endpoint in a Spring Boot service.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Client sends Idempotency-Key (UUID per logical operation). Server: (1) in a short REQUIRES_NEW transaction insert (tenant,key,request_hash,IN_PROGRESS) with a unique constraint; (2) if the insert conflicts: same hash and DONE → return the stored response (same status/body); IN_PROGRESS → 409 (or wait briefly); different hash → 422 (key reuse). (3) Execute the business operation in its own transaction; also make the downstream call idempotent by forwarding the key. (4) Store response and mark DONE; on a technical failure before side effects delete/expire the reservation, after unknown outcome keep IN_PROGRESS with a timeout and reconcile. TTL cleanup job (ShedLock). Natural idempotency (PUT, unique business key) is preferable where possible. Test concurrent duplicates (two threads) and crash between steps.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designs idempotency with a durable reservation, race handling and defined behaviour for in-progress, key-reuse and crash cases, rather than a naive 'check then insert'.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'store the request id in a set and reject duplicates' or checks existsById before processing. Ignores concurrent duplicates and what happens when the service crashes.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes an Idempotency-Key header, a short REQUIRES_NEW insert of (tenant, key, request_hash, IN_PROGRESS) with a unique constraint, and returning the stored response when the key is DONE with the same hash.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Specifies the conflict paths: IN_PROGRESS gives 409 or a brief wait, a different hash gives 422 for key reuse. Executes the business operation in its own transaction and forwards the key downstream. Stores the response and marks DONE; after an unknown outcome keeps IN_PROGRESS with a timeout and reconciles. Adds a TTL cleanup job guarded by ShedLock. Rule: prefer natural idempotency (PUT, unique business key), and test two concurrent duplicates and a crash between steps.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats it as a reusable platform capability rather than per-endpoint code: a shared library or filter with consistent semantics, metrics on replays, conflicts and stuck IN_PROGRESS records, and documented client guidance on key generation and retries. Aligns with the payment provider's idempotency so the chain is end-to-end. Includes a reconciliation process and alerts for stuck reservations, and mentors teams to prove it with a concurrency test in CI.
Offset vs keyset (cursor) pagination: when and how in Spring Data?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Offset (Pageable, LIMIT x OFFSET n) is simple, supports page numbers and totals, but cost grows with offset (DB scans and discards n rows), results shift if rows are inserted (duplicates/gaps), and Page triggers an extra count(*) (use Slice to skip). Keyset: WHERE (created_at,id) < (:ts,:id) ORDER BY ... LIMIT n is O(page) with an index, stable under writes, but no random page jump. Spring Data 3.1 adds ScrollPosition/Window (repo.findFirst20ByStatusOrderByIdAsc(status, KeysetScrollPosition.of(...)), or OffsetScrollPosition) producing the next position. API: return an opaque cursor, a stable total order (unique tie-breaker), and a max limit.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Chooses between offset and keyset pagination on cost, consistency under writes and API shape, and knows the Spring Data 3.1 scroll API.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'use Pageable' for everything. Does not know the cost of large offsets or that Page triggers a count query.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains offset (LIMIT x OFFSET n) is simple and supports page numbers and totals, but cost grows with offset and results shift when rows are inserted. Notes Page runs an extra count(*), so use Slice to skip it. Keyset uses WHERE (created_at,id) < (:ts,:id) and is O(page) with an index.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds that keyset is stable under writes but lacks random page jump, and shows Spring Data 3.1 ScrollPosition and Window (KeysetScrollPosition.of(...) or OffsetScrollPosition). API design: return an opaque cursor, require a stable total order with a unique tie-breaker, and cap the max limit. Rule: use keyset for large or feed-like data, offset for small admin lists needing page numbers.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets an API standard: cursor pagination by default for public and high-volume endpoints, with a shared cursor encoding, max page size and an index-backed sort check in review. Verifies with EXPLAIN on production-sized data and monitors slow queries by offset depth. Handles migration of existing clients that depend on page numbers with a deprecation window, and documents the trade-off for product teams who want total counts.
Design a file upload endpoint for 2 GB files. What do you configure in Spring and what would you avoid?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Avoid proxying big files through the app (memory, thread/connection hold, pod timeouts). Preferred: the API issues a pre-signed URL (S3 multipart or resumable), the client uploads directly, an event notifies the service which validates (size, type by magic bytes, antivirus scan async) and records metadata; checksum verification (SHA-256). If it must pass through: MultipartFile with spring.servlet.multipart.max-file-size/max-request-size, stream with getInputStream() to storage, never getBytes(); set temp location (location/file-size-threshold), timeouts on proxies/ingress (proxy-body-size), and authorisation before reading the body. Return 202 and a status resource for long processing.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designs large file ingest that keeps bytes off the application tier, with validation, integrity and async processing, and knows the Spring and proxy limits if it must pass through.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'raise spring.servlet.multipart.max-file-size to 2GB and use MultipartFile'. Does not consider memory, timeouts or the ingress limit.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Proposes a pre-signed URL (S3 multipart or resumable upload) so the client uploads directly, with an event notifying the service. If proxying is unavoidable, configures max-file-size, max-request-size, temp location and file-size-threshold, and streams with getInputStream().
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Avoids getBytes(), sets proxy-body-size and timeouts on the ingress, and authorises before reading the body. Validates asynchronously: size, type by magic bytes, antivirus scan and SHA-256 checksum. Returns 202 with a status resource for long processing. Rule: never hold a request thread, connection or heap for the whole file.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises an upload service or pattern across teams, including quarantine buckets until scanned, lifecycle rules for abandoned multipart uploads, and per-tenant quotas. Tracks upload success rate, abandoned parts and scan latency as SLIs. Considers cost of egress and storage, security review of pre-signed URL scope and expiry, and documents the pattern so teams stop sending big payloads through the API.
Design multi-tenancy for a Spring Boot SaaS API: options, trade-offs and the failure modes.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Options: (1) shared schema + tenant_id column (Hibernate 6 @TenantId + CurrentTenantIdentifierResolver; or Postgres row-level security with SET app.tenant per connection): cheapest, easiest ops, weakest isolation, noisy neighbours, must never forget the filter; (2) schema per tenant (MultiTenantConnectionProvider switching schema): good isolation, migrations ×N; (3) DB per tenant (AbstractRoutingDataSource): strongest isolation, per-tenant backup/encryption, highest cost and pool count. Tenant resolution: from a verified JWT claim (never a client-supplied header alone), stored in a TenantContext. Failure modes: tenant context lost in @Async/Kafka listeners/schedulers (propagate with TaskDecorator and message headers), cache keys without tenant, pooled connection retaining the previous tenant’s schema/session variable (reset on release), migration drift, hot tenants (rate limit per tenant). Test with a two-tenant integration test that must not leak.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Compares multi-tenancy isolation models by cost, isolation and operational burden, and anticipates the leak paths such as lost tenant context and pooled connection state.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add a tenant_id column and filter in every query'. Has not considered forgotten filters, async context or stronger isolation needs.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists the three options: shared schema with a tenant column (Hibernate 6 @TenantId with CurrentTenantIdentifierResolver, or Postgres row-level security), schema per tenant (MultiTenantConnectionProvider), and DB per tenant (AbstractRoutingDataSource), with their cost versus isolation trade.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Resolves the tenant from a verified JWT claim, never a client header alone, held in a TenantContext. Names failure modes: context lost in @Async, Kafka listeners and schedulers (propagate with TaskDecorator and message headers), cache keys without tenant, pooled connections retaining the previous schema or session variable, migration drift, and hot tenants. Verifies with a two-tenant integration test that must not leak. Rule: isolation must be enforced below the application code where possible.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Frames it as a product and risk decision: tenant tiers (shared for small, dedicated for regulated or large) with a migration path between them. Adds defence in depth with RLS plus app filtering, automated leak tests in CI, per-tenant rate limits and metrics, and an ArchUnit rule against queries that bypass the tenant mechanism. Plans for schema migration tooling at N tenants and per-tenant backup, restore and deletion requirements.
Design a read-heavy aggregation endpoint that calls several services, must stay under a 500 ms p99, and degrade gracefully. What do you build?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Parallel fan-out on a bounded executor/virtual threads; latency budget split (e.g. 300 ms per branch hard timeout, total deadline 450 ms) with orTimeout; classification critical/optional; per-dependency circuit breaker + bulkhead; caching layer (Caffeine with refreshAfterWrite for slow-moving data); stale-while-error fallbacks; partial response with degraded list and Cache-Control: no-store on degraded outputs so a gateway does not cache them; request hedging only for idempotent reads and with budgets; per-branch metrics and an SLO alert; load shedding (429/503 with Retry-After) when queues fill. Verification: MockServer fault injection and a load test with one dependency down, asserting p99 and error rate. This mirrors the fork-and-join aggregation with graceful degradation.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designs a latency-budgeted aggregation with explicit critical versus optional dependencies, bounded concurrency and tested degradation, rather than just parallel calls.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'call the services in parallel with CompletableFuture and add a cache'. No timeouts, no fallback and no plan for a failing dependency.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes parallel fan-out on a bounded executor or virtual threads with per-branch timeouts, a total deadline (e.g. 300 ms per branch, 450 ms overall) using orTimeout, and Caffeine caching with refreshAfterWrite for slow-moving data.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Classifies dependencies as critical or optional, uses per-dependency circuit breakers and bulkheads, and returns a partial response with a degraded list. Sets Cache-Control: no-store on degraded output so gateways do not cache it. Hedges only idempotent reads within budget, sheds load with 429/503 and Retry-After when queues fill, and emits per-branch metrics with an SLO alert. Verifies with MockServer fault injection and a load test with one dependency down asserting p99 and error rate. Rule: the budget is allocated, not hoped for.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats the p99 target as an SLO with an error budget, and derives per-dependency latency and availability contracts from it so downstream owners know what they must meet. Standardises the fan-out pattern in a shared library and uses chaos or game days to prove degradation works. Reviews cost of hedging and cache refresh on downstream load, and decides with product which fields may be omitted without harm.
How do you evolve a REST API without breaking clients (versioning, deprecation, compatibility)?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Additive changes only within a version (new optional fields; clients ignore unknown fields: Boot’s Jackson sets FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES=false), never change meaning or remove fields; new behaviour behind a new version (URI /v2 or Spring 7 versioning attribute) or feature flags; announce with Deprecation/Sunset headers and usage metrics per version/client so you know when it is safe to remove; consumer-driven contract tests; OpenAPI diff in CI (openapi-diff) to catch breaking changes; expand/contract for DB changes (add column, dual write, backfill, switch reads, drop later). Events (Kafka) follow schema-compatibility rules (backward/forward) with a registry.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Evolves an API through additive compatibility, observable deprecation and automated breaking-change detection, extending the same discipline to databases and events.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'create /v2 whenever we change something' or just changes the response and tells clients. No notion of usage tracking or compatibility rules.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains additive-only changes within a version (new optional fields, clients ignoring unknown fields, with Boot's Jackson FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES=false), never changing meaning or removing fields, and introducing new behaviour through a new version (URI /v2 or Spring 7 versioning).
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds Deprecation and Sunset headers with usage metrics per version and client to know when removal is safe, consumer-driven contract tests, and an OpenAPI diff in CI (openapi-diff) to catch breaks. Applies expand/contract for DB changes (add column, dual write, backfill, switch reads, drop later) and schema-compatibility rules with a registry for Kafka events. Rule: you can only remove what you can prove nobody calls.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Publishes an API lifecycle policy: support windows, deprecation notice period, version limits and a review gate for breaking changes with owners. Automates compatibility checks in the pipeline for HTTP and event schemas, and gives consumers dashboards of their own deprecated usage. Balances the cost of maintaining multiple versions against client migration effort, and escalates to product when a stuck consumer blocks a sunset.

26 · REST API design for microservices

Resources, verbs, status codes, pagination, versioning, conditional requests, idempotency and one error contract.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Resources, verbs, status codes, pagination, versioning, conditional requests, idempotency, one error contract.
  • Must-know: ★ status code choices · ★ safe vs idempotent methods · ★ ETag/If-Match with optimistic lock
  • Top pitfall: POST retried without an idempotency key creates duplicates.
  • Version markers: ProblemDetail Spring 6.0; Window scroll Data 3.1; API versioning Framework 7 (verify)

For a REST microservice the controller is the contract. Model resources as nouns, use HTTP verbs with their real semantics, return the right status code and one error shape, and make every write safe to retry.

MethodSemanticsSafeIdempotentTypical successSince
GET ★ MUST KNOWread a representationyesyes200 (304 if conditional)HTTP/1.0
POST ★ MUST KNOWcreate / run an action (server picks the id)nono (needs Idempotency-Key)201 + Location, or 202HTTP/1.0
PUT ★ MUST KNOWreplace the whole resource at a known URInoyes200 / 204 (201 if created)HTTP/1.1
PATCHpartial updatenonot guaranteed200 / 204RFC 5789 (2010)
DELETEremovenoyes (2nd call 404 or 204 is fine)204HTTP/1.1
🔑 KeySafe = no state change (GET/HEAD/OPTIONS). Idempotent = N identical requests leave the same server state as one (GET, PUT, DELETE, plus the safe ones). POST is neither, so retries by clients, gateways or Kafka-driven callers can double-create unless you add an idempotency key.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceRetail-platform-style update endpoints use optimistic locking (no pessimistic locks). Surfacing the row version as an ETag and requiring If-Match on PUT/PATCH is the HTTP-native way to expose that: a stale write returns 412 (or 409) instead of silently overwriting. Q-answers below show the code.

Worksheet

Which status code would you return for: create, async accepted, update with no body, validation failure, missing auth, forbidden, version conflict, rate limit, downstream down?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Create: 201 Created with Location: /orders/42. Accepted for later processing: 202 plus a status resource. Update/delete with nothing to return: 204. Bad JSON or failed Bean Validation: 400 (some teams use 422 for semantically invalid but well-formed bodies; pick one and be consistent). Missing/invalid token: 401 (+WWW-Authenticate); authenticated but not allowed: 403; hiding existence of another tenant's resource: 404. Concurrent modification or business-state clash (e.g. cancel a shipped order): 409; failed If-Match precondition: 412. Too many requests: 429 + Retry-After. Dependency down or load shedding: 503 + Retry-After; upstream timeout from a gateway: 504.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Precise HTTP semantics under pressure: picking the status that tells a client what to do next (retry, fix input, re-authenticate), not just a 2xx/4xx/5xx bucket.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says '200 for success, 400 for errors, 500 if something breaks' and mixes up 401 and 403. Has no answer for 202, 409/412 or 429.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Gives the right table: 201 with Location, 202 plus a status resource, 204, 400 or 422 for validation, 401 vs 403, 409 for version conflict, 429, and 503 for downstream down. Explains 401 means unauthenticated and 403 means authenticated but not allowed.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds WWW-Authenticate on 401, Retry-After on 429/503, and 502/504 vs 503 depending on whether the dependency failed or timed out. Picks 400 or 422 and stays consistent, and checks the mapping with a @WebMvcTest that asserts status and headers. Decision rule: the status must tell the client whether a retry is safe.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Publishes a status-code policy in the API guidelines and enforces it through a shared @RestControllerAdvice starter and contract tests, so every team returns the same codes with ProblemDetail. Links 429/503 to SLO and error-budget dashboards so client faults (4xx) do not pollute availability metrics. Reviews new services against the policy in design review.
Explain safe vs idempotent methods. Why does it matter for retries, and is DELETE idempotent if the second call returns 404?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Safe methods must not change server state (GET, HEAD, OPTIONS), so caches, crawlers and prefetchers may call them freely. Idempotent methods (those plus PUT and DELETE) have the same effect on server state after N calls as after one; the response may differ. So DELETE is idempotent even if the first call returns 204 and the next 404. It matters because clients, service meshes (Envoy retries), Resilience4j and gateways automatically retry only idempotent calls; retrying POST can create duplicates. Failure mode: a "GET /reports/generate" that triggers work is unsafe and will be hit by link prefetchers and retries. Make POST retry-safe with an idempotency key (see below) and never use GET for side effects.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands safe vs idempotent as a contract about server state that drives retry, caching and gateway behaviour, and can reason about the DELETE-404 edge case.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Treats 'safe' and 'idempotent' as synonyms, or says DELETE is not idempotent because the second call returns 404.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Safe means no state change (GET, HEAD, OPTIONS). Idempotent means N calls leave the same server state as one (PUT, DELETE too), and the response may differ, so DELETE with 204 then 404 is still idempotent. POST is neither.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Connects this to retries: clients, gateways and HTTP client libraries can auto-retry idempotent methods after a timeout, but a POST retry can double-charge, so it needs an Idempotency-Key. Notes that idempotency is about state, not logging or updatedAt side effects, and that a PUT with server-generated fields can quietly break it. Verifies with a test that sends the call twice and asserts a single row.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets the retry policy centrally (Resilience4j or gateway rules) so only idempotent methods or keyed POSTs are retried automatically. Adds a lint or contract check that flags non-idempotent PUT/DELETE handlers. Documents idempotency per endpoint in OpenAPI so client teams do not guess.
How do you model resources and URIs for an Order service (nouns, nesting, actions that do not fit CRUD)?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Plural nouns, identifiers in the path, filters in the query: /orders, /orders/{id}, /orders/{id}/items, GET /orders?status=OPEN&sort=createdAt,desc. Nest only one level for true ownership (/customers/{id}/orders), otherwise expose the sub-resource at the top level with a filter so URIs stay stable. Non-CRUD actions: model the result as a resource (POST /orders/{id}/cancellation, POST /payments) or as a sub-resource state transition; use a verb endpoint (POST /orders/{id}:cancel) only when truly RPC-like and document it. Never expose the JPA entity or its table shape: use DTOs so the schema can evolve independently. Use kebab-case or lowerCamel consistently, avoid file extensions, and keep ids opaque.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Resource modelling judgment: stable noun-based URIs and a principled way to handle actions that do not fit CRUD.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Designs verb URIs like /getOrder and /createOrder, or nests everything five levels deep.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Uses plural nouns, ids in the path and filters in the query, for example /orders/{id}/items and ?status=OPEN&sort=createdAt,desc. Models actions as a resource, such as POST /orders/{id}/cancellations.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Nests only one level for true ownership and exposes other sub-resources at the top level with a filter so URIs stay stable. For genuine commands, weighs a sub-resource such as /payments against an explicit action endpoint, and explains the choice with idempotency and auditability in mind. Avoids leaking database ids or internal structure.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Writes the URI and naming conventions into a shared API style guide enforced by a linter (Spectral) in CI. Considers how the model survives a service split or ownership change, since URIs outlive implementations. Coaches teams to design the resource model with the consumer and review it before coding.
PUT vs PATCH: when do you use each, and what are JSON Merge Patch and JSON Patch?
Answer

1. Model Answer

PUT replaces the full representation (idempotent; omitted fields become null/default). PATCH sends a delta. JSON Merge Patch (RFC 7396, application/merge-patch+json) is a partial document: present fields overwrite, null deletes, absent fields untouched; simple but cannot patch array elements or distinguish "set to null" from "remove" for nullable columns. JSON Patch (RFC 6902, application/json-patch+json) is an array of operations (add/remove/replace/move/copy/test) with paths; precise and supports test for guards, but verbose. Spring has no built-in patch converter: use Jackson (ObjectMapper.readerForUpdating) for merge patch or the zjsonpatch/json-patch libraries for RFC 6902. Always validate the merged result, not the patch.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows the semantics of replace vs partial update and the real limits of Merge Patch and JSON Patch, including null handling.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'PUT updates, PATCH updates partially' and cannot name the patch formats.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: PUT replaces the whole representation and is idempotent. PATCH sends a delta. Merge Patch (RFC 7396) is a partial document where null deletes a field. JSON Patch (RFC 6902) is an array of operations such as add, remove, replace and test.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Points out that Merge Patch cannot patch array elements or distinguish 'set null' from 'remove', and that Java DTOs lose the 'absent vs null' distinction unless you use JsonNullable or a Map. Notes JSON Patch is powerful but order-dependent and a security risk if it can touch any path, so whitelist paths and validate the result. Pairs PATCH with If-Match so concurrent edits do not silently overwrite.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises on one patch format per organisation (usually Merge Patch for simplicity) and ships a shared helper so teams do not each hand-roll null semantics. Documents the choice and its limits in the API guide. Challenges the premise where a dedicated command endpoint is safer than a generic patch of a domain object.
Offset vs keyset (cursor) pagination: trade-offs, and what do Page, Slice and Window give you in Spring Data?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Offset (?page=3&size=20, SQL OFFSET) is simple and allows jumping to a page, but cost grows with the offset (DB must skip rows) and results shift/duplicate when rows are inserted during paging. Keyset/cursor (WHERE (created_at,id) < (:ts,:id) ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC LIMIT 21) is O(log n) with an index, stable under inserts, but only next/prev and needs a deterministic unique sort key. Spring Data: Page<T> runs an extra count(*) (expensive on big tables), Slice<T> fetches size+1 rows to know hasNext without a count, and Window<T> with ScrollPosition.keyset() (Spring Data 3.1) supports keyset scrolling. Return an opaque, signed/base64 cursor so clients cannot craft it; cap size (spring.data.web.pageable.max-page-size, default 2000).

Pitfall: sorting only by a non-unique column (e.g. createdAt) with any pagination gives duplicates/missing rows; always add the id as tie-breaker.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Trade-off thinking between offset and keyset pagination, plus knowing what Spring Data's Page, Slice and Window actually cost.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'use Pageable with page and size' and does not know why deep pages get slow or why rows are duplicated.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Offset is simple and allows jumping to a page, but the database must skip rows and results shift on inserts. Keyset uses WHERE (created_at,id) < (:ts,:id) ... LIMIT n+1, is stable and index-friendly, but offers only next/previous. Page runs an extra count query, Slice fetches n+1, and Window/ScrollPosition supports keyset scrolling.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Requires a deterministic tie-breaker (unique id) in the sort and a matching composite index, and encodes the cursor opaquely. Notes that the Page count query can be the slowest part on large tables, so prefer Slice unless the UI needs a total. Verifies with EXPLAIN and load tests at deep offsets. Rule: infinite scroll or feeds use keyset, admin grids with page jumps use offset with capped size.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a platform default (max page size, keyset for large tables) and publishes a pagination contract so clients across teams behave the same. Adds slow-query monitoring that flags large OFFSET values. Plans the migration of existing offset APIs to cursors with a deprecation window.
How do you accept Pageable and filters in a controller and keep them safe?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Declare Pageable as a controller argument (resolved by PageableHandlerMethodArgumentResolver, auto-registered by Boot via @EnableSpringDataWebSupport): ?page=0&size=20&sort=createdAt,desc. Defaults via @PageableDefault(size=20); cap size with spring.data.web.pageable.max-page-size. Whitelist sortable properties: a client-supplied sort=password or a non-indexed column is both a leak and a DoS risk. Filters: bind a small @ModelAttribute record (OrderFilter(status, from, to)) and translate into a Specification or Querydsl predicate; never concatenate into JPQL. Return a stable envelope (content, nextCursor/page) instead of serialising PageImpl directly (Boot 3.3 warns about it; use PagedModel via @EnableSpringDataWebSupport(pageSerializationMode=VIA_DTO)).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Treats client-supplied paging and sorting as untrusted input and knows the Spring mechanisms that constrain it.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Declares Pageable in the controller and passes it straight to the repository.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Pageable is resolved by PageableHandlerMethodArgumentResolver. Uses @PageableDefault and spring.data.web.pageable.max-page-size to cap size. Whitelists sortable properties.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that sort=password or a non-indexed or nested property can leak data or trigger full scans, so maps public sort keys to known columns and rejects others with 400. Bounds filters (date ranges, list sizes) and tests the rejected cases. Uses a dedicated filter DTO with validation rather than a free-form map.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides a shared starter or argument resolver with the whitelist and limits baked in so teams cannot forget it. Adds a review checklist and an integration test template. Tracks slow queries and abusive page sizes in metrics, and ties limits to the rate-limit policy.
Compare API versioning strategies (URI, header, media type, query param). What did Spring Framework 7 add?
Answer

1. Model Answer

URI (/v1/orders): visible, cacheable, easy to route at the gateway; but the "resource" changes identity and clients must rewrite URLs. Header (X-API-Version: 2) keeps URIs clean but is invisible in the browser and needs Vary for caches. Media type (Accept: application/vnd.acme.order.v2+json) is the purist content-negotiation approach; harder to test and document. Query param (?version=2) is simple but pollutes caching keys. Pragmatic advice: prefer additive, backward-compatible evolution so you rarely need a new version; when you must break, run v1 and v2 side by side with a deprecation (Sunset/Deprecation headers) period.

Spring Framework 7 (Boot 4) added first-class API versioning: @RequestMapping(version="1.1") (also on @GetMapping etc.), configured through WebMvcConfigurer#configureApiVersioning(ApiVersionConfigurer) choosing where the version is read (useRequestHeader, usePathSegment, useQueryParam, useMediaTypeParameter), with baseline syntax like "1.2+", plus RestClient/WebClient support to insert the version on outbound calls (verify exact API and Boot properties in release notes).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Weighs API versioning strategies on caching, routing, client cost and evolution, and knows the framework support available.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'put /v1 in the URL' and cannot explain alternatives or downsides.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: URI versioning is visible, cacheable and easy to route. Header versioning keeps URIs clean but needs Vary and is harder to test. Media type is the purist approach, and a query param is simple but pollutes cache keys. Mentions that Spring Framework 7 adds first-class API versioning support in MVC.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Argues that the best strategy is to avoid breaking changes at all with additive evolution and the tolerant reader, and to version only when forced. Considers gateway routing, OpenAPI per version, how long to support old versions, and sunset signals via Deprecation and Sunset headers. Treats details of the Spring 7 versioning API carefully, checking the docs before relying on specifics.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets an org-wide versioning and deprecation policy (support window, telemetry on version usage, sunset process) and measures which clients still call old versions. Funds migration tooling and contract tests for consumers. Pushes back on the premise that every change needs a new version.
Implement conditional requests with ETag/If-Match tied to optimistic locking. What status do you return on a stale write?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Expose the entity @Version as a strong/weak ETag on GET. Clients send it back as If-Match on PUT/PATCH/DELETE. If it does not match the current version return 412 Precondition Failed (or 428 Precondition Required if the header is missing and you require it). This turns the lost-update problem into an explicit HTTP contract, with no pessimistic locks. For reads, If-None-Match gives 304 Not Modified and saves bandwidth (request.checkNotModified(etag) or ShallowEtagHeaderFilter, which still computes the body). Still catch ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException for races between the check and the commit and map it to 409/412.

🧑‍💻 From my experienceIn retail-platform-style flows I chose optimistic locking only. Mapping the version to ETag/If-Match is how I would expose that to REST clients; the bounded retry or "reload and re-apply" decision belongs to the caller.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Maps optimistic locking onto an HTTP contract, closing the lost-update problem without server-side locks.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'use @Version' but does not link it to the client, or returns 500 on a stale write.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Expose the @Version as an ETag on GET. The client sends If-Match on PUT/PATCH/DELETE. A mismatch returns 412 Precondition Failed, and a missing required header can return 428.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes a stale write may surface as OptimisticLockingFailureException and must be mapped to 412 (or 409) in the advice. Checks the version in the same transaction as the update, not in a separate read, to avoid a race. Treats weak vs strong ETags carefully, supports If-None-Match for 304 caching, and tests with two concurrent updates.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes conditional writes the platform standard for mutable resources, with a shared filter and a documented client retry pattern (re-GET, merge, resubmit). Measures the 412 rate as a signal of contention hot spots. Documents when pessimistic locks are still justified.
Design an idempotent POST using an Idempotency-Key header.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Client sends a unique key per logical operation (Idempotency-Key: 7f0c...). Server stores (key, request-hash, status, response, created_at) under a unique constraint. Flow: insert key row as IN_PROGRESS in the same transaction as the business write (or before it); if the insert hits the unique constraint, load the row: same hash and COMPLETED means replay the stored response (same status and body), IN_PROGRESS means 409 (or wait), different hash means 422 (key reuse). TTL 24h with a cleanup job. Scope the key by tenant/user. Do the business write and key completion atomically, otherwise a crash between them re-executes or loses the response. For Kafka-triggered callers the equivalent is an idempotent consumer keyed on a message id. The header is an IETF draft, not a final RFC (verify status).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designs idempotency end to end: storage, race handling, request fingerprinting and failure modes, not just 'store the key'.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'check if the key exists, and if so skip', which ignores concurrent duplicates and crashes mid-request.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Client sends an Idempotency-Key. Server stores key, request hash, status and response under a unique constraint, inserts the key as IN_PROGRESS, and replays the stored response when the same key and hash come back.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Relies on the unique constraint to resolve concurrent duplicates, returns 409 for an in-flight duplicate, and returns 422 (or 409) when the same key arrives with a different payload hash. Writes the key in the same transaction as the business change so a crash does not leave a half-done record, and expires keys with a TTL. Tests with parallel requests and a crash between steps.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides this as a shared library or gateway capability so every payment-like endpoint behaves identically. Defines retention, storage cost and PII handling for stored responses. Aligns clients on key generation and retry guidance, and monitors replay rates as a health signal.
How do you expose a long-running operation (report, bulk import) over REST?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Return 202 Accepted immediately with Location: /jobs/{id} (and optionally Retry-After). The job resource shows state (QUEUED/RUNNING/SUCCEEDED/FAILED), progress, and on success a link to the result (303 See Other to the created resource is a common pattern). Execute on a separate worker (Kafka consumer, @Async executor, or Spring Batch) persisting state in a table so it survives restarts and multiple pods; polling is the default, add webhook/SSE if clients need push. Make job creation idempotent, support DELETE /jobs/{id} for cancel, and expire old jobs. Do not hold the HTTP thread (DeferredResult/Callable only helps for seconds, not minutes).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designs asynchronous request-reply over HTTP with clear state, polling and failure semantics.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Keeps the HTTP request open for minutes or just returns 200 and hopes the work finishes.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Returns 202 Accepted with Location: /jobs/{id}, a job resource with QUEUED/RUNNING/SUCCEEDED/FAILED and progress, and a link or 303 to the result. Work runs on a separate worker, such as Kafka, @Async or Spring Batch.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Persists job state so it survives restarts, supports Retry-After, cancellation and idempotent submission, and defines result retention. Warns that a plain @Async in-memory job is lost on pod restart. Considers webhooks or SSE as alternatives to polling and tests the failure and timeout paths.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises a job-resource contract and a shared job service so every team does not reinvent status models. Sets quotas, priorities and SLOs per job type and alerts on queue age. Decides when to push for event-driven notifications instead of client polling.
How do you handle file upload and large download in a Spring REST service?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Upload: multipart/form-data with @RequestPart MultipartFile file (plus a JSON part). Limits: spring.servlet.multipart.max-file-size / max-request-size (defaults 1MB/10MB). For big files stream to storage (S3 presigned upload URL is better: the service never touches the bytes). Download: return Resource/InputStreamResource or StreamingResponseBody so content is streamed instead of buffered; set Content-Disposition, Content-Type, and support Range (ResourceRegion/ResourceHttpMessageConverter handle ranges). Validate content type by sniffing, not by filename; generate your own storage key to avoid path traversal.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Handles large payloads without exhausting memory or threads, and knows when the service should stay out of the data path.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Reads the whole MultipartFile or file into a byte[] and returns it, and does not know about the default size limits.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Upload uses multipart/form-data with @RequestPart MultipartFile and configures spring.servlet.multipart.max-file-size and max-request-size. Download returns Resource or StreamingResponseBody so bytes are streamed.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Prefers presigned URLs to object storage so the service never touches the bytes, validates content type and size server-side, and avoids path traversal on file names. Supports Range requests for large downloads and sets Content-Disposition. Verifies with a heap-bounded load test and watches thread-pool or connection usage for slow clients.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines a platform pattern for file handling (presigned upload, virus scan, retention, encryption) and enforces size limits at the gateway too. Accounts for storage and egress cost. Reviews data-protection requirements for uploaded content.
Design the error contract: what is ProblemDetail and how do you make every endpoint return it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

RFC 7807 (obsoleted by RFC 9457, 2023) defines application/problem+json with type, title, status, detail, instance plus extension members. Spring 6 has ProblemDetail and ErrorResponse; enable defaults with spring.mvc.problemdetails.enabled=true (Boot 3.0) or extend ResponseEntityExceptionHandler in a @RestControllerAdvice so framework exceptions (400/404/405/415) are also problem+json. Add stable machine-readable members (code, errors[] with field and message, traceId); never leak stack traces or SQL. One shape for 4xx and 5xx lets clients and OpenAPI document it once.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Builds one consistent machine-readable error contract across the whole service, including framework-generated errors.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Returns ad hoc JSON like {"error":"something"} per controller, or lets Boot's default error page leak stack details.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes RFC 7807 (now RFC 9457) application/problem+json with type, title, status, detail, instance. Spring 6 provides ProblemDetail, and spring.mvc.problemdetails.enabled=true or extending ResponseEntityExceptionHandler in a @RestControllerAdvice covers framework exceptions.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds extension members such as errors for field problems and a traceId, keeps type URIs stable, and never exposes stack traces or internal messages. Checks that 404s, 405s, 415s and security errors also produce the format, not only controller exceptions. Tests the advice with @WebMvcTest and a contract test.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Ships the error contract as a shared starter and documents the type catalogue so clients can branch on it. Makes sure the Spring Security entry point and gateway produce the same format. Uses error type as a metrics dimension for operational dashboards.
How do you design bulk endpoints and partial failure (e.g. create 500 items in one call)?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Options: (1) POST /items:batch returning 207 Multi-Status or 200 with a per-item result array (index, status, id|error); (2) all-or-nothing in one transaction returning 201/400 (simplest to reason about); (3) async job (202) for large batches. Set a hard cap on batch size and body size, make each item idempotent (client item id), and document whether processing is ordered. Prefer small bounded batches over huge payloads that tie up a thread and a transaction; for truly large imports use the job pattern plus Spring Batch. The same per-item-result idea is how a fork-and-join aggregator reports partial success.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designs bulk operations with explicit partial-failure semantics and limits.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Loops over items in one transaction and returns 200, so one bad item either kills everything silently or hides failures.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists the options: per-item results with 207 or 200, all-or-nothing in one transaction, or an async job via 202. Sets a hard cap on batch size and documents behaviour.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Makes items idempotent with a client item id so retries are safe, returns per-item index, status and error, and decides atomicity by business need. Watches transaction size, lock duration and request timeouts for large batches. Tests with mixed valid and invalid items and with a retry of the whole batch.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Publishes a bulk-API pattern (limits, result shape, atomicity) so clients see consistent behaviour across services. Questions whether a bulk endpoint should be an async import job at scale. Monitors batch sizes and partial-failure rates for capacity and abuse.
What is HATEOAS and is it worth it for internal microservices?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Hypermedia As The Engine Of Application State: responses carry links (self, next, cancel) so clients discover actions and state transitions instead of hard-coding URIs. Spring HATEOAS gives EntityModel, CollectionModel, PagedModel, WebMvcLinkBuilder.linkTo(methodOn(...)) and HAL (application/hal+json) rendering. Value: pagination links, state-dependent actions, URI changes without client changes. Cost: bigger payloads, tooling and client discipline; most internal service-to-service REST (and OpenAPI-generated clients) stays at Richardson level 2 and only uses hypermedia for pagination links or workflow actions. Say it honestly: know it, use it where clients are many and unknown (public APIs).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Pragmatic judgment on a REST maturity idea: knows what HATEOAS delivers and when it is not worth the cost.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Gives the textbook definition and says 'we should always use HATEOAS because it is REST', or has never used it.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Responses carry links (self, next, cancel) so clients discover transitions. Spring HATEOAS offers EntityModel, CollectionModel, PagedModel, linkTo(methodOn(...)) and HAL.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes the real value is pagination links and state-dependent actions (only show cancel when allowed), but that most internal clients hard-code URIs from OpenAPI, so full hypermedia adds payload size, a learning curve and little benefit. Chooses selectively, for example links for paging and workflow states only. Tests that links are correct.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets a guideline that internal service-to-service APIs use contract-first OpenAPI without full hypermedia, while public or workflow-heavy APIs may justify links. Makes the decision explicit in the style guide to avoid per-team debates. Revisits it if client generation tooling changes.
Why validate sort and filter parameters, and how do you document and enforce rate limits (429)?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Free-form sort/filter lets a client order by an unindexed column, request size=100000 or inject expressions: whitelist fields, cap page size, bound date ranges. Rate limiting belongs at the gateway (Spring Cloud Gateway RequestRateLimiter with Redis, or Bucket4j/Resilience4j RateLimiter in-service) returning 429 with Retry-After and, if you want, RateLimit-* headers (IETF draft; verify). Key the limiter by client id/API key/tenant, not IP alone (NAT). Clients must honour Retry-After with jittered backoff.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Protects the service from abusive or expensive requests through input validation and rate limiting, and communicates limits to clients.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Accepts any sort and size value and says rate limiting is 'something the infrastructure does'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Whitelists sort and filter fields, caps page size and date ranges. Rate limits at the gateway (Spring Cloud Gateway RequestRateLimiter with Redis) or in-service with Bucket4j or Resilience4j, returning 429 with Retry-After.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Chooses limit keys carefully (API key, user, tenant, not just IP), distinguishes burst from sustained rates, and documents limits in OpenAPI and headers. Notes in-service limits are per instance unless backed by shared storage. Load-tests the 429 path and checks that limiters fail open or closed deliberately when Redis is down.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines tiered quotas per client class, enforces them at the platform gateway, and feeds limit hits into capacity and abuse dashboards. Aligns with cost and fairness goals across tenants. Documents client backoff expectations so retries do not amplify load.

27 · Spring Web and core annotations deep-dive (with since versions)

Which annotation, from which package, since when, on which layer, with an annotation cheat sheet.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Which annotation, from which package, on which layer; includes a cheat sheet.
  • Must-know: ★ @RestController, binding annotations · ★ @Valid vs @Validated · ★ @Transactional on services
  • Top pitfall: putting @Transactional/@Async on controllers or self-calling them.
  • Version markers: @RestController 4.0; composed mappings 4.3; HandlerMethodValidationException 6.1

Annotations are metadata read by Spring post-processors, HandlerMapping/argument resolvers and AOP proxies. Knowing which layer an annotation belongs to, and which package it comes from, avoids the classic mistakes (transaction on the controller, wrong @RequestBody import, @Valid without effect).

Web layer@RestController @RequestMapping @GetMapping @PathVariable @RequestParam @RequestBody @ExceptionHandler @RestControllerAdvice
Service layer@Service @Transactional @Async @Cacheable @Retryable @Scheduled (proxy-based, on beans called from outside)
Configuration@Configuration @Bean @Profile @Conditional* @Value @ConfigurationProperties @Enable*
Data@Repository @Entity @Version @Query @Modifying
🔑 KeyRule of thumb: controller = HTTP translation only (bind, validate, call, map status). @Transactional, @Async, @Cacheable go on service methods: they are proxy advice, the transaction boundary should wrap a business use case (possibly several repositories), and a controller-level transaction keeps the DB connection open during serialization (and with OSIV, long after).
