I also wrote a custom annotation processor (plus an IntelliJ plugin) for Splunk audit parameters, so I know annotations from both sides: runtime metadata read by Spring, and compile-time metadata read by javax.annotation.processing.

Worksheet

@RestController vs @Controller + @ResponseBody: what is the difference?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@RestController (Spring 4.0) is meta-annotated with @Controller and @ResponseBody, so every handler return value is written through an HttpMessageConverter (JSON) instead of being resolved as a view name. With plain @Controller you return view names (Thymeleaf) unless the method (or class) has @ResponseBody, or returns ResponseEntity (which always acts as a body). Mixing is legal: a @Controller can have both HTML and JSON methods. Failure mode: returning a String from @Controller without @ResponseBody triggers a view lookup and a 404/template error.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows how return values are handled (view resolution vs message converters) rather than just the annotation shortcut.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says '@RestController is for REST and @Controller is for normal controllers' without mechanism.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: @RestController is @Controller plus @ResponseBody, so return values go through an HttpMessageConverter instead of view resolution. With plain @Controller you need @ResponseBody or ResponseEntity.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains what happens if you return a String from @Controller (it is treated as a view name), how content negotiation picks the converter, and that ResponseEntity gives control over status and headers. Notes mixed apps (MVC pages plus JSON) and tests with MockMvc that the body is JSON.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Keeps JSON API and server-rendered concerns in separate controllers or modules with clear conventions. Standardises on ResponseEntity or status annotations in guidelines. Treats this as a quick-check item in code review rather than a debate.
Walk through the binding annotations in one handler: @PathVariable, @RequestParam, @RequestHeader, @CookieValue, @RequestBody, @RequestPart, @ModelAttribute.
Answer

1. Model Answer

All are resolved by HandlerMethodArgumentResolvers. required defaults to true (missing gives 400 MissingServletRequestParameterException); defaultValue makes it optional; Optional<T> also makes it optional. Parameter names need -parameters (Boot parent configures it) or an explicit name. @RequestBody reads the whole body once (JSON to object; invalid gives 400 HttpMessageNotReadableException); only one per method. @ModelAttribute (implicit for non-simple types) binds query/form fields to a bean/record, not JSON. @RequestPart picks a multipart part and can deserialize JSON parts.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Command of the argument-resolution annotations, including defaults, required-ness and body reading rules.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Lists the annotations and says each 'gets data from the request' with no detail on missing values or body consumption.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: All are resolved by HandlerMethodArgumentResolvers. required defaults to true and a missing value gives 400, defaultValue or Optional makes it optional. @RequestBody reads the body once via a converter. @RequestPart handles multipart parts, and @ModelAttribute binds form or query data to an object.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes names need the -parameters compiler flag or explicit names, that the body stream can only be read once (so logging filters must wrap it), and that @ModelAttribute binds setters, so unexpected fields can be bound unless restricted. Maps the resulting exceptions to 400 in the advice and tests missing and malformed inputs.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides controller conventions (DTOs for bodies, no entities, validated params) in a template or archetype and enforces them with ArchUnit or review checks. Considers mass-assignment risks as a security review item. Documents common pitfalls for new joiners.
How do @ExceptionHandler, @ResponseStatus and @RestControllerAdvice interact, and which wins?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Resolution order in DispatcherServlet exception resolvers: ExceptionHandlerExceptionResolver (@ExceptionHandler: first in the controller, then in @ControllerAdvice beans by @Order), then ResponseStatusExceptionResolver (exception class annotated @ResponseStatus, or ResponseStatusException), then DefaultHandlerExceptionResolver (framework exceptions to 4xx). Most specific exception type wins within a class. A @ResponseStatus on a handler method sets the success status (e.g. 201); on an exception class it sets the error status. @RestControllerAdvice (4.3) = @ControllerAdvice + @ResponseBody, can be narrowed by basePackages/annotations. Pitfall: a catch-all @ExceptionHandler(Exception.class) hides AccessDeniedException (Spring Security) behaviour: rethrow or exclude security exceptions.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands the exception resolver chain in DispatcherServlet well enough to predict which handler wins.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'the @ControllerAdvice handles all exceptions' and cannot explain precedence.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: ExceptionHandlerExceptionResolver runs first, using a local @ExceptionHandler in the controller before @ControllerAdvice beans by @Order. Then ResponseStatusExceptionResolver handles @ResponseStatus and ResponseStatusException, then DefaultHandlerExceptionResolver for framework exceptions.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes the most specific exception type match wins within a handler class, that unordered advice beans are non-deterministic, and that exceptions thrown in filters never reach @ControllerAdvice. Shows how to debug by checking which resolver handled it, and tests with @WebMvcTest.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Keeps a single global advice per service (shared starter) with a clear escape hatch for local handlers, enforced by review or ArchUnit. Standardises logging level per exception class so alerts are not noisy. Documents the filter-versus-advice gap for security errors.
@Valid vs @Validated: group validation, class-level validation of @PathVariable/@RequestParam, and HandlerMethodValidationException (6.1).
Answer

1. Model Answer

@Valid (jakarta.validation) triggers validation and cascades into nested objects/collection elements but has no groups. @Validated (Spring) adds groups (@Validated(OnCreate.class)) and, on a class, turns on method validation via an AOP proxy (MethodValidationPostProcessor) for constraints on simple parameters (@PathVariable @Min(1) long id); violations throw ConstraintViolationException (which you must map to 400, otherwise it becomes 500). Spring 6.1 built-in method validation for controllers: if a controller method has constraint annotations on parameters (or @Valid on the arguments combined with other constraints) Spring validates without class-level @Validated and throws HandlerMethodValidationException (a 400 ErrorResponse) instead; @RequestBody @Valid alone still throws MethodArgumentNotValidException. Do not use class-level @Validated on controllers in 6.1+ (it triggers the older AOP path and exception).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Distinguishes @Valid from @Validated and knows the method-validation model and exception changes in Spring 6.1.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Treats @Valid and @Validated as interchangeable and does not know validation of path or query params needs extra setup.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: @Valid triggers validation and cascades into nested objects but has no groups. @Validated adds groups and, at class level, enables method validation through an AOP proxy. Spring 6.1 introduces HandlerMethodValidationException for controller method validation.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that failures differ by path: MethodArgumentNotValidException for @RequestBody, constraint violations for proxy-based method validation, and HandlerMethodValidationException in 6.1+, so the advice must map each to the same 400 format. Notes the proxy is bypassed on self-invocation. Tests each path.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines where validation lives and provides one advice that normalises all three exception types so clients see a single error shape. Monitors migration impact when upgrading Boot, since exception types changed. Documents group usage limits so teams prefer separate DTOs over many groups.
Why must @Transactional, @Async and @Cacheable go on service methods rather than on controllers, and what does self-invocation do?
Answer

1. Model Answer

They are implemented by AOP proxies (JDK or CGLIB) created by a BeanPostProcessor; only calls that go through the proxy are advised. A service is the natural use-case boundary: the transaction should wrap the business operation, release the connection before JSON serialisation, and be reusable from Kafka listeners/schedulers, not just HTTP. @Async on a controller returns control to the client thread semantics you did not intend; @Cacheable on a controller would cache HTTP-specific objects. Self-invocation (this.other()) bypasses the proxy: the annotation silently does nothing. Fixes: move the method to another bean, inject self, or use TransactionTemplate. Methods must be non-private (public for the default proxy mode) and the class non-final for CGLIB.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands proxy-based AOP as the reason annotations work, and the limits of it (self-invocation, visibility, placement).

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'annotations just work on any method' and is surprised when @Transactional does nothing on an internal call.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: @Transactional, @Async and @Cacheable are applied through JDK or CGLIB proxies created by a BeanPostProcessor, so only calls through the proxy are advised. Self-invocation bypasses the proxy. Service methods are the right boundary.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains why not controllers: the transaction would span view/JSON serialisation and hold a connection, and logic would not be reusable from Kafka listeners or schedulers. Names the fixes for self-invocation (move to another bean, inject self, or AspectJ weaving) and checks with a test or a breakpoint on TransactionInterceptor. Notes private or final methods are not advised.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Enforces boundaries with ArchUnit rules (no @Transactional in controllers, no cross-calls inside one bean for advised methods) and a review checklist. Adds a startup or test check that critical advice actually applies. Teaches the proxy model in onboarding to prevent a recurring class of production bugs.
@Component vs @Service vs @Repository vs @Controller: any functional difference?
Answer

1. Model Answer

All are stereotypes detected by @ComponentScan; @Service and @Controller add semantics only (documentation, pointcut targeting, @Controller is recognised by MVC). @Repository has real behaviour: PersistenceExceptionTranslationPostProcessor wraps it to translate vendor exceptions (SQLException, Hibernate) into Spring's DataAccessException hierarchy. Spring Data repository interfaces get this automatically. Use the specific stereotype for readability and for slice tests (@WebMvcTest scans only controllers/advice, not @Service).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows which stereotypes carry behaviour versus semantics, so does not over- or under-state their difference.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'they are all the same' or 'they each do something different' without specifics.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: All are detected by @ComponentScan. @Service and @Controller add semantics, and @Controller is recognised by MVC. @Repository triggers PersistenceExceptionTranslationPostProcessor, which translates vendor exceptions into DataAccessException.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes that exception translation matters for catch blocks and retry logic, and that Spring Data repositories get it automatically. Uses stereotypes as pointcut and ArchUnit targets for layering. Does not claim @Service has runtime effect.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Uses the stereotypes to express architecture, enforced with ArchUnit layering rules. Avoids spending review time on it beyond naming consistency. Documents the few places where it has actual behaviour.
@Bean, @Primary, @Qualifier, @Profile, @Conditional*, @Value and @ConfigurationProperties: when do you use each?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@Bean for third-party objects or conditional construction; @Primary picks the default among candidates, @Qualifier selects by name/label at the injection point. @Profile("prod") for environment-specific beans (!prod, a & b expressions). @Conditional is the generic mechanism; Boot provides @ConditionalOnProperty/OnClass/OnMissingBean/OnWebApplication (use @ConditionalOnMissingBean in your own starters so users can override). @Value("${x:default}") is fine for a single value; once you have a group of related settings use @ConfigurationProperties (relaxed binding, validation with @Validated, immutable constructor binding on records, metadata for IDE). Prefer constructor injection; never read Environment deep inside business code.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Chooses the right configuration and wiring mechanism for each need and understands precedence between them.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Mixes @Value everywhere and cannot say when @Primary or @Qualifier is appropriate.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: @Bean for third-party or conditional construction, @Primary for a default, @Qualifier at the injection point, @Profile for environments, @Conditional* for auto-config-style logic, and @ConfigurationProperties for typed grouped config.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Prefers @ConfigurationProperties with @Validated for fail-fast startup and relaxed binding, uses @ConditionalOnMissingBean so users can override, and avoids profile sprawl in favour of config externalisation. Verifies with the conditions report (--debug) or the actuator configprops and conditions endpoints.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises typed properties with validation across teams and limits @Profile usage in favour of externalised config per environment. Enforces rules such as no @Value for grouped settings with review or ArchUnit. Documents the property contract as part of the service's operational doc.
What does @SpringBootApplication actually compose, and what goes wrong if you put the main class in the default package?
Answer

1. Model Answer

It is @SpringBootConfiguration (a @Configuration), @EnableAutoConfiguration (imports the candidates listed in META-INF/spring/org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.AutoConfiguration.imports, filtered by conditions) and @ComponentScan (with excludeFilters for TypeExcludeFilter and AutoConfigurationExcludeFilter) rooted at the annotated class's package. Default package: scanning would cover the entire classpath (slow, picks up library classes, warning); beans outside the main class's package tree are silently not found (the classic "No qualifying bean" after moving a class). Use exclude/excludeName or spring.autoconfigure.exclude to disable an auto-config.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands what @SpringBootApplication composes and how component scanning and auto-configuration are bounded.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it starts the app' and does not know the composed annotations or why the default package breaks things.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: It is @SpringBootConfiguration, @EnableAutoConfiguration and @ComponentScan. Auto-config candidates come from the AutoConfiguration.imports file and are filtered by conditions. Scanning starts at the main class's package.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that in the default package the scan covers the whole classpath, including libraries, causing slow startup, errors or unwanted beans. Notes sub-package placement of main classes controls scan scope and that exclude and --debug help diagnose auto-config. Checks with the conditions report.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides a project template with the right package layout and ArchUnit checks that the main class is in a named package. Keeps auto-config footprint small and audits excluded configs on upgrades. Documents custom auto-configuration for shared starters.
@Scheduled, @EventListener and @TransactionalEventListener: what are the pitfalls in a clustered REST service?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@Scheduled (needs @EnableScheduling; Boot auto-configures the scheduler, one thread by default, or virtual threads when enabled) runs on every instance: with N pods the job fires N times, so use ShedLock/Quartz JDBC store or a K8s CronJob for singletons, and keep methods short on the single scheduler thread. @EventListener methods are synchronous in the publisher thread by default (add @Async to decouple; then exceptions are not seen by the publisher). @TransactionalEventListener(phase=AFTER_COMMIT) fires only if the transaction commits; the listener runs outside the transaction, so a DB write there needs REQUIRES_NEW. In-process events are not durable: for must-not-lose work use the outbox pattern and Kafka.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Spots the distributed-systems traps in seemingly simple annotations once the service runs on multiple pods.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says '@Scheduled runs a job on a schedule' and doesn't realise it fires on every instance.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: With N pods, @Scheduled runs N times, so use ShedLock, Quartz with JDBC store, or a Kubernetes CronJob. @EventListener is synchronous and in the publisher's thread, and @TransactionalEventListener runs relative to the transaction phase.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes AFTER_COMMIT listeners run after commit so a failure there is not rolled back and work can be lost, which points to the outbox pattern for reliability. Mentions the single scheduler thread being blocked by long jobs and lock TTL vs job duration. Tests with two instances.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides a platform scheduling approach (locking library or CronJob standard) and bans unguarded @Scheduled in review or ArchUnit. Standardises outbox for cross-service events. Adds job metrics and alerts on missed or overlapping runs.
@CrossOrigin vs global CORS config: how do they work with Spring Security?
Answer

1. Model Answer

CORS is enforced by browsers; the server declares allowed origins/methods/headers. @CrossOrigin(origins="https://app.acme.com") is per controller/method; global config via WebMvcConfigurer#addCorsMappings. With Spring Security you must enable http.cors(withDefaults()) (or supply a CorsConfigurationSource bean) so the CORS filter runs before authentication, otherwise preflight OPTIONS gets 401. Never combine allowCredentials(true) with "*" origins (rejected). Service-to-service calls are not subject to CORS; the gateway is the usual place to configure it once.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands CORS as a browser-enforced policy and how it must be ordered relative to Spring Security.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Adds @CrossOrigin(origins="*") everywhere and wonders why preflight still returns 401/403 once security is on.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: CORS is enforced by browsers. @CrossOrigin is per controller or method, global config via WebMvcConfigurer#addCorsMappings. With Spring Security, enable http.cors(...) or supply a CorsConfigurationSource so the CORS filter runs before authentication.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains preflight OPTIONS requests need to be allowed without credentials, that allowCredentials(true) cannot be combined with wildcard origins, and that CORS is not a security control against non-browser clients. Tests with a preflight request in an integration test.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Centralises CORS at the gateway or a shared config with an approved origin list per environment, and bans wildcard origins in production via review. Keeps the policy in config rather than annotations. Documents it so frontend teams do not ship per-service workarounds.

28 · Jackson and Bean Validation annotations

Shape the JSON contract and guard the boundary: names, nulls, dates, unknown fields, constraints and groups.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Shape the JSON contract with Jackson and guard it with Bean Validation.
  • Must-know: ★ @JsonProperty/@JsonIgnore/@JsonInclude · ★ ignore unknown properties · ★ @Valid cascade, groups
  • Top pitfall: javax.validation imports on Boot 3 or missing validation starter: constraints do nothing.
  • Version markers: Records in Jackson 2.12; jakarta.validation Boot 3.0

Jackson turns your DTOs into the wire contract; Bean Validation guards the boundary. Both are annotation-driven and both changed at Boot 3 (javax.validation to jakarta.validation) and, per the Boot 4 line, Jackson 3 (verify in release notes).

AnnotationEffectSince
@JsonProperty("name") ★ MUST KNOWRename / force a field or constructor param into JSONJackson 1.x
@JsonIgnore / @JsonIgnoreProperties({"a"}, ignoreUnknown=true) ★ MUST KNOWExclude a property / tolerate unknown input fieldsJackson 1.x
@JsonInclude(NON_NULL | NON_EMPTY) ★ MUST KNOWOmit null/empty values on outputJackson 1.x / 2.x
@JsonFormat(shape, pattern, timezone) Date/number format; ISO-8601 with JavaTimeModuleJackson 2.0
@JsonCreator / @JsonValue Choose constructor/factory for input; single value for output (enums, value objects)Jackson 1.2
@JsonView(Public.class) Per-view field filtering (response shaping)Jackson 1.4
@JsonAlias, @JsonNaming Accept alternative names; naming strategy (snake_case)Jackson 2.9 / 2.1
@JsonTypeInfo / @JsonSubTypes Polymorphic types (security risk with default typing)Jackson 1.x
⚠ PitfallJackson annotations live in com.fasterxml.jackson.annotation (and still do in Jackson 3, while the databind/core packages moved to tools.jackson; verify). Bean Validation lives in jakarta.validation.constraints: if you see javax.validation imports compiling on Boot 3 you have a stale dependency and the constraints silently do nothing.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceAt California Student Aid Commission, SSN/PII was protected with AES-256/RSA field-level encryption. On the Jackson side that same discipline means @JsonIgnore/DTO separation so sensitive fields are never serialised by accident, and never logging request bodies. In the retail platform the encryption happens at the gateway via KMS, so DTOs only ever see ciphertext or masked values at the persistence edge.

Worksheet

How do you control JSON names, hide fields and omit nulls with Jackson annotations? Show a DTO.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Prefer DTOs (records) separate from entities. @JsonProperty renames; @JsonIgnore hides (use @JsonProperty(access=WRITE_ONLY) for password-like input-only fields); @JsonInclude(NON_NULL) trims output (class-level, or global spring.jackson.default-property-inclusion=non_null).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Shapes the JSON contract deliberately with DTOs rather than leaking entities.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Serialises JPA entities directly and uses @JsonIgnore to patch over leaks.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Uses record DTOs with @JsonProperty to rename, @JsonIgnore or @JsonProperty(access=WRITE_ONLY) to hide, and @JsonInclude(NON_NULL) to omit nulls, possibly globally with spring.jackson.default-property-inclusion.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains why separate DTOs avoid lazy-loading exceptions, accidental exposure and coupling the API to the schema. Notes that omitting nulls changes the contract (absent vs null) and may surprise clients. Tests serialisation with @JsonTest.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes 'no entities in API' a rule enforced via ArchUnit, and standardises naming and null policy in the style guide. Considers generated DTOs from OpenAPI to avoid drift. Reviews exposure of sensitive fields as part of security checklists.
How does Jackson handle unknown properties in Spring Boot, and what should a service do for backward-compatible evolution?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Boot configures its ObjectMapper with FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES=false (plain Jackson default is true), so extra fields in requests are ignored. Property: spring.jackson.deserialization.fail-on-unknown-properties. Tolerant reader is the right default for microservices: adding a response field must not break consumers, so consumers must ignore unknown fields (@JsonIgnoreProperties(ignoreUnknown=true) if you build your own mapper). For strict public inputs you may turn it on so typos return 400. Removing or renaming a field, changing a type or tightening validation is a breaking change: add the new field, keep the old one (@JsonAlias/deprecated) and remove after consumers migrate.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Applies the tolerant-reader principle and knows Boot's Jackson defaults differ from plain Jackson.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Believes Jackson fails on unknown fields by default in Boot, or sets @JsonIgnoreProperties(ignoreUnknown=true) everywhere without thinking.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Boot sets FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES=false, though plain Jackson defaults to true. Tolerant reader is right for microservices so additive changes do not break consumers.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes the trade-off: ignoring unknowns hides client typos on inputs, so strict mode may suit some public inputs. Recommends additive-only changes, not reusing field meaning, and consumer-driven contract tests (Pact or Spring Cloud Contract) to catch breaks. Verifies behaviour with a test.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines an organisation-wide compatibility policy (additive only, deprecation process) enforced via schema diff in CI. Aligns producer and consumer rules for events as well as REST. Documents which services run strict versus tolerant and why.
Do records work with Jackson, and what about @JsonCreator and single-arg constructors?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Jackson 2.12+ supports records natively (canonical constructor, accessor names without get). Spring Boot 3 requires 2.13+, fine. Records are immutable DTOs ideal for request/response bodies; annotations placed on record components propagate to the field/accessor/constructor parameter as the annotation target allows. Use @JsonCreator on a static factory when you need custom parsing or when a class has several constructors; a single-argument constructor can be ambiguous (delegating vs properties mode): mark it mode=PROPERTIES or DELEGATING. Compile with -parameters (or use jackson-module-parameter-names, included by the Boot starter) for classes without annotations. @JsonValue on an enum method controls the output string; add @JsonCreator to parse it back.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows how Jackson handles immutable types and construction, avoiding broken deserialisation.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Adds setters and a no-arg constructor to records or DTOs because 'Jackson needs them'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Jackson 2.12+ supports records natively using the canonical constructor. Records are ideal immutable DTOs, and annotations on components propagate. @JsonCreator on a static factory handles special cases.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Covers the single-argument constructor ambiguity (delegating vs properties mode) and fixes it with explicit mode, plus the -parameters flag or ParameterNamesModule for classes. Notes validation annotations on record components and tests round-trip with @JsonTest.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises on records for DTOs in the style guide and migration plan from Lombok-heavy classes. Adds shared tests or modules for Jackson config. Tracks Jackson major-version changes during Boot upgrades.
How do you configure date/time serialisation (ISO-8601) and the ObjectMapper in Boot?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Use java.time types; Boot registers JavaTimeModule and sets WRITE_DATES_AS_TIMESTAMPS=false, so Instant/OffsetDateTime serialise as ISO-8601 strings (2026-11-08T10:15:30Z). Send UTC instants in APIs; keep LocalDate for pure dates. Configure via properties (spring.jackson.serialization.write-dates-as-timestamps=false, spring.jackson.time-zone=UTC, spring.jackson.property-naming-strategy=SNAKE_CASE, spring.jackson.default-property-inclusion) or a Jackson2ObjectMapperBuilderCustomizer bean (Boot 1.4+). Defining your own ObjectMapper bean replaces Boot's (loses the defaults): prefer the customizer. In Boot 4 with Jackson 3 the customizer type changes (JsonMapperBuilderCustomizer; verify in release notes). Use @JsonFormat only for exceptions to the global rule.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Gets date/time contracts right: ISO-8601, UTC and time-type choice, and consistent ObjectMapper configuration.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Uses Date or LocalDateTime and sends epoch millis or local times with no zone, causing off-by-hours bugs.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Uses java.time. Boot registers JavaTimeModule and disables timestamp output, so Instant and OffsetDateTime serialise as ISO-8601. Sends UTC instants and uses LocalDate for pure dates. Configures via spring.jackson.* properties.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Warns that LocalDateTime has no zone and is ambiguous across clients, and that a hand-built new ObjectMapper() loses Boot's configuration, so inject the Boot mapper or use Jackson2ObjectMapperBuilder. Tests serialisation across DST and zone boundaries.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Publishes a time-handling standard (UTC on the wire, zone only at the UI edge) and a shared Jackson customiser used by all services, including for Kafka payloads. Adds lint rules against Date/LocalDateTime in API DTOs. Considers it in data-contract reviews.
What is @JsonView for, and what are its limitations compared with separate DTOs?
Answer

1. Model Answer

It tags fields with view classes and the controller picks a view (@JsonView(Views.Summary.class) on the handler) so one class can produce summary/detail shapes. Boot disables DEFAULT_VIEW_INCLUSION so unannotated fields are excluded when a view is active. Limitations: coupling of all shapes in one class, hard to document in OpenAPI (springdoc support is limited), easy to leak a field by forgetting the annotation, and no static type differs per use case. For L5 judgement: use separate records per use case (OrderSummary, OrderDetail), plus MapStruct; use @JsonView only for small, stable variations.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Evaluates a convenience feature against maintainability and documentation costs.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says '@JsonView lets you show different fields' and recommends it widely without drawbacks.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Views tag fields so one class gives summary and detail shapes, and Boot disables DEFAULT_VIEW_INCLUSION so unannotated fields are excluded. Limitations include coupling all shapes to one class and weaker OpenAPI support.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Prefers separate DTOs (records) for clarity, type safety and documentation, using @JsonView only for small, stable variations. Notes that forgetting the view annotation on a handler leaks all fields. Tests payload shapes.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets the guideline 'DTO per use case' and treats @JsonView as an exception requiring justification. Uses mapping tools such as MapStruct to keep DTO mapping cheap. Includes API shape in contract tests to catch accidental leaks.
List the main jakarta.validation constraints and show cascading validation of nested objects and collections.
Answer

1. Model Answer

@NotNull (not null), @NotEmpty (not null and size>0), @NotBlank (String with a non-whitespace char), @Size(min,max), @Min/@Max/@Positive/@PositiveOrZero/@DecimalMin, @Pattern(regexp), @Email, @Past/@Future, @Digits. Bean Validation 2.0 (2017) added container-element constraints (List<@NotBlank String>) and the @NotEmpty/@NotBlank/@Email family; Jakarta Bean Validation 3.0 (Boot 3) is the namespace move. Nested objects are validated only if the field has @Valid (cascade). Null is considered valid by most constraints except @NotNull/@NotBlank, so combine them.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Fluent with Bean Validation vocabulary, especially the null/empty/blank differences and cascading rules.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Uses @NotNull for everything, and is surprised nested objects or list elements are not validated.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: @NotNull, @NotEmpty, @NotBlank, @Size, @Min/@Max, @Positive, @Pattern, @Email, @Past/@Future. Cascading requires @Valid on nested fields and container-element constraints like List<@NotBlank String>.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Points out most constraints treat null as valid, so @NotNull is needed alongside @Size or @Email. Notes @Email is permissive, @Pattern regex can be a ReDoS risk, and the jakarta.validation package since Boot 3. Tests with a plain Validator unit test.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides shared composed constraints and a validation style guide so rules (e.g. id formats) are consistent across services. Keeps API validation in sync with OpenAPI schema constraints. Treats regex review as part of security checks.
Write a custom ConstraintValidator and use validation groups (create vs update).
Answer

1. Model Answer

Define the annotation (@Constraint(validatedBy=...), with message, groups, payload) and implement ConstraintValidator<A,T>. Validators are Spring-managed (can inject beans) when created by Spring's LocalValidatorFactoryBean; keep them free of slow I/O (a DB uniqueness check is better enforced by a unique constraint plus a 409). Groups let the same DTO have different rules: OnCreate requires no id, OnUpdate requires id and version; activate with @Validated(OnCreate.class). Cross-field rules: class-level constraint.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Builds custom validation that is correct, testable and kept in the right layer.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Writes a custom validator that calls the database to check uniqueness and treats that as sufficient.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Defines the annotation with message, groups, payload and @Constraint(validatedBy=...), then implements ConstraintValidator. Validators can inject Spring beans when created by LocalValidatorFactoryBean. Groups let create and update share a DTO with different rules.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes a DB uniqueness check in a validator is racy and slow, so enforce with a unique constraint and map the violation to 409. Keeps validators pure and fast, handles null explicitly, and weighs groups against separate create/update DTOs. Unit-tests validators in isolation.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Maintains a small library of vetted cross-cutting validators and discourages per-team variants. Prefers separate DTOs over groups for readability. Establishes the rule that race-free invariants live in the database.
What changes for validation and Jackson when moving Boot 2 to Boot 3?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Imports move javax.validation.* to jakarta.validation.* (same for javax.persistence, javax.servlet); use OpenRewrite UpgradeSpringBoot_3_0 or IDE migration. Hibernate Validator 8 implements Jakarta Validation 3.0 (needs spring-boot-starter-validation, which is no longer pulled in by the web starter since Boot 2.3: missing starter means constraints are silently ignored). Third-party libs (springfox, old swagger-annotations javax builds) break and must be replaced (springdoc 2.x). Jackson annotations keep their package. Other: ProblemDetail support, trailing-slash matching off by default in 6.0. See section 22 for the full checklist.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Plans a framework migration realistically, knowing the specific breakages and how to verify them.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'just change javax to jakarta' and expects it to work.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Imports move from javax.* to jakarta.* for validation, persistence and servlet. OpenRewrite UpgradeSpringBoot_3_0 helps. Hibernate Validator 8 implements Jakarta Validation 3.0, and spring-boot-starter-validation must be present.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes a missing validation starter means constraints are silently ignored, which is a quiet production risk, so adds tests that assert 400 on invalid input. Checks third-party libraries for Jakarta compatibility and Jackson module versions. Upgrades via 2.7 first, then 3.x, with a green test suite at each step.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Runs the migration as a programme: inventory of services, automated OpenRewrite recipes, a shared compatibility test suite, and a staged rollout with canaries. Tracks EOL dates for Boot lines to set priority. Publishes a migration guide and office hours.
Where should validation live: DTO constraints, service checks or the database? How do you map failures to the error contract?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Layered: (1) syntactic/format checks on DTOs at the edge (400 with field errors); (2) business rules in the service (e.g. "cannot cancel shipped order") throwing domain exceptions mapped to 409/422; (3) invariants as DB constraints (unique, FK, check, not null), the only thing that is race-free; map DataIntegrityViolationException to 409. Do not validate entities in controllers or rely on UI validation. Return all field errors at once in ProblemDetail errors[], never echo secrets from rejected values.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Places validation at the right layers and maps each failure type to the right status in the error contract.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Puts all validation in DTO annotations, or all in the controller, and trusts it to prevent bad data.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Layered: DTO constraints at the edge give 400 with field errors, business rules in the service raise domain exceptions mapped to 409 or 422, and DB constraints are the final race-free guard, with DataIntegrityViolationException mapped to 409.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that only DB constraints prevent concurrent violations, avoids duplicating rules inconsistently across layers, and keeps error messages free of internals. Tests each layer, including a concurrent insert test for the unique constraint.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines a validation policy and error taxonomy shared across services, backed by a common advice and exception hierarchy. Keeps constraint names mapped to friendly error codes. Reviews schema changes together with API validation to avoid drift.

29 · OpenAPI / Swagger: springdoc, annotations, code-first vs contract-first

Document and govern the REST contract: springdoc-openapi, Swagger annotations, spec in CI, client generation, mocks and AI context.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: The spec is the contract: springdoc code-first or openapi-generator contract-first, checked in CI.
  • Must-know: ★ springdoc 2.x for Boot 3, not springfox · ★ @Operation/@ApiResponse/@Schema · ★ spec in CI with breaking-change diff
  • Top pitfall: Swagger @RequestBody vs Spring @RequestBody name clash; Swagger UI exposed in prod.
  • Version markers: springdoc 2.0 (2022); OpenAPI 3.0/3.1

OpenAPI is the machine-readable contract of a REST service. The Spring ecosystem moved from Swagger 2 / springfox (abandoned about 2020, breaks on Boot 2.6+ because of the path-matching change and on Boot 3) to OpenAPI 3.x with springdoc-openapi.

OptionSpecBootNotesSince
springfox (io.swagger.annotations.@Api, @ApiOperation)Swagger 2.0up to 2.5last release 3.0.0 (2020); dead, do not start new work2015
springdoc-openapi 1.x (springdoc-openapi-ui)OpenAPI 3.02.xjavax namespace2019
springdoc-openapi 2.x (springdoc-openapi-starter-webmvc-ui, -webflux-ui) ★ MUST KNOWOpenAPI 3.0/3.13.xjakarta; /v3/api-docs, /swagger-ui.html2022 (2.0)
springdoc-openapi 3.xOpenAPI 3.1/3.2 (verify)4.x (verify)for Boot 4 / Framework 7 (verify in release notes)2025-26
🔑 KeyAnnotation names that clash: Spring's org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.RequestBody binds the HTTP body; Swagger's io.swagger.v3.oas.annotations.parameters.RequestBody only documents it. Use the fully-qualified Swagger one (or just rely on inference from the Spring annotation and the DTO). Swagger 2 annotations (io.swagger.annotations.*) vs OpenAPI 3 (io.swagger.v3.oas.annotations.*) are different packages.
