Resulting fragment of /v3/api-docs (trimmed). Note springdoc inferred required, maxLength and minItems from @NotBlank/@Size/@NotEmpty:

🧑‍💻 From my experienceKeeping an openapi.yaml plus request/response samples in the repository is exactly the kind of repository-level context engineering I do for Claude Code: the spec is a compact, unambiguous description of every endpoint that an AI assistant can read instead of guessing from controllers. The same spec can seed MockServer expectations, which I already use in hermetic component tests.

Worksheet

Swagger 2 (springfox) vs OpenAPI 3 vs springdoc: what is what, and which would you pick on Boot 3?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Swagger 2.0 is the old spec; springfox generated it from Spring code with @Api/@ApiOperation/@ApiModel, but was abandoned (last release 2020), relied on PathPatternParser internals and broke on Boot 2.6+ (spring.mvc.pathmatch.matching-strategy=ant_path_matcher workaround) and does not support Jakarta (Boot 3). OpenAPI 3.x is the current spec and io.swagger.v3.oas.annotations its annotation set. springdoc-openapi is the maintained generator: 1.x for Boot 2 (springdoc-openapi-ui), 2.x for Boot 3 (springdoc-openapi-starter-webmvc-ui, or -webflux-ui for reactive; -api variants without UI), and a 3.x line for Boot 4 (verify). Pick springdoc 2.x on Boot 3, or contract-first with openapi-generator if the spec must lead. Mapping of old to new: @Api to @Tag, @ApiOperation to @Operation, @ApiParam to @Parameter, @ApiModelProperty to @Schema, @ApiResponse stays (new package), @ApiIgnore to @Hidden.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows the API documentation ecosystem and picks maintained tooling for the current platform.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Conflates Swagger, OpenAPI and springfox and picks whatever a tutorial used.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Swagger 2.0 is the old spec, OpenAPI 3.x is the current one, springfox was abandoned (last release 2020), broke on Boot 2.6+ and lacks Jakarta support. springdoc-openapi is the maintained choice for Boot 3.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Selects springdoc for generated docs, with the right starter for MVC or WebFlux, and plans to remove springfox annotations in favour of io.swagger.v3 ones. Verifies the generated spec in CI rather than trusting the UI.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes the OpenAPI document a governed artifact: publishing it to a catalogue, linting it, and diffing it in CI. Sets the documentation tooling standard once for the organisation. Handles the migration of older services with codemods.
Which core Swagger annotations do you use and what does each document?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@OpenAPIDefinition(info=@Info(title,version,description), servers=@Server(url), security=...) once per app; @Tag groups operations; @Operation(summary, description, operationId, tags, security) per endpoint (set operationId explicitly: generated client method names derive from it); @ApiResponse/@ApiResponses with @Content(mediaType, schema=@Schema(implementation=...), examples=@ExampleObject); @Parameter(description, example, required, in=ParameterIn.QUERY|PATH|HEADER|COOKIE) and @Parameters; @Schema(description, example, requiredMode, accessMode=READ_ONLY, allowableValues, hidden, implementation) on models; @ArraySchema for collections; @Hidden to remove an endpoint or model; @SecurityScheme declares auth (type HTTP bearer, OAuth2 flows, apiKey) and @SecurityRequirement(name=...) applies it; @ParameterObject (springdoc) expands Pageable/filter objects into individual query parameters. Springdoc already infers most of this from Spring annotations and Jackson/validation, so annotate only for what inference cannot know: examples, descriptions, error responses.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Uses OpenAPI annotations to produce a spec that generated clients and readers can actually use.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Annotates every field with long descriptions but omits operation ids and response codes.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: @OpenAPIDefinition once per app, @Tag to group, @Operation for summary and operationId, @ApiResponse with @Content and @Schema for responses, @Parameter for inputs.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Sets operationId explicitly because generated client method names derive from it, documents error responses and security, and avoids noise by relying on inference for the obvious. Checks the result by generating a client or viewing the spec diff.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines annotation conventions and a spec linter ruleset so documentation quality is consistent. Moves repeated responses into global customisers. Measures spec quality via consumer feedback.
Show OpenAPI bean customisation, security schemes (bearer JWT, OAuth2) and GroupedOpenApi.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Declare a @Bean OpenAPI for global info, servers, components and security; use GroupedOpenApi to split the docs (public vs internal vs admin) each served at /v3/api-docs/{group}.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Configures global OpenAPI metadata and security schemes correctly so docs and generated clients can authenticate.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Leaves default docs and cannot show how to try secured endpoints.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Declares an OpenAPI bean for info, servers, components and security. Defines @SecurityScheme for bearer JWT and OAuth2, and uses GroupedOpenApi to split public, internal and admin docs under /v3/api-docs/{group}.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Applies security requirements globally or per operation so the lock icon is accurate, uses OAuth2 flows consistent with the real identity provider, and keeps internal or admin groups out of public docs. Verifies by trying authorisation in Swagger UI against a non-prod environment.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides a shared OpenAPI configuration starter with org-standard security schemes and server URLs. Controls which groups are published externally. Reviews doc exposure as part of security sign-off.
Which springdoc properties matter, and what do you do in production?
Answer

1. Model Answer

springdoc.api-docs.path (default /v3/api-docs), springdoc.api-docs.enabled=false and springdoc.swagger-ui.enabled=false in prod (reduces attack surface and startup/scan cost; publish the committed spec instead), springdoc.swagger-ui.path=/docs, springdoc.paths-to-match=/api/**, springdoc.packages-to-scan=com.acme.orders.api, springdoc.show-actuator=false (default; true documents actuator endpoints), springdoc.swagger-ui.operations-sorter=method, springdoc.default-produces-media-type. If you keep docs reachable in prod, protect them with Spring Security (permitAll only in dev/test profiles) because the spec exposes your full endpoint surface and schema. Hide internal endpoints with @Hidden, a separate group, or paths-to-exclude.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Operational awareness of documentation tooling: what to expose, where, and at what cost in production.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Leaves Swagger UI enabled in production because 'it is useful for debugging'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Key properties are springdoc.api-docs.path, springdoc.api-docs.enabled=false and springdoc.swagger-ui.enabled=false in prod, paths-to-match, packages-to-scan, and show-actuator. Publishes the committed spec instead.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the reasons: reduced attack surface and avoiding scan cost at startup. Restricts scanning to API packages for faster startup and cleaner docs, and uses profile-specific config to disable it. Verifies that the endpoints return 404 in prod.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes the production-off setting a platform default and tests it in a deployment checklist or policy check. Hosts docs centrally from the committed spec. Includes the exposure review in security audits.
How do you document ProblemDetail errors globally instead of repeating @ApiResponse on every method? What are OperationCustomizer and OpenApiCustomizer?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Two hooks: OperationCustomizer (io.swagger.v3.oas.models.Operation customize(Operation, HandlerMethod)) runs per operation; GlobalOpenApiCustomizer/OpenApiCustomizer runs once on the whole model. Register the ProblemDetail schema once under components, then add 400/401/403/500 responses referencing it to every operation (skip those with an existing response). @RestControllerAdvice methods with @ResponseStatus are also scanned by springdoc (springdoc.override-with-generic-response=true by default) which can add responses automatically, but explicit customizer output is more predictable.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Reduces documentation duplication through global customisation, and understands the two springdoc extension points.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Copies @ApiResponse(400/401/500) onto every method.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: OperationCustomizer runs per operation with the HandlerMethod, and OpenApiCustomizer runs once on the whole model. Registers the ProblemDetail schema once under components and adds common error responses referencing it.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Skips operations that already declare a response, only adds 401/403 where security applies, and keeps the documented errors aligned with what the advice actually returns. Verifies with a spec diff and a test that asserts the customiser output.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Ships the customisers in a shared starter so docs match the error contract for every service. Adds a CI check that the spec's error schema equals the runtime format. Treats documentation drift as a defect class.
How does springdoc infer schemas from records and validation annotations, and where does it get it wrong?
Answer

1. Model Answer

It reflects over the controller return/parameter types with the swagger-core ModelResolver, which uses Jackson introspection (so @JsonProperty/@JsonIgnore/@JsonInclude and records are respected) and translates Bean Validation constraints: @NotNull/@NotBlank/@NotEmpty to required, @Size to minLength/maxLength/minItems, @Min/@Max/@Positive to minimum/maximum, @Pattern to pattern, @Email to format: email (validation groups are ignored). Weak spots: generic wrappers (ResponseEntity<Page<T>> yields a huge Pageable/Page schema, so use your own envelope or PagedModel), polymorphism (@Schema(oneOf=...)), Optional semantics, custom serializers (spec says string, wire says object), and @JsonView. Verify the output; the spec is only as accurate as the DTOs.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands how schemas are inferred, so can spot and fix cases where the spec lies.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Assumes the generated schema is always right and never looks at it.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Springdoc uses swagger-core's ModelResolver over Jackson introspection, so records and Jackson annotations are respected. Bean Validation maps to required, minLength, minimum and similar.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Lists failure cases: polymorphism without @Schema(oneOf), generics, custom serialisers, @JsonView, nullable vs required confusion, and groups-based validation. Adds @Schema hints or custom converters, and verifies with a spec diff and generated-client compile.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats the spec as a tested contract: runs diff and lint in CI and uses contract tests for edge types. Sets rules for polymorphism and generics in API DTOs to keep the spec accurate. Tracks inference regressions on library upgrades.
Code-first vs contract-first: trade-offs, and how would you do contract-first with openapi-generator in Spring?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Code-first: write controllers, generate the spec (springdoc). Fast, no drift between code and spec at build, good for a single service; risk: the contract is an accident of implementation, breaking changes sneak in, and consumers wait for the code. Contract-first: review the YAML as the source of truth (design review, mock servers, parallel consumer work), then generate server stubs and clients; the compiler enforces the controller matches the contract. With openapi-generator-maven-plugin generator spring, options interfaceOnly=true (generate only the API interface and DTOs) and delegatePattern=true (generated controller delegates to your ApiDelegate) so hand-written code never gets overwritten; useSpringBoot3=true for jakarta, useTags=true, skipDefaultInterface. For teams with strict compatibility needs choose contract-first; for fast-moving single-owner services, code-first plus the committed spec and CI diff gives most of the benefit.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Weighs code-first against contract-first on governance, speed and drift, and can set up the contract-first pipeline.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'code-first is easier' or 'contract-first is better' without reasoning.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Code-first is fast with no build-time drift but the contract is accidental and breaking changes sneak in. Contract-first treats reviewed YAML as truth, enabling mock servers and parallel work, and uses openapi-generator (spring generator, interfaceOnly, delegate pattern) to produce interfaces.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Implements generated interfaces in controllers so compile errors flag drift, keeps generated code out of version control, and adds linting plus breaking-change detection. Chooses contract-first for public or multi-consumer APIs and code-first for a single small service.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets the org policy by API tier (public contract-first, internal either) with a central spec repository, review process and generator templates. Plans migration of existing services. Funds tooling and mentors teams on design reviews.
How do you generate the spec at build time, commit it, and detect breaking changes in CI?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Run the app during integration-test with spring-boot-maven-plugin start/stop goals and springdoc-openapi-maven-plugin (org.springdoc:springdoc-openapi-maven-plugin; Gradle: org.springdoc.openapi-gradle-plugin) which fetches /v3/api-docs into target/openapi.json; copy to api/openapi.yaml and commit it. A CI step regenerates and fails if git diff is non-empty (spec is stale), and runs openapi-diff (or oasdiff) of the PR spec against main/the released one to flag breaking changes: removed endpoint/field, new required request field, narrowed enum in a request, changed type, new response status the client cannot handle. Fail the build on breaking, allow with an explicit label. Spec drives: client SDK generation (generatorName=java with library=restclient/native, typescript-axios), documentation portals, gateway import, MockServer expectations and contract tests (e.g. mockserver-client openAPIExpectation), and AI context files.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Automates contract governance: generating the spec in CI and blocking breaking changes before they reach consumers.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Updates the Swagger doc by hand when someone remembers.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Runs the app during integration tests (start/stop goals), uses the springdoc Maven or Gradle plugin to fetch /v3/api-docs to a file, commits it, and fails CI if the regenerated spec differs from the committed one.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds a breaking-change check (such as openapi-diff or oasdiff) comparing against the main branch or last release, with an explicit override process. Keeps the generation deterministic (stable ordering) to avoid noisy diffs. Verifies the pipeline by intentionally breaking the contract in a test PR.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes spec generation, linting and breaking-change detection a reusable pipeline template for every service, with the spec published to a catalogue and consumer notification on change. Ties it to versioning and deprecation policy. Tracks the number of blocked breaking changes as a quality metric.
How would you use the OpenAPI spec for MockServer, contract tests and AI-assisted development?
Answer

1. Model Answer

MockServer: load the spec as expectations (mockServerClient.upsert(openAPIExpectation("api/openapi.yaml"))), giving consumer tests a hermetic stand-in that returns schema-valid responses, with specific scenarios overridden by hand-written expectations. Contract tests: validate real responses against the spec in provider tests (e.g. swagger-request-validator with MockMvc) so code cannot drift; consumers use the same spec (or Pact/Spring Cloud Contract, below). AI context: keep the spec, a short CLAUDE.md that names it, and 2-3 example payloads in the repo so Claude Code can generate controllers, clients and tests consistent with the contract; small curated samples beat dumping the whole spec. Caveat: review generated code, and never put secrets or real data in the samples.

🧑‍💻 From my experienceMy component tests used containerised Postgres + MockServer + Kafka. Seeding MockServer from the OpenAPI spec is a natural extension, and the same repository-level context (spec + samples) is what I feed Claude Code.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Sees the OpenAPI spec as a single source of truth that feeds mocks, provider verification and AI context, rather than as documentation that is generated once and left to rot.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'we generate Swagger docs and share the link' and maybe mentions that MockServer can return fake data. Cannot explain how the spec stops provider code from drifting, and treats AI-assisted development as 'paste the spec into the chat'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Loads the spec into MockServer with mockServerClient.upsert(openAPIExpectation("api/openapi.yaml")) so consumer tests get schema-valid responses, and validates real responses against the spec in provider tests with swagger-request-validator and MockMvc. Adds that the spec plus example payloads can be given to Claude Code to generate controllers and clients.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that the spec-generated expectations are only schema-valid, so scenarios such as 404, 429 or timeouts need hand-written expectations layered on top. Notes the provider-side validator is what turns the spec from a document into a failing build when code drifts. For AI use, keeps a short CLAUDE.md naming the spec plus 2-3 curated example payloads because small samples beat dumping the whole spec, and never puts secrets or real data in samples. Rule: generated code is always reviewed and the spec change is made first.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats the spec as a governed contract: spec-first PRs with breaking-change detection (an OpenAPI diff check in CI), one validator config shared across provider repos, and consumer-driven tools (Pact or Spring Cloud Contract) where consumers need to pin behaviour the spec cannot express. Sets a team convention for what goes in the repo for AI context (spec, samples, CLAUDE.md) and a data-hygiene checklist for samples. Ties it to their component tests (Postgres + MockServer + Kafka) as the lowest-cost place to prove the idea before standardising.

30 · Cross-cutting concerns for REST microservices

Gateway, outbound calls, identity and trace propagation, mTLS, discovery/config, DTOs, contract testing and degradation.

⚡ At a glance
  • What: Gateway, outbound calls, identity propagation, mTLS, config, DTOs, contracts and degradation.
  • Must-know: ★ timeouts + retry + circuit breaker · ★ JWT and trace id propagation · ★ graceful degradation
  • Top pitfall: MDC lost across threads; retries multiply load.
  • Version markers: RestClient 6.1; SSL bundles Boot 3.1; Micrometer Tracing Boot 3.0

The REST service itself is the easy part. The L5 questions are about what surrounds it: the gateway, outbound calls that must not hang, identity and correlation propagation, discovery and config on Kubernetes, contract testing, and degrading gracefully.

🧑‍💻 From my experienceIn the retail platform, aggregation endpoints used fork-and-join across downstream services with graceful degradation on partial failure: each branch has its own timeout, and the response says which parts are missing instead of failing the entire call. Field-level encryption was done with KMS at the gateway so persistence never stored plaintext.

Worksheet

What does an API gateway do for REST microservices and what should NOT be in it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Single entry point: routing/path rewriting, TLS termination (or mTLS), authentication (JWT validation), rate limiting, CORS, request/response header manipulation, correlation-id injection, canary/weighted routing, coarse caching. Spring Cloud Gateway (WebFlux/Netty based; in the 2025.0 release train the artifacts were renamed to spring-cloud-starter-gateway-server-webflux with a MVC variant; verify in release notes) defines routes with predicates and filters (RequestRateLimiter, CircuitBreaker, RewritePath, TokenRelay). Keep business logic and orchestration out of the gateway (it becomes a distributed monolith and a deployment bottleneck); keep it thin and stateless and keep authorisation decisions about resources inside the services (defence in depth). Alternatives: Envoy/Istio ingress, cloud gateways (AWS API Gateway), Kong.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Judges where cross-cutting edge concerns end and business logic begins, and can articulate why an overloaded gateway becomes a distributed monolith.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'the gateway routes requests to services and does authentication'. When asked what should not be in it, answers vaguely or suggests aggregating data from several services there because 'it is convenient'.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Lists the real responsibilities: routing and path rewriting, TLS termination, JWT validation, rate limiting, CORS, correlation-id injection, canary routing. Names Spring Cloud Gateway predicates and filters such as RequestRateLimiter, CircuitBreaker, RewritePath, TokenRelay, and states that business logic stays out.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains why business logic and orchestration in the gateway create a deployment bottleneck and a distributed monolith, and keeps it thin and stateless. Insists resource-level authorisation stays inside services as defence in depth, since JWT validation at the edge only says who the caller is. Notes the Redis-backed RequestRateLimiter adds a dependency to every request, so decide the fail-open or fail-closed behaviour deliberately. Mentions the 2025.0 artifact rename and says to verify it in release notes.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Frames the gateway as a platform product with a contract: a short list of allowed concerns, a review checklist that rejects PRs adding domain rules or cross-service joins, and per-route SLOs for latency added and error rate. Weighs Spring Cloud Gateway against Envoy/Istio ingress, Kong or AWS API Gateway on ownership, skills and cost. Pushes aggregation needs to a BFF owned by the consuming team, and plans route config rollout via GitOps with canary weights.
RestClient vs @HttpExchange vs OpenFeign vs WebClient for service-to-service calls. Set timeouts correctly.
Answer

1. Model Answer

RestClient (6.1) is the modern blocking fluent client replacing RestTemplate (maintenance mode); WebClient for reactive/streaming. @HttpExchange interfaces (6.0) generate a client proxy over a RestClient/WebClient via HttpServiceProxyFactory (Framework 7 adds @ImportHttpServices group config; verify). OpenFeign (Spring Cloud OpenFeign) gives the same declarative style plus Spring Cloud integration; it is in feature-complete/maintenance status (verify), so for new code prefer @HttpExchange. Always set connect and read timeouts: JDK/Apache defaults are infinite or very long, and a single hung dependency then exhausts your threads. Boot 3.4+: spring.http.client.connect-timeout / read-timeout configure the auto-configured builder; otherwise set them on the ClientHttpRequestFactory. Use the injected RestClient.Builder (not RestClient.create()) to keep observability and customizers.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Chooses the right HTTP client abstraction for current Spring and, more importantly, knows that missing timeouts are the real production hazard.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'we use RestTemplate or Feign' without knowing RestTemplate is in maintenance mode, and never mentions timeouts. Believes the defaults are fine.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Says RestClient (6.1) is the blocking fluent replacement for RestTemplate, WebClient is for reactive or streaming, and @HttpExchange interfaces are built via HttpServiceProxyFactory. Sets connect and read timeouts and uses spring.http.client.connect-timeout and read-timeout on Boot 3.4+.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains that JDK and Apache defaults are infinite or very long, so one hung dependency exhausts the thread pool and cascades. Injects RestClient.Builder rather than RestClient.create() to keep observability and customizers, and sets the timeouts on the ClientHttpRequestFactory otherwise. Prefers @HttpExchange over OpenFeign for new code since Feign is feature-complete and in maintenance (verify), and checks with a stub that sleeps past the read timeout. Rule: every outbound client has explicit timeouts shorter than the caller's deadline.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises it as a platform starter: one client-factory configuration with default timeouts, observation and metrics tags per client, and an ArchUnit rule banning new RestTemplate() and RestClient.create() in services. Plans a migration path from Feign and RestTemplate by module, using @HttpExchange interfaces so call sites do not change. Sets alerts on client-side timeout and pool-saturation metrics and teaches that a timeout is a budget decision, not a default.
How do you combine timeout, retry and circuit breaker on an outbound call, and which retries are safe?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Order (outermost to innermost, Resilience4j default aspect order): Retry( CircuitBreaker( RateLimiter( TimeLimiter( Bulkhead(call))))). Always bound with a timeout shorter than the caller's own deadline (propagate a deadline budget so retries cannot outlive the client). Retry only idempotent operations or those with an idempotency key, only on transient errors (IOException, 502/503/504, 429 with Retry-After), with exponential backoff + jitter and a small max (2-3), otherwise retries amplify an outage (retry storm, multiplying load by the attempt count at every tier). The circuit breaker opens on failure-rate/slow-call-rate, fails fast, then half-opens to probe. Provide a fallback or degrade (cached value, partial response). Count in your capacity maths: retries x layers.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Composes timeout, retry and circuit breaker in the correct order and reasons about retry amplification and idempotency under failure.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add @Retry and @CircuitBreaker and it handles failures'. Retries every exception, including POSTs, with a fixed delay, and cannot say what order the decorators run in.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: States the Resilience4j aspect order, Retry( CircuitBreaker( RateLimiter( TimeLimiter( Bulkhead(call))))), and that retry only applies to idempotent calls and transient errors with exponential backoff and jitter. Shows maxAttempts: 3, enableExponentialBackoff, enableRandomizedWait, and a CB with failureRateThreshold: 50.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains retry storms: with 3 attempts at each of several tiers the load multiplies, so retries x layers belongs in capacity maths. Restricts retries to IOException, 502/503/504 and 429 honouring Retry-After, or to operations with an idempotency key, and bounds total time with a timeout shorter than the caller's deadline. Notes the CB opens on failure-rate and slowCallDurationThreshold, then half-opens to probe, and that a fallback should degrade (cached or partial) rather than hide errors. Verifies with fault injection and the resilience4j metrics.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Pushes for a deadline budget propagated across services so retries cannot outlive the client, and for retrying at one layer only, usually the edge-most, to avoid multiplicative load. Standardises named profiles for retry and CB config in a shared library, with alerts on circuit state transitions and retry ratio and an SLO-linked review of fallbacks. Runs game days to confirm that a dependency outage degrades instead of cascading, and uses the question to teach idempotency keys.
How do you propagate the JWT and a correlation/trace id across service calls and into logs?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Trace/correlation: with Boot 3 add micrometer-tracing-bridge-otel (or brave); the auto-configured RestClient.Builder/WebClient.Builder/RestTemplateBuilder add the W3C traceparent header, and the Observation API puts traceId/spanId into the SLF4J MDC; log pattern %X{traceId} (Boot 3.2 adds logging.pattern.correlation). Also accept/return a business X-Request-Id via a servlet filter that sets MDC and clears it in finally. MDC is thread-local: it is lost across @Async/executors and fork-join threads unless you wrap the executor (ContextPropagatingTaskDecorator, Micrometer context-propagation) which matters for fork-and-join aggregation. JWT: for user-context calls forward the caller's bearer token (ClientHttpRequestInterceptor reading SecurityContextHolder, or TokenRelay at the gateway); for service identity use client-credentials with the OAuth2 client so downstreams can distinguish user from service (Security 6.4 adds an OAuth2ClientHttpRequestInterceptor; verify in release notes). Do not forward tokens to third parties.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands how trace context, MDC and security context actually travel (and fail to travel) across HTTP hops and threads, and separates user identity from service identity.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'add a request id header and log it' or 'Sleuth does it'. Does not know MDC is thread-local and assumes the JWT is automatically passed on outgoing calls.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Adds micrometer-tracing-bridge-otel so the auto-configured RestClient.Builder or WebClient.Builder adds the W3C traceparent header and traceId lands in the MDC, shown with %X{traceId} in the log pattern. Forwards the bearer token with a ClientHttpRequestInterceptor reading SecurityContextHolder.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Stresses that the builder must be the injected one or tracing is silently lost, and that the MDC is thread-local so it vanishes across @Async, executors and fork-join aggregation unless wrapped with ContextPropagatingTaskDecorator or Micrometer context-propagation. Distinguishes forwarding the user token from client-credentials service identity so downstreams can tell user from service, and never forwards tokens to third parties. Sets a servlet filter for X-Request-Id that clears MDC in finally. Verifies by checking the same traceId in logs of two services.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats propagation as a platform guarantee: a shared starter that configures the builder, the executor decorators and the log pattern, with a contract test that a call through two services keeps one traceId. Defines a token-forwarding policy (audience restriction, token exchange for downstreams, no forwarding outside the trust boundary) and reviews it with security. Tracks the percentage of requests with a complete trace as an observability SLO, and checks the Security 6.4 OAuth2 interceptor against release notes before adopting it.
How do you do mTLS between services in Spring Boot?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Server requires client certs: Boot 3.1 SSL bundles (spring.ssl.bundle.pem.server.keystore.certificate/private-key, truststore.certificate), server.ssl.bundle=server and server.ssl.client-auth=need. Client: RestClientSsl/WebClientSsl (Boot 3.2) restClientBuilder.apply(ssl.fromBundle("client")). The CN/SAN of the client certificate can be mapped to a principal with Spring Security http.x509(...) for authorisation. Operations: short-lived certs and rotation (bundles can reload with spring.ssl.bundle.watch.file.quiet-period), usually issued by cert-manager/SPIFFE; or offload to a mesh (Istio/Linkerd sidecar mTLS) so apps stay plain HTTP inside the pod. Trade-off: mesh gives uniform policy and rotation but hides identity from the app unless you read forwarded headers. Pair mTLS (who is the service) with JWT (which user/permissions).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows how to implement mTLS in Boot and, more importantly, how to operate it: rotation, identity mapping, and when to push it to a mesh.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'you configure a keystore and truststore in application.yml' and cannot explain client authentication or what happens when certificates expire.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Uses Boot 3.1 SSL bundles with spring.ssl.bundle.pem, sets server.ssl.bundle and server.ssl.client-auth=need, and on the client uses RestClientSsl or WebClientSsl with ssl.fromBundle("client"). Maps the cert CN/SAN to a principal with http.x509(...).
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Covers operations: short-lived certs from cert-manager or SPIFFE and automatic reload through spring.ssl.bundle.watch.file.quiet-period, since expiry is the classic outage. Compares a mesh (Istio or Linkerd sidecar) giving uniform policy and rotation but hiding the peer identity from the app unless forwarded headers are read. Pairs mTLS (which service) with JWT (which user and permissions). Verifies with a call lacking a client cert failing at handshake and an expiry alert.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Decides per estate: mesh mTLS as the default on Kubernetes, app-level bundles only where there is no mesh or where the app needs the identity for authorisation. Defines a CA hierarchy, certificate lifetime and rotation SLO, with alerts on days-to-expiry and handshake failure rate. Writes a runbook for emergency rotation and tests it in a game day, and keeps the trust-store contents under review so a leaked key has a bounded blast radius.
Service discovery and configuration: Eureka + Config Server vs Kubernetes Service DNS + ConfigMaps/Secrets?
Answer

1. Model Answer

On Kubernetes the platform already does discovery (Service DNS orders-svc.ns.svc.cluster.local, kube-proxy/mesh load balancing, readiness gating) and config (ConfigMap/Secret as env vars or mounted files, spring.config.import=optional:configtree:/etc/config/ or kubernetes:). Adding Eureka and Config Server then means two more stateful components to run; choose them only for non-K8s or hybrid estates. Config Server (git-backed, spring.config.import=configserver:, /actuator/refresh or Bus) gives versioning and encryption of properties; on K8s do the same with GitOps (Argo CD) rendering ConfigMaps from git and an external secrets operator for secrets. Note ConfigMap env changes need a rollout; mounted files can refresh with a watcher. Twelve-factor: config in the environment, not in the image; dev/prod parity; logs to stdout; stateless processes; disposability (fast start and graceful shutdown: server.shutdown=graceful).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Decides between a Spring Cloud stack and platform-native Kubernetes primitives, and avoids running redundant infrastructure.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Eureka is for service discovery and Config Server is for config' and would add both everywhere, without noticing Kubernetes already provides these.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains that on Kubernetes Service DNS (orders-svc.ns.svc.cluster.local) and readiness gating already do discovery, and ConfigMaps and Secrets are consumed as env vars or via spring.config.import=optional:configtree:/etc/config/. Eureka and Config Server only make sense in non-K8s or hybrid estates.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Points out that Eureka and Config Server are two more stateful components to run and secure. Notes the practical gotchas: ConfigMap env var changes need a rollout while mounted files can refresh with a watcher, and /actuator/refresh or Bus is the Config Server equivalent. Replaces Config Server versioning with GitOps (Argo CD rendering ConfigMaps from git) and an external secrets operator. Ties to twelve-factor, including server.shutdown=graceful for disposability.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Frames it as removing platform duplication: an inventory of which services still depend on Eureka or Config Server, a migration plan by service tier with parallel running, and a cost/ops comparison. Sets a standard that config comes from git via GitOps with audit trail and secrets never in ConfigMaps. Defines an exception process for hybrid or legacy cases and measures config-related incidents and rollout time before and after.
Why separate DTOs from entities, and how does MapStruct fit?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Entities model persistence (lazy associations, bidirectional links, columns, internal flags); DTOs model the wire contract. Exposing entities couples the API to the schema, risks leaking fields (mass-assignment/over-posting on write, PII on read), triggers LazyInitializationException or N+1 during serialisation and makes backward-compatible evolution impossible. Use request DTOs (commands) and response DTOs (records), mapped at the service boundary. MapStruct generates plain mapping code at compile time (annotation processor, no reflection, errors at build time): @Mapper(componentModel="spring"), @Mapping(target=..., source=...), @MappingTarget for updates, unmappedTargetPolicy=ERROR to force you to handle new fields. Put it in the build after Lombok with lombok-mapstruct-binding. Spring Data projections can replace DTO mapping for read-only queries.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Explains why entities must not cross the API boundary and uses MapStruct's compile-time guarantees to make that boundary safe to evolve.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'DTOs are cleaner' or 'it is best practice' and treats MapStruct as a convenient copy tool. Cannot name a concrete failure from returning entities directly.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Names the failures: coupling the API to the schema, leaking fields (over-posting on write, PII on read), LazyInitializationException or N+1 during serialisation, and no backward-compatible evolution. Uses request DTOs as commands and response records, with MapStruct @Mapper(componentModel="spring") and @Mapping.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains MapStruct generates plain code at compile time with no reflection so mapping errors fail the build, and sets unmappedTargetPolicy=ERROR so a new entity field forces a decision. Uses @MappingTarget for updates and ignores id and version on toEntity so clients cannot set them. Orders it after Lombok with lombok-mapstruct-binding, and uses Spring Data projections for read-only queries instead of map-after-load.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises the boundary: mapping happens in the service layer, an ArchUnit rule forbids @Entity types in controller signatures, and the compiler option for unmapped targets is set in the parent POM. Considers cost, noting mappers add boilerplate and that projections or records can remove a layer for read paths. Uses the over-posting example in code review to teach juniors why a missing mapper is a security issue.
How do you do API contract testing: Spring Cloud Contract vs Pact vs spec-based with MockServer?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Consumer-driven (Pact): consumer tests record expectations into a pact file; the provider verifies it in CI (@Provider, @PactBroker); polyglot, great when consumers are many teams, with a broker and can-i-deploy. Spring Cloud Contract: provider-owned contracts (Groovy/YAML DSL) generate provider tests and publish a WireMock stubs jar for consumers (@AutoConfigureStubRunner); best when both sides are Spring/JVM. Spec-based: OpenAPI as the contract, validate provider responses against it and run consumers against MockServer/Prism generated from it; lowest ceremony, weakest on behavioural detail. Choose by who owns the contract; combine with CI breaking-change detection. Keep a few hermetic component tests (Testcontainers Postgres + MockServer + Kafka) for wiring, as in the retail platform, and avoid shared long-lived integration environments for correctness.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Chooses a contract-testing strategy by who owns the contract and how many consumers exist, and places it correctly relative to component tests.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'contract testing is when we test the API' and confuses it with integration tests against a shared environment. Cannot distinguish Pact from Spring Cloud Contract.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Describes Pact as consumer-driven with a pact file verified by the provider (@Provider, @PactBroker), Spring Cloud Contract as provider-owned contracts generating tests and a WireMock stubs jar via @AutoConfigureStubRunner, and spec-based testing with OpenAPI and MockServer or Prism.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Chooses by ownership: Pact for many consumer teams and polyglot with a broker and can-i-deploy, Spring Cloud Contract when both sides are Spring/JVM, spec-based for the lowest ceremony but weakest on behavioural detail. Combines it with CI breaking-change detection. Keeps a few hermetic Testcontainers component tests (Postgres + MockServer + Kafka) for wiring and avoids long-lived shared integration environments for correctness.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Treats contracts as a deployment gate: can-i-deploy in the pipeline, a pact retention and versioning policy, and a rule that provider changes without verification cannot ship. Starts with the highest-risk integrations rather than mandating everywhere, to control cost. Measures contract-caused production incidents and flaky shared-env failures to justify the approach, and trains teams to write consumer expectations that pin only what they actually use.
How do you implement idempotent consumers and keep REST commands safe when called from Kafka or retried clients?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Delivery is at-least-once, so the handler must be a no-op on duplicates. Pattern: processed-message table with a unique key (message_id or business key) inserted in the same DB transaction as the side effect; duplicate key means skip and ack. Alternatives: naturally idempotent operations (upsert by id, PUT, set-state-to-X), version checks (apply only if event version = current+1), and the outbox pattern on the producer side for atomic "write + publish". For REST commands use the Idempotency-Key design above. Failure mode: dedupe in memory or Redis without the DB transaction leaves a window where the side effect committed but the key was not saved (or vice versa), causing duplicates or lost work. For Kafka-based eventing (as in the leave system), consumers must tolerate redelivery and reordering.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designs for at-least-once delivery by making the dedupe and the side effect atomic, and can name the exact window where naive dedupe fails.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'check if we already processed the message' and keeps a HashSet or a Redis key. Does not notice that crashes between the side effect and the key write cause duplicates or lost work.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Uses a processed-message table with a unique key inserted in the same DB transaction as the side effect, e.g. INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING, skipping and acking on a duplicate. Mentions upserts, PUT and set-state operations as naturally idempotent.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the failure mode: dedupe in memory or Redis outside the DB transaction leaves a window where the effect committed but the key was not saved, or the reverse, causing duplicates or lost work. Adds version checks (apply only if event version = current+1) for reordering and the outbox pattern on the producer side for atomic write and publish. Uses the Idempotency-Key for REST commands. Tests by replaying the same event twice and by killing the consumer between steps.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets this as a platform pattern: a shared idempotency starter with the table schema, a retention job so the dedupe table does not grow unbounded, and a review checklist item for every new consumer. Monitors duplicate-skip rate and consumer lag, and agrees a DLT and replay procedure. Notes it generalises beyond Kafka and uses their leave-system eventing as the worked example when mentoring.
Health and readiness for a REST service on Kubernetes, and graceful shutdown.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Boot 2.3+ exposes /actuator/health/liveness and /readiness (auto-enabled on K8s; management.endpoint.health.probes.enabled=true elsewhere). Liveness = "process is not wedged" and must not include dependencies (a DB outage would restart every pod: cascade). Readiness = "can take traffic" and may include critical dependencies via management.endpoint.health.group.readiness.include=readinessState,db (think carefully: an optional dependency should not unready the pod). Graceful shutdown: server.shutdown=graceful + spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase=30s, a preStop sleep so endpoints deregister before SIGTERM handling, and terminationGracePeriodSeconds above that. Secure actuator, expose only health/info/metrics/prometheus.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Separates liveness from readiness correctly and understands how shutdown ordering interacts with Kubernetes endpoint removal.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'we expose /actuator/health and Kubernetes pings it'. Puts the database in the same check for everything.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Uses /actuator/health/liveness and /readiness (management.endpoint.health.probes.enabled=true outside K8s). Sets server.shutdown=graceful and spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase=30s.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Keeps dependencies out of liveness because a DB outage would restart every pod and cascade, and puts only critical ones in readiness through management.endpoint.health.group.readiness.include=readinessState,db, leaving optional dependencies out. Adds a preStop sleep so endpoints deregister before SIGTERM handling, and terminationGracePeriodSeconds above the shutdown timeout. Exposes only health, info, metrics and prometheus and secures actuator. Verifies by rolling a deployment under load and watching for 5xx.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises probe config and shutdown timings in a base chart and shared starter, with a rollout test in CI or staging that fails on dropped requests. Tracks readiness flaps and restart counts as signals, and reviews which dependencies are allowed into readiness. Teaches the cascade scenario as a postmortem pattern and ties grace periods to the longest legitimate request.
Design graceful degradation for an aggregation endpoint that fans out to several services.
Answer

1. Model Answer

Fork the independent calls concurrently (CompletableFuture with a bounded executor, virtual threads on Java 21+, or Reactor Mono.zip), each with its own timeout and circuit breaker, then join with an overall deadline. Classify branches as critical (fail the request, 502/503) or optional (substitute a default/cached/empty value). Return 200 with a partial marker ("unavailable":["recommendations"], or a custom header) so clients can render what they have; never cache a degraded response as if it were complete. Mind: a bounded thread pool per dependency (bulkhead) so a slow service cannot starve others, MDC/security context propagation into worker threads, and metrics per branch so degradation is visible, not silent. Test it with MockServer injecting delays and 5xx.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Designs a fan-out endpoint that degrades predictably, with per-branch budgets, critical-versus-optional classification, and visible partial results.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Calls services one after another and wraps the whole thing in a try/catch returning 500, or says 'use CompletableFuture' without timeouts or any notion of partial results.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Forks independent calls with CompletableFuture on a bounded executor (or virtual threads, or Mono.zip), each with its own timeout like orTimeout(500, MILLISECONDS) and exceptionally fallbacks, then joins under an overall deadline.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Classifies branches as critical (fail with 502/503) or optional (default, cached or empty), and returns 200 with a partial marker such as unavailable or a header so clients can render what they have. Never caches a degraded response as if complete. Uses a bounded pool per dependency as a bulkhead, propagates MDC and security context into workers, and records metrics per branch so degradation is visible. Tests with MockServer delays and 5xx.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Defines the degradation contract with product: which fields are optional, what the client shows, and the SLO for partial responses. Ensures the overall deadline is below the gateway timeout and retries do not multiply load. Provides a reusable aggregator helper plus a dashboard of per-branch degradation rate, and challenges whether the fan-out belongs in a BFF or a precomputed read model.
🧑‍💻 From my experienceThis is the pattern I used for the aggregation: partial failure returns what is available. Combined with optimistic locking for writes and hermetic MockServer-based failure injection in component tests.

31 · Life of a REST request: filters, interceptors, controller, service, repository

One request, every layer, in order, with the owner of each piece: Servlet API, Spring Security, Spring MVC, Spring AOP, Spring Data, JPA/Hibernate, JDBC.

⚡ At a glance
  • Order in: Tomcat thread → Servlet Filters → DispatcherServlet → HandlerMapping → Interceptor.preHandle → argument resolvers + JSON conversion + @Valid → Controller → Service (AOP proxy, @Transactional) → Repository → Hibernate → JDBC → DB.
  • Order out: reverse. Return value → HttpMessageConverter → postHandle → afterCompletion → filters (code after chain.doFilter).
  • Who owns what: Filter = Servlet API (Jakarta EE). Interceptor, DispatcherServlet, @ControllerAdvice = Spring MVC. Security filters = Spring classes plugged into the servlet filter chain. @Transactional = Spring AOP/tx.
  • Top pitfall: exceptions thrown in a filter never reach @ControllerAdvice (that lives inside DispatcherServlet).

Execution order with the main methods (sequence view)

Read top to bottom: each number is one step in time. Solid arrows go into the application; green dashed arrows are the return trip, which runs in reverse order. Orange notes are work done inside one component. The method names are the real framework methods you can set a breakpoint on.

Main methods per class (in the order they are called)

ComponentOwnerMain methods (call order)You usually override / implement

🧠 RememberFilters wrap everything (like onion layers: in order, out reverse). Inside the middle layer, DispatcherServlet.doDispatch() runs: mapping → preHandle → adapter (args, JSON, validation) → controller → postHandle → afterCompletion. Under the controller the service proxy owns the transaction.
