Step by step

  1. Connector and thread. Tomcat accepts the TLS connection, parses HTTP and takes a worker thread (200 by default; or a virtual thread with spring.threads.virtual.enabled=true).
  2. Filter chain. Filters run in order (@Order, Ordered, or FilterRegistrationBean#setOrder). Each filter does work, calls chain.doFilter(req, res), then (optionally) does work on the way back. A filter can stop the chain by not calling it (for example, return 401).
  3. DispatcherServlet. It asks the HandlerMapping for a HandlerExecutionChain (the controller method plus its interceptors).
  4. Interceptors preHandle in registration order. Returning false ends processing; earlier interceptors still get afterCompletion.
  5. HandlerAdapter. Argument resolvers fill the parameters (@PathVariable, @RequestHeader, @RequestParam); @RequestBody is deserialised by a Jackson HttpMessageConverter; @Valid triggers Bean Validation, failing with MethodArgumentNotValidException.
  6. Controller → service → repository. The service bean is a proxy: @Transactional opens a transaction (borrowing a Hikari connection when the first SQL needs it); the Spring Data proxy calls EntityManager; Hibernate flushes SQL through JDBC.
  7. Back out. The service returns, the transaction commits (or rolls back on a runtime exception), the controller returns ResponseEntity, the return-value handler writes JSON through an HttpMessageConverter (ResponseBodyAdvice can alter it first), then postHandle and afterCompletion run in reverse order, and finally control returns through the filters.
  8. Errors. Inside DispatcherServlet a HandlerExceptionResolver chain turns exceptions into responses (@ExceptionHandler in @ControllerAdvice first, then ResponseStatusException, then Spring’s defaults). Exceptions escaping the filters go to the container’s error dispatch (/error, BasicErrorController in Boot).

Code: one of each

🧑‍💻 From my experienceIn my current multi-module project the gateway registers its request filters through FilterRegistrationBean in a config class, with URL patterns and an order that come from configuration. A request-scoped filter extends a shared base class that itself extends OncePerRequestFilter: so those are servlet filters that run before DispatcherServlet, and an exception thrown there bypasses any controller advice (the project maps errors with its own exception-mapper bean). The controllers rely on @RequestHeader + @NotBlank + @Validated (an AOP proxy), and persistence goes through @Transactional repository implementations with @Version. I did not find any HandlerInterceptor in the gateway: cross-cutting work is done in filters.
⚠ Pitfalls(1) Filter exceptions skip @ControllerAdvice. (2) postHandle is useless for @ResponseBody endpoints: the body is already written, so you cannot change it (use ResponseBodyAdvice). (3) The request body is a stream: read it in a filter and the controller gets nothing unless you wrap it. (4) ThreadLocals (MDC, SecurityContext) do not follow you into @Async/reactive threads. (5) spring.jpa.open-in-view (default true) registers an interceptor that keeps the EntityManager open for the whole request; turn it off in REST services.
💡 Tip: where does it go?Needs raw HTTP or must apply to every request (auth, CORS, correlation id, compression, rate limit) → filter. Needs to know which controller method will run (per-handler audit, timing by handler) → interceptor. Needs the method arguments/return value or a transaction → AOP. Needs to shape an error or the response body → @ControllerAdvice / ResponseBodyAdvice.

Worksheet 32

Walk me through what happens to POST /v1/orders from the moment it reaches the server until the row is saved, naming each layer.
Answer

1. Model Answer

(1) Tomcat accepts the connection and assigns a request thread. (2) The servlet filter chain runs in order (framework filters, Spring Security’s FilterChainProxy, your filters). (3) DispatcherServlet asks the HandlerMapping for the controller method and its interceptors. (4) Interceptors’ preHandle. (5) The HandlerAdapter resolves arguments, Jackson converts the JSON body to a DTO, @Valid validates it. (6) The controller calls the service, which is a proxy: @Transactional begins a transaction. (7) The repository (Spring Data proxy) calls the EntityManager; Hibernate generates SQL and runs it via JDBC/Hikari. (8) The transaction commits when the service method returns; the controller returns a ResponseEntity; the message converter writes JSON; postHandle/afterCompletion run; the response travels back out through the filters.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Traces a request end to end through the servlet container, Spring MVC, proxies and persistence layers, and places each concern at the right layer.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'the controller calls the service, which calls the repository, which saves to the database' and skips filters, DispatcherServlet and transactions.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Walks the order: Tomcat thread, servlet filter chain including FilterChainProxy, DispatcherServlet finding the handler via HandlerMapping, interceptor preHandle, argument resolution with Jackson and @Valid, the controller, the @Transactional service proxy, repository, EntityManager, Hibernate SQL via JDBC/Hikari, then commit and the response.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains the ordering consequences: filters run before the DispatcherServlet so security and logging see requests that never reach MVC, validation failures occur before the transaction starts, the commit happens when the service method returns (so a commit failure can surface after the controller logic), and afterCompletion runs even on exception. Knows where to look: a breakpoint in DispatcherServlet.doDispatch, logging.level.org.springframework.web, and SQL logging.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Uses the lifecycle as a map for placing concerns: authentication in the filter chain, correlation id in a filter, cross-cutting request rules in interceptors, business invariants in the service transaction. Points to failure modes at each hop (thread pool exhaustion, Hikari pool wait, commit-time constraint errors) and the metric for each. Uses it as an onboarding exercise and an ArchUnit-backed layering rule.
What is the difference between a Filter and a HandlerInterceptor, and which framework does each belong to?
Answer

1. Model Answer

A Filter is part of the Servlet API (jakarta.servlet): the container runs it around the whole servlet, before DispatcherServlet, for every request, and it can wrap or replace the request/response objects. A HandlerInterceptor is part of Spring MVC: it runs inside DispatcherServlet, only for requests that matched a handler, and receives the HandlerMethod, but it cannot wrap the request and has postHandle/afterCompletion hooks tied to MVC. Spring ships filters too (OncePerRequestFilter, Spring Security): these are Spring classes that implement the servlet Filter interface and are plugged into the container’s chain.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Places Filter (Servlet API) versus HandlerInterceptor (Spring MVC) on the correct side of the DispatcherServlet and knows what each can and cannot do.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'both intercept requests before the controller; filters are older'. Cannot say which has access to the handler or which can wrap the request.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains a Filter is jakarta.servlet, run by the container around the whole servlet before DispatcherServlet for every request, and can wrap or replace request and response. A HandlerInterceptor runs inside DispatcherServlet only for matched handlers, receives the HandlerMethod, and has postHandle/afterCompletion.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Draws the decision rule: need to wrap the request or touch requests that never reach MVC (static resources, 404s, security) means a filter; need handler metadata (annotations on the HandlerMethod) means an interceptor, which cannot wrap the request. Notes Spring's OncePerRequestFilter and Spring Security filters are Spring classes implementing the servlet Filter interface and plugged into the container chain. Verifies by logging in both and calling a nonexistent path.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Writes a short placement guide for the team: cross-cutting transport concerns in filters, handler-aware concerns in interceptors, with an example of each in the repo. Adds a review check that interceptors are not used for security or body manipulation. Considers the WebFlux equivalent so shared libraries do not assume servlet types.
An exception is thrown inside a servlet filter. Will your @RestControllerAdvice handle it? Why, and what are your options?
Answer

1. Model Answer

No. @ControllerAdvice is applied by DispatcherServlet’s HandlerExceptionResolvers, and the filter runs before and outside it. The exception propagates to the container, which performs an error dispatch to /error (Boot’s BasicErrorController, producing the default JSON error). Options: (1) catch the exception in the filter and write the response yourself (status, JSON body, Content-Type); (2) inject the HandlerExceptionResolver bean (@Qualifier("handlerExceptionResolver")) and delegate: resolver.resolveException(req, res, null, ex), so the same advice formats the error; (3) for Spring Security failures configure AuthenticationEntryPoint and AccessDeniedHandler; (4) customise the /error response with an ErrorAttributes or error controller.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows @ControllerAdvice only covers exceptions inside DispatcherServlet and can route filter errors to the same formatting.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'yes, @RestControllerAdvice handles all exceptions in the app' and is surprised by the default error page from a filter failure.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Says no: advice is applied by DispatcherServlet's HandlerExceptionResolvers while the filter runs outside it, so the exception goes to the container which dispatches to /error (Boot's BasicErrorController). Options include catching and writing the response in the filter.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Offers the clean fix: inject @Qualifier("handlerExceptionResolver") HandlerExceptionResolver and call resolver.resolveException(req, res, null, ex) so the same advice formats the error. For Spring Security failures configure AuthenticationEntryPoint and AccessDeniedHandler, and customise /error via ErrorAttributes or an error controller. Tests with MockMvc including the filter, asserting body shape.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Aims at one error contract: advice for MVC, a shared resolver delegate for filters, and security handlers producing the same ProblemDetail. Adds a contract test covering a filter-thrown, security-thrown and controller-thrown error. Documents the pattern in a shared starter so teams do not each hand-write JSON in filters.
What is the execution order of multiple filters and multiple interceptors, including the “way back”?
Answer

1. Model Answer

Filters: ordered by @Order/Ordered or FilterRegistrationBean#setOrder (lowest value first). Code before chain.doFilter runs in that order; code after it runs in reverse. Spring Security’s chain is registered at order −100, so it runs before default-ordered custom filters. Interceptors: preHandle in registration order; postHandle and afterCompletion in reverse order. If interceptor 2’s preHandle returns false, interceptor 3 and the controller are skipped, and afterCompletion runs only for interceptor 1 (those whose preHandle returned true).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Predicts execution order of filters and interceptors in both directions, including the short-circuit behaviour of preHandle returning false.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'they run in the order you add them' and has no idea about the return path or what happens when one stops the chain.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Filters run lowest @Order/FilterRegistrationBean#setOrder first with code after chain.doFilter in reverse. Interceptors run preHandle in registration order, postHandle and afterCompletion in reverse.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Adds the specifics: Spring Security's chain is registered at order -100 so it runs before default-ordered custom filters, and if interceptor 2's preHandle returns false, interceptor 3 and the controller are skipped and afterCompletion runs only for interceptor 1. Uses this to put cleanup like MDC clearing in finally or afterCompletion. Verifies with a log line in each hook.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Makes ordering explicit: named order constants in one class instead of magic numbers, and a test that asserts the filter chain order. Reviews new filters against Security's position, and avoids relying on implicit ordering across shared libraries. Uses a diagram of the in/out path in onboarding to cut ordering-related incidents.
Why is postHandle of limited use in a REST API, and where would you modify a response body instead?
Answer

1. Model Answer

For @ResponseBody/ResponseEntity handlers the return value is written by the message converter during handler invocation, so by the time postHandle runs the response may already be committed and headers or body cannot be changed (postHandle was designed for view rendering with a ModelAndView). To change the body use a ResponseBodyAdvice (@ControllerAdvice implementing it, e.g. a response envelope), to add headers do it in a filter before chain.doFilter or in the controller, and to observe the outcome use afterCompletion (status and exception are available, body is not).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Understands that response writing happens during handler invocation for REST, so postHandle cannot reliably change the response, and picks the correct hook per goal.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'use postHandle to modify the response' because it sounds like the hook for it, and cannot say why it silently does nothing.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains for @ResponseBody/ResponseEntity the message converter writes the body during handler invocation, so by postHandle the response may be committed; postHandle was designed for ModelAndView view rendering.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Maps goals to hooks: change the body with ResponseBodyAdvice in a @ControllerAdvice (e.g. a response envelope), add headers in a filter before chain.doFilter or in the controller, and observe the outcome in afterCompletion, where status and exception are available but the body is not. Verifies with a test that sets a header in postHandle and asserts it is missing.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets guidance in the engineering handbook so envelopes and headers are applied in one place, and flags postHandle header edits in review. Questions whether an envelope is worth the cost for clients and generated specs. Uses Observation for timing rather than custom hooks.
A filter logs the request body, and now the controller receives an empty body. What happened and how do you fix it?
Answer

1. Model Answer

The body is an InputStream that can be consumed once. The filter read it, so the later @RequestBody conversion sees EOF (and throws HttpMessageNotReadableException). Fix: wrap the request in a ContentCachingRequestWrapper (caches bytes as they are read by the controller; log after chain.doFilter) or a custom caching wrapper that supports re-reading (needed if you must read before the controller, e.g. signature checks). Trade-offs: memory use for large payloads (cap the cached size) and logging PII; never log secrets, mask sensitive fields.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Recognises the single-read InputStream problem and chooses the right request wrapper while weighing memory and PII.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'the filter breaks the body' and tries reading it again or storing it in a static variable.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains the body is an InputStream consumed once, so the controller's @RequestBody conversion sees EOF and throws HttpMessageNotReadableException. Fixes with ContentCachingRequestWrapper, logging after chain.doFilter.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Distinguishes the two cases: ContentCachingRequestWrapper caches bytes as the controller reads them, so log afterwards; a custom re-readable wrapper is needed if the filter must read first, such as signature checks. Caps cached size for large payloads and masks sensitive fields since logging bodies risks PII and secrets. Tests with a large body and with a POST to confirm the controller still binds.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Questions body logging at all: prefer structured metadata logs, sampling and a gateway-level audit with redaction, and permit body capture only on selected endpoints with a flag. Puts the masking rules in a shared library with tests, and sets a size cap and metric. Reviews the privacy and cost implications of log volume.
Why do people extend OncePerRequestFilter instead of implementing Filter directly?
Answer

1. Model Answer

The container may invoke a filter more than once for the same logical request: for forward/include, for the error dispatch, and for async dispatch (Callable, DeferredResult). OncePerRequestFilter guards with a request attribute so doFilterInternal runs once, gives convenient hooks (shouldNotFilter(request), shouldNotFilterAsyncDispatch(), shouldNotFilterErrorDispatch()), and casts to HttpServletRequest/Response for you. Use plain GenericFilterBean only if you really want per-dispatch behaviour.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Knows the dispatch types that cause repeated filter invocation and why OncePerRequestFilter exists.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'it is just a convenience base class' and cannot give a case where a plain filter runs twice.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains the container can invoke a filter more than once for forward/include, error dispatch and async dispatch (Callable, DeferredResult), and OncePerRequestFilter guards with a request attribute so doFilterInternal runs once, casting to HttpServletRequest/Response.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Names the hooks shouldNotFilter(request), shouldNotFilterAsyncDispatch() and shouldNotFilterErrorDispatch() and the case for plain GenericFilterBean, when per-dispatch behaviour is wanted. Warns that a once-only filter can miss work on the error dispatch, so decide deliberately. Verifies by logging inside the filter and triggering an async handler and an error.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Standardises on OncePerRequestFilter as the base for custom filters with an ArchUnit rule, and documents the dispatcher-type choices for each shared filter. Checks that cleanup, such as MDC clearing, is correct across async dispatch. Uses it as a teachable example of the container, not Spring, deciding invocation count.
Where in this pipeline do @Transactional and the database connection come into play, and what does spring.jpa.open-in-view change?
Answer

1. Model Answer

@Transactional is AOP: the service proxy starts the transaction when a public method is called from outside, binds an EntityManager/connection to the thread, and commits or rolls back (runtime exceptions by default) when the method returns. Controllers and filters run outside any transaction unless you annotate them. With open-in-view=true (Boot default, with a warning at startup) an OpenEntityManagerInViewInterceptor keeps one EntityManager open for the whole request, so lazy loading works in the controller or while serialising JSON, but queries can run outside the service transaction, the pool connection can be held longer, and N+1 queries hide in serialisation. For REST services set it to false, fetch what you need in the service (JOIN FETCH, projections, DTOs) and map before returning.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Locates transaction and connection lifetime in the request pipeline and judges the open-in-view trade-off.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says '@Transactional starts a transaction when the controller is called' and leaves open-in-view at the default without knowing what it does.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains @Transactional is AOP: the service proxy starts the transaction on an external public call, binds the EntityManager/connection to the thread, and commits or rolls back on runtime exceptions by default. Controllers and filters run outside a transaction.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Explains open-in-view=true (Boot default, with a startup warning) registers an OpenEntityManagerInViewInterceptor keeping one EntityManager per request, so lazy loading works in the controller or JSON serialisation, but queries can run outside the transaction, connections are held longer and N+1 hides in serialisation. For REST sets it to false and fetches in the service with JOIN FETCH, projections or DTOs, mapping before returning. Verifies with SQL logging and Hikari active-connection metrics.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Sets spring.jpa.open-in-view=false as a platform default and fixes the resulting LazyInitializationException sites in a measured rollout. Adds a query-count assertion in tests and monitors connection hold time. Treats the setting as a way to find places where entities leak past the service boundary.
Which thread executes each layer, and what happens to ThreadLocal state such as the MDC and SecurityContext?
Answer

1. Model Answer

In Spring MVC the same Tomcat request thread (platform thread, or a virtual thread with spring.threads.virtual.enabled=true) runs filters, DispatcherServlet, the controller, the service and the blocking repository call, so ThreadLocals set in a filter (MDC correlation id, Spring Security’s SecurityContextHolder) are visible all the way down and must be cleared in a finally. They are not propagated to threads you hand work to: @Async methods, CompletableFuture pools and reactive operators need a TaskDecorator, DelegatingSecurityContextExecutor or Micrometer context propagation. With Callable/DeferredResult the container releases the thread and later re-dispatches, which is why filters may run again (async dispatch).

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Tracks which thread runs each layer and which ThreadLocal state does or does not follow work onto other threads.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'Spring handles threads for you' and expects the security context and MDC to appear in @Async methods.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains the same Tomcat request thread (or a virtual thread with spring.threads.virtual.enabled=true) runs filters, DispatcherServlet, controller, service and blocking repository, so MDC and SecurityContextHolder are visible throughout and must be cleared in finally.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): States ThreadLocals are not propagated to @Async, CompletableFuture pools or reactive operators, which need a TaskDecorator, DelegatingSecurityContextExecutor or Micrometer context propagation. Explains with Callable/DeferredResult the container releases the thread and re-dispatches, so filters may run again. Verifies by logging the thread name and MDC value in each stage.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Provides one shared executor configuration that carries MDC, security and trace context, and bans raw new Thread and unmanaged pools through a lint rule. Checks leaked ThreadLocals on pooled threads as an incident class. Evaluates virtual threads as simplifying but still requires explicit propagation for work handed to other pools.
How does this pipeline differ in Spring WebFlux?
Answer

1. Model Answer

There is no servlet container requirement (Netty by default) and no DispatcherServlet: the equivalent is DispatcherHandler. Servlet Filter becomes WebFilter (filter(exchange, chain) returning Mono<Void>); there is no HandlerInterceptor (use WebFilter, or HandlerFilterFunction for functional endpoints); @Controller/@ExceptionHandler/@ControllerAdvice still work; controllers return Mono/Flux; thread-locals are unreliable, so context travels in the Reactor Context (Micrometer context propagation bridges the MDC). Blocking calls (JDBC, JPA) must be moved off the event loop.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Maps the servlet-stack pipeline to WebFlux counterparts and knows the thread-model consequences.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Says 'WebFlux is the same but with Mono and Flux' and is unaware of the filter and interceptor differences.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Explains no servlet container is required (Netty default), DispatcherHandler replaces DispatcherServlet, WebFilter (filter(exchange, chain) returning Mono<Void>) replaces Filter, and @Controller, @ExceptionHandler, @ControllerAdvice still work with Mono/Flux.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Notes there is no HandlerInterceptor (use WebFilter or HandlerFilterFunction for functional endpoints), thread-locals are unreliable so context travels in the Reactor Context with Micrometer context propagation bridging the MDC, and blocking calls such as JDBC and JPA must be moved off the event loop. Verifies with BlockHound or by checking event-loop thread names.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Asks whether WebFlux is justified at all given virtual threads on the servlet stack, weighing team skills, debugging complexity and library blocking. Keeps shared libraries from depending on servlet types and tests propagation in both stacks. Documents a decision rule: reactive for streaming and high fan-out I/O, servlet plus virtual threads for typical CRUD.
Design question: for a microservice you need (a) a correlation id on every log line, (b) JWT validation, (c) audit logging of which controller method was called and how long it took, (d) a uniform error body, (e) a transaction around one use case, (f) rate limiting. Say where you implement each and why.
Answer

1. Model Answer

(a) Servlet filter (OncePerRequestFilter, very early order): sets/reads the header, puts it in the MDC, clears in finally; it must cover errors from later filters too. (b) Spring Security filter chain (resource server): runs before everything else, rejects with 401/403 through AuthenticationEntryPoint/AccessDeniedHandler; do not hand-roll it in an interceptor. (c) HandlerInterceptor (or Micrometer @Observed/Observation): it knows the HandlerMethod and gets afterCompletion with status and exception. (d) @RestControllerAdvice returning ProblemDetail, plus filter-level handling for security errors that never reach MVC. (e) @Transactional on the service method (use-case boundary), not on the controller or repository. (f) At the edge: API gateway or a servlet filter backed by a shared store (Redis/bucket4j) so it applies before expensive work and across instances.

2. Interviewer Intent & The L5/L6 Bar

  • Core Competency: Assigns each cross-cutting concern to the layer that sees the right information at the right time, and justifies the placement.

The Candidate Ladder:

  • ❌ Average Candidate: Puts everything in one filter or in controllers, e.g. hand-rolls JWT checking in an interceptor, and puts @Transactional on the controller.
  • ⚠️ Good Candidate: Places correlation id in an early OncePerRequestFilter using MDC cleared in finally, JWT in the Spring Security resource-server chain, audit timing in a HandlerInterceptor, errors in @RestControllerAdvice with ProblemDetail, @Transactional on the service method, rate limiting at the gateway or a filter.
  • ✅ Strong Candidate (L5): Justifies each: the correlation filter must cover errors from later filters; security rejects with 401/403 through AuthenticationEntryPoint/AccessDeniedHandler and is not hand-rolled in an interceptor; the interceptor knows the HandlerMethod and gets status and exception in afterCompletion (or use @Observed); filter-level handling covers security errors that never reach MVC; rate limiting uses a shared store (Redis/bucket4j) before expensive work and across instances.
  • ⭐ Staff Candidate (L6): Packages this as a platform starter with ordering constants, one error contract and per-concern tests, enforced by ArchUnit rules. Defines SLOs for each concern, such as the share of logs with correlation id and the rate-limit rejection rate. Decides edge versus in-service enforcement by blast radius and ownership, and uses the exercise to teach juniors the request lifecycle.

Commonly used code snippets

Copy-ready patterns for the things you write every week, each with the one trap interviewers like to probe.

1 · REST controller: constructor injection, validation, ResponseEntity

💡 TipKeep controllers thin: map DTO, call a service, return. @Valid on @RequestBody fails with MethodArgumentNotValidException; @Validated on the class is what enables constraints on @RequestParam/@PathVariable (fails with HandlerMethodValidationException in 6.1+, ConstraintViolationException before).

2 · Global error handling with ProblemDetail (RFC 7807, Spring 6+)

💡 TipExtending ResponseEntityExceptionHandler gives you RFC 7807 bodies for all the standard MVC exceptions (400, 405, 415 ...) for free. Never leak stack traces or SQL in detail.

3 · Type-safe configuration with @ConfigurationProperties (record, validated)

💡 TipRecords bind through the constructor automatically since Boot 3.0 (@ConstructorBinding only needed on 2.x). Precedence, high to low: command line, env vars, application-{profile}.yml, application.yml, defaults. Prefer this over scattered @Value.

4 · @Transactional service: readOnly, rollback rules, self-invocation

💡 Tip@Transactional is proxy AOP. It is ignored on private methods, on self-invocation (this.other()), on objects created with new, and in other threads. Fix by moving the method to another bean, not by hacks. Checked exceptions do not roll back unless you say so.

5 · Spring Data JPA: derived queries, @Query, N+1 fix, projections, locking

💡 TipDefault every association to LAZY, then fetch what you need per use case (@EntityGraph, join fetch, DTO projection). Turn on spring.jpa.open-in-view=false, and watch SQL with spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.generate_statistics or a query-count test to catch N+1 early.

6 · HTTP clients: RestClient (6.1+), @HttpExchange (6.0+), WebClient

💡 TipAlways set connect and read timeouts on every outbound client; a default of "wait forever" is the classic cascading-failure cause. RestTemplate is in maintenance mode: use RestClient for blocking code and WebClient only when you are reactive.

7 · Spring Security: stateless JWT resource server (SecurityFilterChain DSL)

💡 TipWebSecurityConfigurerAdapter is gone (removed in Security 6): declare a SecurityFilterChain bean. Order matters in authorizeHttpRequests: first match wins, so put specific rules before anyRequest(). Scopes map to SCOPE_x, roles need a custom JwtAuthenticationConverter.

8 · Testing: slices, MockMvc, Testcontainers with @ServiceConnection

💡 TipPick the smallest slice that proves the behaviour: @WebMvcTest (controllers), @DataJpaTest (repositories), @JsonTest, then full @SpringBootTest sparingly. Test against the real database engine with Testcontainers, not H2, when SQL dialect matters.

9 · Caching, async, scheduling and events

💡 TipAll of these are proxy-based, so the self-invocation rule applies. With multiple instances, @Scheduled runs on every instance: use ShedLock or a leader/lock if it must run once. Caches need a TTL and a size bound (Caffeine/Redis) or they become memory leaks.

10 · Resilience, actuator and observability essentials

💡 TipExpose only what you need from Actuator and put it on a separate management port or behind auth. Retries multiply load: retry only idempotent calls, with backoff and jitter, and always combine with a circuit breaker and a timeout.

11 · Handy conditional and lifecycle snippets

💡 TipUse @Bean methods with proxyBeanMethods = false and inject collaborators as parameters. For a custom starter register auto-configuration in META-INF/spring/org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.AutoConfiguration.imports (2.7+; spring.factories before).

(b) 30 rapid-fire one-liners

QuestionOne-liner
Singleton scope means?One instance per container per definition; not thread-safe by itself.
Constructor vs field injection?Constructor: immutable, testable, fails at startup.
BFPP vs BPP?Edit definitions vs wrap instances (proxies).
Why is @Transactional ignored?Proxy bypassed: self-invocation, private, new, other thread.
Default rollback?RuntimeException and Error only.
REQUIRES_NEW danger?Needs another connection: pool deadlock; inner commits independently.
proxyBeanMethods=false?No CGLIB config proxy; do not call @Bean methods directly.
Boot 3 auto-config registration?AutoConfiguration.imports, not spring.factories.
@ConditionalOnMissingBean works where?Auto-configurations (processed after user config).
ConfigurationProperties with records?Implicit constructor binding in Boot 3.
Profile activation?spring.profiles.active; groups in 2.4; on-profile in YAML docs.
JDK vs CGLIB?Interface proxy vs subclass; Boot defaults to CGLIB.
ProblemDetail?RFC 7807/9457 error body, Spring 6.0.
Filter vs interceptor?Servlet level vs MVC handler level.
RestClient?Blocking fluent client, Spring 6.1.
Set which two HTTP timeouts?Connect and read (response).
N+1 fixes?EntityGraph, join fetch, batch fetch size, DTO projection.
OSIV?Open-in-view holds connection for the request; disable.
Optimistic locking?@Version; conflict → 409 or retry whole tx.
IDENTITY and batching?IDENTITY disables JDBC insert batching; use SEQUENCE.
CSRF off when?Stateless bearer-token API without cookies.
hasRole vs hasAuthority?ROLE_ prefix added by hasRole.
Validate JWT how?JWKS signature, iss, exp, plus your aud check.
@ServiceConnection?Testcontainers to Boot connection details (3.1).
Kafka delivery guarantee?At-least-once: consumers idempotent.
DB + Kafka atomic?No: outbox + idempotent consumer.
@Cacheable sync=true?Single computation per key per JVM; stampede guard.
Why bound executors?Unbounded queue hides overload and memory growth.
Liveness must not check?The database or other external dependencies.
Hikari pool size?Small; fleet total below DB max_connections.
Boot 2.7 purpose?Bridge release to Boot 3 (Java 17 + Jakarta).

(c) Top 15 must-say talking points

  1. Prefer constructor injection; keep singletons stateless and thread-safe.
  2. Know that @Transactional/@Async/@Cacheable/@PreAuthorize are all proxy AOP, with the same limits.
  3. Auto-configuration = conditional @Configuration loaded late; debug with --debug and /actuator/conditions.
  4. Externalise config: one immutable image, env/ConfigMap/Secret per environment, @ConfigurationProperties validated at startup.
  5. Return ProblemDetail errors via @RestControllerAdvice; never leak internals; include a trace id.
  6. Every outbound call: connect + read timeout, bounded pool, retries only for idempotent operations, circuit breaker and bulkhead.
  7. Turn off open-in-view; fetch what you need; fix N+1 with graphs/projections; use SEQUENCE ids for batching.
  8. Optimistic locking with @Version and 409/retry of the whole transaction instead of pessimistic locks.
  9. Stateless JWT resource server: validate iss/aud/exp, map claims, method security; mTLS for service identity.
  10. Test pyramid: unit, slices, a few @SpringBootTest, Testcontainers with @ServiceConnection, MockServer for downstreams.
  11. Kafka at-least-once + idempotent consumers + DLT; outbox for DB-to-Kafka; @TransactionalEventListener for in-process after-commit.
  12. Graceful degradation: critical vs optional dependencies, per-branch timeouts and fallbacks, degraded flag.
  13. Operational basics: probes (liveness internal), graceful shutdown + preStop, MaxRAMPercentage, heap dump on OOM, structured logs with traceId.
  14. Virtual threads help blocking I/O but pools/downstreams still need limits; measure before and after.
  15. Version fluency: Boot 2.3 probes/graceful, 2.4 config data, 2.7→3.0 Jakarta/Java 17, 3.1 ServiceConnection, 3.2 virtual threads/RestClient, 3.4 structured logs, Spring 7/Boot 4 modularisation and JSpecify (verify).

(d) 60-second “how my experience maps” box

🧑‍💻 60 secondsI am a senior engineer with 15+ years, 10+ at Apple building Spring Boot services: retail, identity and B2B integration platforms, the Attestation Authority (x509/HSM, 27 ms latency, 5x load tuning), and HR systems moving from Oracle + MongoDB to Kafka event-driven flows (payroll outbound, hourly batch jobs, leave management with 4,000+ leave types). On the retail platform I used optimistic locking instead of pessimistic locks, fork-and-join aggregation with graceful degradation on partial failure, KMS field-level encryption at the gateway so persistence never stores plaintext, and hermetic component tests with containerised Postgres, MockServer and Kafka. I tune with JMeter, Gatling and YourKit (30%), fix leaks with heap dumps, and tune SQL with AWR (45% faster at California Student Aid Commission, where I also built Angular + Spring Boot + enterprise IAM RBAC and AES-256/RSA field-level encryption). I operate on Docker, Kubernetes, Argo CD, Jenkins/GitHub Actions with Splunk, Grafana and ELK for observability.
💡 TipInterview habit: for every answer state (1) what it is, (2) the version it appeared in or changed, (3) the trade-off, (4) the failure mode, (5) a one-line example from your own work. That is the L4 → L5 jump.
